INQUEST '1 

• Inquest is the pre lim inary legal/judicial enquiry or investigation into the cause of death . 

• Conducted in cases of sudden, suspicious, mysterious or unnatural deaths (suicide, homicide, accidents, 
killing by an animal or machinery, deaths due to medical negligence, anesthetic or surgical deaths etc.) 

• If the death is due to natural circumstances, (e.g. heart attack) no further investigation is required . 

- --- ---------- ~ 
Inquest is usually conduc~ d for deaths under follow ing circumstances (4 U's)

Types 

• Police inquest 

• Magistrate inquest 

• Medical examiners system 

• Coroner's inquest 

• Procurator fiscal' s inquest 

In India, only Police inquest and Magistrate's inquest are followed.

POLICE INQUEST -...; 

• It is the most common type of inquest in India . 

• Section 174 CrPC (Cl 194 BNSS) 

• Pol ice Officer conducting t he inquest is ca lled Investigating Officer (10) 

• The lowest rank of police to conduct an inquest - Head constable . 

• Usually in most cases - Sub-inspector (SI) of police conducts t he inquest . 

• After receiving the information about deat hs raising suspicion, t he 10 informs the m agist rate of t he area 

and proceeds to t he place of death. 
• The 10 invest igates in the presence of minimum two witnesses (Panchas, Panch witnesses or 

Panchayatdars) 
• The 10 prepares an inquest report (Panchanama) about t he apparent cause of death descr ibing t he 

wounds in t he body, clothes, articles, crime scene etc. 

• If no foul play is suspected - body re leased for disposal. 

• Suicidal, homicidal, accidental or suspicious deaths - body sent for PM examinat ion t o t he nearest 

Government or authorized private inst itution. 

MAGISTRATE INQUEST "-
• Section 176 CrPC (Cl 196 BNSS) 
• It is superior to police inquest. 

• Can be conducted by Executive Magistrate or Judicial Magistrate 

Executive Magistrates 
• District Magist rate (Collector), 

• Deputy Collector or Sub-Collector 

• Sub-divisional Magistrate (Revenue Divisional Officer) or 

• Tahsildar 
Magistrates conduct inquest in cases of 

• Dowry deaths 

• Exhumation 
• Second autopsy 
• Death in police custody 

• Death during police interrogation 

• Death due to police firing 

• Death in prison, reformatories or Borstal school 

• Death in a psychiatric hospital 

❖ In any case, the Magistrate may conduct an inquest , instead of or in addition t o the police inquest .

• A person qualified both legally and medically (Medical examiner) conducts inquest as well as 
postmortem examination. 

• BeSt syStem o f inquest - because it is conducted by a forensic pathologist with medica l, scientific and 
legal knowledge.

SUPREME COURT 

• The Constitution states that the Supreme Court is a federal court, Guardian of the Constitution, and the 
highest court of appeal. 

• Situated in New Delhi . 

• The Supreme Court comprises the Chief Justice and 33 other judges . 

• Primarily it is an appellate court (appeal against judgments of High courts) 

• It also takes writ petitions in cases of serious human rights violations, or any petition filed under Article 
32 (right to constitutional remedies) or if a case involves a serious issue that needs immediate resolution. 

• Can pass any sentence . 

• Can sustain or alter punishment awarded by High Court . 

• Appeal can be made to the President of India t o commute punishment. 

HIGH COURT 

• Highest court of the state . 

• Appellate and trial court . 

• Can pass any sentence . 

• Confirmation of death sentence passed by the sessions court . 

• Usually situated in the capital of the state (except a few: Kerala-Kochi; M adhya Pradesh-Jabalpur; Uttar 
Pradesh-Allahabad; Rajasthan - Jodhpur)

3. Describe the procedure of recording evidence in a Court of Law .. 

It is done in the following order. 

I. OATH TAKING 
A pro

• m
 ise m ade in the name of God, to speak truth and nothing but the truth . 

An at
• h
 eist, who does not want to swear in the name of the God, may solemnly affirm . 

• Child bPlow 12 years is not required to take an oath (Unsworn evidence) . 
Pers

• o
 ns above 12 years of age shou ld take an oath. (Sworn evidence) . 

• No holy book is given to swear . 

II. EXAMINATION IN CHl[f_(Q!~ECT EXAMINATIO_N} 

• First examination of the Witness . 
The witness 

• is
  examined by the lawyer of the party which ca lls him . 

In a criminal case (e.g. rape, m
• u
 rder) public prosecutor usua lly examines the medical witness . 



,, VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

• To elicit all the re levant fact s. 
• No leading questions. 
• Leading questions may be asked if the witness is hostile. 

Ill. CROSS EXAMINATION 
• W itness examined by th e lawyer of the opposite party. 
• To elicit facts favorable to the cross-examining side. 
• To test the accuracy of the statements made by the w itness. 
• To modify or explain what has been said. 
• The credibility and competence of the witness will be tested. 
• Leading Questions allowed. 
• No time limit. 
• It acts like a double-edged weapon, dangerous if a lawyer is inexperienced, unprepared. 

IV. RE-EXAMINATION (RE DIRECT EXAMINATION) 
• Similar to direct examination. 
• No leading Questions. 
• To add details to the statements made by the witness during direct examination. 
• To clear any ambiguities and correct mistakes. 
• If any new issue is raised, it can be crossed. 

V. QUESTIONS BY THE JUDGE/ COURT QUESTIONS 
• Can ask questions at any time of proceedings to clear his doubts.

EVIDENCE: the word evidence refers to the proofs produced in the court of law in favor or against t he disputed

in proving or disproving a medicolegal dispute are cal led medical evidence.

DOCUMENTARY EVIDENC~ '-., 

M EDICAL CERTIFICATES 

• Document prepared by the doctor at the request of the patient and handed over to him. 

• Examples 
, Death certificate 

, Sickness certificate 

, Vaccination certificate 

, Disability certificate 

• A doctor issuing false certificate is punishable u/s. 197 IPC (Cl 232 BNS) - 7 years imprisonment+ fine. 

M EDICOLEGAL REPORTS 

• Document prepared by the doctor at the request of an investigating authority like police, magistrate etc. 

and handed over to them. 

• E.g.: wound certi f icate, potency certificate, postmortem report etc. 

DYING DECLARATION '--..., 

• A statement (written or oral) made by a_p~rson dying due to an unlawful act, regarding the facts that 

resulted in his death . (S. 32 IEA) (Cl 26 (1) BSA] 

• An example of Hearsay evidence. 

General rules 
• Ideally, the Executive Magistrate (ideally) should be called to record the declaration (if t here is sufficient 

time). If there is no t ime, the doctor, police or any person can record t he de<:_!aration in t he presence of 

two witnesses. 
• Prel iminary part iculars of the patient noted: Name, age, sex, address, hospital admission number, MLC 

number, police station, date and time of admission, ident ificat ion marks, etc. 

• Evidentiary value is maximum when recorded by a magistrate. 

• Compos Mentis: Before recording the declaration, the doctor should certify t hat the person is conscious, 

and his mental functions are normal. 
• Oath is not administered - based on the belief that a person wit h the sense of impending death speaks 

truth. 
• Statement of the patient should be recorded in t he language spoken by the victim, in his own words 

without any alteration of terms and phases. 

• Can be recorded in any fo rm - written by the person himself, video recorded, written by ot her person. 

• No prompting or suggestions - Statement should be free and spontaneous. 

• No opinions or judgments - Statement must be on facts. 

• No leading questions. 
• l~is ~ot necessary that the words should be spoken. If the decla rant is unable to speak, but able to make 

signs or gestures to questions, the gestures should be recorded, which should be interpreted by the 

court. 
• The invest igating pol ice officer should n_ot be pregent when the declaration is recorded (to avoid undue 

influence) 
• Signature or thumb impression of the declarant obtained. 

• Declaration should also be signed by the doctor and witnesses and forwarded to the magistrate. 

• Treatment should never be stopped or delayed to obtain declaration.

ORAL EVIDENCE 

• Evidence given orally in the court of law. 
• Direct evidence: evidence of a person who has been seen, heard or perceived the fact . 
• Indirect evidence 

,. Circumstantial evidence: several related things that strongly point towards the commission of 
crime. E.g.: blood stained shirt over the body of the accused 

, Hearsay evidence: witness states what he has heard others saying about the crime . 
• Oral evidence is superior to documentary evidence because it is made under oath and subjected to 

cross examination. 
• Exceptions to oral evidence 

, Dying declaration 
, Evidence given by a witness in a lower court. 
, Evidence given by a Medical witness in a lower court. 
, Expert opinion in a treatise or book. 
, Reports of certain Government scientific experts. E.g. Director of fingerprints, Director of 

Central or State forensic science laboratory, Chemical Examiner etc. 
, Public records kept in public offices li ke birth, death and marriage certificates. 
, Hospital records. E.g. case sheet with date of admission, discharge, recordings of BP pulse, 

investigations etc.

7. fSUBPOENA or SUMMONS. ...!  1 

• Meaning: an orde, to appear before a judge or magistrate (sub - under; poena - penalty) 
• A summons is a legal document issued by a court to a person involved in a court proceeding. 
• It orders a speci fic person to appear in court at a specific time . 
• Th e sum mon s may be iss ued either to a person accused of a cr im e or to a witn ess · 
• Issued by the court, signed by t he presiding officer . 
• Name of the accused, Case details, crime number, date and time to arrive at the court will be mentioned 

in the summons. 
• Served usually by a police officer (usually a constable), officer of the court or any government servant or 

by registered post . 
• If the witness is in government service, a summons is sent to the Head of the Office . 
• If witness fail s t o attend the court without valid reason, in civil cases - fine will be imposed; in criminal 

cases - fine or bail able or non bailable warrant may be issued. 
• Punishment for not obeying summons: 6-12 months imprisonment with or without Rs.5000 fine (S. 172-

174 IPC). (Cl 204-206 BNS) 
• If summoned on the same date by both civil and criminal courts, criminal court should be attended . 
• Higher courts should have priority over lower courts. 
• Criminal courts have priority over civi l courts. 
• Courts of same status - court summoned first should be attended. 

❖ Summons duces tecum: a court summons ordering the recipient to appear before the court and 
produce documents. 

---❖ Summons ad t~gificandum: a court summons t o appear and give oral testimony. 

8. ~ ONDUCTMONEYj 
• Money paid to the witness to meet t he expenses for attending the court. 
• Only for civil cases. 
• Paid to him at the time of serving the summons. 
• If the fee is not paid, or if the witness feels it is not sufficient, he can bring it to the notice of the Judge 

after giving evidence. 
• The judge wi l l decide the amount to be paid . 
• No fee to the witness for criminal cases. Government servants can claim from their office . 
• Private doctors may request for conc!uct money at the end of the evidence . 

9 . What is First Information Report? Write about Documentary evidence. 
• First Information Report {FIR) is a written document prepared by the police when they receive 

information about the commission of a cognizable offence. 
• It is generally a complaint lodged with the police by the victim of a cognizable offence or by someone on 

his/her behalf. 
• An FIR is a very important document as it set s the process of crimina l justice in motion. 
• It is only after the FIR is registered in the po lice station that the police t akes up investigation of the case

13. a) Witness•

WITNESS 

• A witness is a person who gives sworn testimony in a court of law regarding facts about a crime or an 

event. 

Types 
COMMON WITNESS (eye-witness, occurrence witness, witness of fact) 

• Testifies the facts what he saw, heard or perceived (first-hand-knowledge) 

• No special knowledge is required. 
• Volunteering a statement not allowed. 

EXPERT WITNESS 
• One who has acquired a special knowledge, skill or experience in any science, art or profession. 
• Capable of drawing opinions and conclusions from the facts observed by himself or noticed by others. 
• E.g.: Doctors, Fingerprint expert, Ballistic experts, Handwriting expert etc. 
• Doctor can be both ordinary witness and expert witness. 

• Volunteering of statement allowed. 

HOSTILE WITNESS (adverse witness, unfavorable witness) 

• When a witness deliberately conceals the truth or gives false evidence. 

• He contradicts his own previous statements in the court of law. 

• If t he witness is declared hostile, leading questions can be asked during direct examination itself. 
• Both common witness and expert witness can turn hostile.

17. CONDUCT OF THE DOCTOR IN THE W ITNESS BOX• 

• Be well prepared w ith the details of t he case . 

• Take all relevant records, cert if icates, documents etc . 

• Do not discuss the case w ith anyone in the court except t he lawyer by whom you w ere asked t o t est ify . 
• Be well dressed and modest . 

• Be relaxed and ca lm; do not get nervous or frightened . 

• Do not try to memorize; you can refresh your memory from the report copies . 
• Speak clearly and audibly.  

• Use simple language, avoid medica l terms as much as possible . 

• Do not exaggerate anything. Avoid the use of superlatives . 

• Do not fumble in referring notes, books etc . 

• Address the Judge by Sir or Your honor . 



LEGAL PROCEDURES 

• Do not underestimate the medical knowledge of the lawyers.  
• Avoid difference between your report and testimony . 
• Never become angry, rude or hostile. An angry witness is a poor witness.  
• Be honest, impartial, unbiased and truthful. 
• Listen to the questions carefully. Answer only to what is asked . 

18. !HISTORY OF FOREN~C MEDICIN§ 

• lmhotep (2900 BC) was the personal physician and Chief Justice to the King of Egypt. He is considered the 
first medico-legal expert. 

• The Code of Hammurabi of Babylon (2000-1000 BC)is the oldest medico-legal code given by King of 
Babylon. It describes punishment for medical practitioners in case of improper treatment. 

• The Code of the Hittites (1400 BC) prescribes legal compensation for personal injury. 
• Hippocrates (Father of Medicine) described lethality of wounds, medical ethics, sudden deaths, etc. 

• The Justinian Code (529-564 AD) prescribed regulation of medical practice and imposition of penalties for 
malpractice. 

• The first Medico-legal autopsy was conducted by Bartolomeo Da Varignana in 1302 AD in Italy. 

• The f irst book on Forensic Medicine was written in 1602 by Fortunato Fedele, an Italian physician. 

• The famous book on Medico-legal questions, Questiones Medico-/ega/es, was written by Paulus Zacchias 
(Father of Forensic Medicine and Forensic Psychiatry) in 1621. 

FORENSIC MEDICINE IN INDIA 

• Manusmriti (3102 BC) is the first treatise on various laws written by King Manu. It prescribed code of 

... conduct for society, prescribed rules for marriages and punishment for various offences viz., adultery, 

seduction unnatural sexual offences etc. 
• The first Indian book on medicine, Agnivesa Charaka Samhita was written by the famous physician 

Charaka (700 BC). It elaborates training, duties, privileges and social status of physicians. It is considered 
as Indian code of medical ethics. It also describes various poisons, symptoms, signs and treatments of 

poisoning. 
• sushruta Samhita written by Sushruta {Father of Indian Surgery). refers to poisons, snake signs and 

t reatment of poisoning. 
• The first Medicolegal autopsy in India was done by Dr. Buckley in 1693 in Chennai for a case of alleged 

arsenic poisoning. 
• The first chair in Medical Jurisprudence was instituted in Calcutta Medical College in 1845, and Dr. CTO 

Woodford was the first Professor of Medical Jurisprudence in the country . 
• Dr Jaising P. Modi {Father of Forensic Medicine in India) wrote the first book of forensic medicine 

"M edical Jurisprudence and Toxicology" in 1920

1. [Define Forensic Medicine/Legal Medicine/State Medicin~ 

• The branch of medicine dealing with the application of scientific medical knowledge to establish facts in 

civil or criminal legal cases, thereby aids in the administration of law and justice. 
• Practit ioners of forensic medicine assist in medical-legal investigations by offering expert opinions to the 

Police/Magistrate.

MEDICAL REGISTER

5. a) Define medical negligence. Explain various types of negligence with suitable examples. What precautions

a) Define professional negligence. Enumerate the types of negligence with examples. Mention the defences of a 
doctor against a charge of medical negligence. 

MEDICAL NEGLIGENCE Synonyms. Prof Pssional negligence, Medical malpractice, Medical malpraxis 

Definitions: 
• Absence of reasonable degree of care and skill 

or 
willful act of a medical practitioner in the treatment of a patient, which results in injury or suffering or 
death of the patient. 

• Fail ing to do something, which, one is supposed to do (act of omission) 
or 
Doing something, which, one is not supposed to do (act of commission) that deviates from the accepted 
standard of medical care causing damage to the patient 

Circumstances where Act of commission and omission are applied: 
❖ Negligence 
❖ Euthanasia 
❖ Infanticide 

Elements of negligence (4 D's) 
❖ DUTY OF CARE 
❖ DERELICTION 
❖ DIRECT CAUSATION 
❖ DAMAGE 

All the 4 elements should be proved to convict a doctor for medical negligence. 
• DUTY - the doctor owes a duty to the pat ient when, 

;.... He agrees to treat him, or if a patient needs emergency care . 
;.... In both the above circumstances the doctor-patient relationship is est ablished . 
',, If the doctor d id not agree t o treat the pat ient or when the patient d oes no t nee d emergency 

care, doctor-patient relationship is not formed. 

• DERELICTION/DEFICIENCY /DEVIATION 
D~viation from duty, deficiency of duty, breach of duty or failure to fulfill the duty. 

,, Disregard, recklessness or carelessness during t reatment. 
Failure on the part of t he doctor to maint ain proper care and skill. 



MEDICAL LAW and ETHICS 

• DAMAGE 

' Damage caused as result of dereliction. 

,. The damage should be of a type that would have been foreseen/anticipated by a reasonable 
physician . 

th
' Even if e doctor is negligent and damage has not occu rred, the patient cannot sue him for 

compensation. 

' Examples for damage: prolonging of illness, aggravation of illness, prolonged hospital stay, pain 
and suffering, death. 

• DIRECT CAUSATION 

Dereliction rs the direct cause (proximate cause) for damage/injury. 

,. That is, failure to exercise reasonable care and ski ll was directly responsible for the damage 
caused . 

Types of Medical Negligence 

• CIVIL NEGLIGENCE 

• CRIMINAL NEGLIGENCE 

• CONTRIBUTORY NEGLIGENCE 

• COMPOSITE NEGLIGENCE 

• CORPORATE NEGLIGENCE 

CIVIL NEGLIGENCE 

• Patient/relatives may charge the doctor for compensation . 
or 

• The doctor may charge the patient who refuses 1o pay fees alleging negligence . 

• Trial occurs in a civil court or consumer court . 

• The burden {onus) of proof is on the patient, i.e., the patient must prove doctor's negligence . 

• If proven guilty, the doctor need to pay compensation as decided by the court . 

• Examples 
, Failure to get informed consent. 
,.. Failure to give proper instructions. 
,... Fai lure to warn the patient about side effects. 

~IMINAL NEGLIGENCE . . . . 
• Gross absence of ski.l l or care 0 f the doctor during treatment that resulted rn serious inJury or death of 

the patient. • · I b rt· ) 
• 'llegal act (e.g. crimina a o 10n 

When the doctor performs an 1 . 

• f ssault victim that resulted rn death. 
U d d I · treatment o an a 

n ue e ay m 
• • of the doctor we nt beyond the matter of compensation . 

1
When the neg rgence I' nee should be proved beyond rea sonable doubt . 

• d ·it the alleged neg ige 
To be foun gui Y, compensation and he may also be sentenced to imprisonment .

• Examples : 
, Amputation of wrong finger or wrong limb 
, Leaving instruments or swabs inside abdomen 

, Performing criminal abortion 

CONTRIBUTORY NEGLIGENCE 

• Both the doctor and the patient or his attendant are negligent 

• Without the negligence of the patient the damage would not have occurred . 

• · ·d ff (d t negligent) ➔ Patient gets side 
Doctor prescribes drug ➔ does not warm him about s1 e e ects oc or . 
effects ➔ does not inform doctor (patient negli gent) ➔ suffers i njury ➔ Contributory negligence. 

Last c~ ar chance doctrine 
• Both the doctor and the patient are negligent . 
• If it is proved that the doctor had a " last clear chance" to avoid damage caused to the patient and he did 

not avai l this chance, 

• The doctor loses the defence of contributory negligence . 

• E.g. Doctor prescribes drugs to the patient ➔ does not inform about its side effects (doctor negligent) ➔ 

patient develops side effects ➔ but does not inform doctor (patient negligent) ➔ patient v isits doctor on 

the next appointment ➔ doctor observes the side effects and does not take remedial action (last clear 

chance) ➔ permanent injury to the patient . 
Avoidable consequence rule 

• Similar to last clear chance doctrine 

• Both t he docto r and t he patient are negligent 

• But here, the patient has the last chance to avoid damage. 

• E.g. a diabetic patient comes to doctor with foot i njury ➔ Doctor does not prescribe antibiotics (doctor 

negligent) ➔ but advises him to keep the wound clean and keep blood sugar under control ➔ instructs 

him for follow up after 1 week ➔ patient does not keep the wound clean and does not follow diabetic 

diet (patient negligent) ➔ wound gets infected ➔ patient does not v isit doctor after 1 week ➔ develops 
foot gangrene(avoidable consequence). 

c:;_pMPOSITE NEGLIGENCE 
• Also called percentage negligence 

• When the combined negligence of two or more doctors at the same time results in the damage to the 
patient, it is called composite negligence. 

CORPORATE NEGLIGENCE 
• When the patients' care is affected by malfunctioning equipment, incompetent staff etc. of a corporate 

hospital. 

• When the hospital fails in any m anner t o meet the accepted standards of care, which may result in the 
injury to the patient. 

• Examples 

, Adverse effects caused due to an expired drug given during hospital stay. 

, Prolonged hospita l stays due to the incompetent hospital staff. 



I MEDICAL LAW and ETHICS 

DEFENCES AGAINST NEGLIGENCE 

• No duty owed to the patient - if the doctor had not agreed to treat the patient (no doctor-patient 
relationship) 

• Duty discharged according to existing standards of medical practice . 
• Calculated risk doctrine - doctor took a reasonable and calculated risk (when benefits overweigh r isks) to 

save the life of the patient, which in turn caused injury. 
• Contributory negligence 
• The damage is due to the intervention of a third person without the doctor's knowledge . 
• The damage is an expected outcome of the disease (a known complication of the disease/treatment) 
• Medical misadventure (Inevitable accident) 

,., An accident or disaster, in which an individual is injured or died due to some unintentional act by 
a doctor/hospital. 

,., Despite all proper care and precautions during treatment, the patient may suffer injuries. 
;.... Therapeutic misadventure - Hypersensitivity reaction or anaphylaxis with drugs like penicillin, 

aspirin etc. occurring after negative test dose. 
:,... Diagnostic misadventure - allergic reaction to contrast media given before CT scan. 

• Novus actus interveniens - a completely unrelated, unexpected and unforeseen act has occurred which 
further worsened the condition caused by the negligence of the doctor. 

• Res-judicata (double jeopardy)- a matter [already] judged: once a lawsuit is decided, the litigant parties 
are barred from raising the same issue again in the courts. 

• Time limit: cases should be fil ed within 2 years of t he alleged date of negligence. 

PRECAUTIONS FOR PREVENTION OF NEGLIGENCE 

• Establish good rapport with the patient 

• Maintain professional secrecy 

• Do not criticize another doctor in front of the patient 

• Keep professional knowledge updated 

• Apply due care and skill in treating a patient 

• Employ and associate with qualified and competent staff 

• Written consent in all steps if it involves a risk 

• Take consent of both husband & wife if a treatment/procedure may result in impotency or sterility 

• Laboratory investigations to confirm clinical diagnosis 

• Record patient's conditions and treatment given regularly 

• Consult professional colleagues whenever necessary 

• Always check the intactness, instructions, date of expiry etc., of an injection vial before use 

• Perform sensitivity test for a drug known to cause anaphylactic reactions 

• Always examine a female patient in the presence of another female/nurse 

• Do not make a statement admitting fault on your part.

• Dereliction 
, The doctor performed the surgery under the influence of alcohol. 
, There was disregard, recklessness or carelessness during treatment. 

• Damage: 
, Cut injury to the small intestine is the damage caused to the pat ient. 
, Small intest inal cut resu lted 1n prolonged hospital stay, pain and suffering.

9. Define consent. What are the types? Add a note on informed consent and its exceptions. 

CONSENT 

• Consent is defined as voluntary agreement, compliance or permission . 

• Signifies that the person who has given consent for an act, knows its consequences and he has accepted it 

• Legally valid consent: Informed consent (best - informed written consent) 

Types of consent 

• Implied (Implicit) consent 

• Expressed (Explicit) consent 
',> Written consent 
,.. Verbal/Oral consent 

IMPLIED CONSENT 

• Implied means understood 

• Consent is indicated by the behavior of the patient (coming for consu ltation, paying consulting fee} 

• Usually fo llow ed for routine general examination 

• E.g.: checking the conjunctiva for pallor, chest auscultation, BP checking etc . 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

EXPRESS CONSENT 

• For vagi·n al , rect al exami·n ati·o ns, verbal consent must b e o b tai·n e d , pre terably in the presence of a thi rd 

person (e .g. attendant of some other patient ) 
• For anest hesia, invasive diagnost ic and surgical procedures, written consent is necessary 

• Consent should refer t o one specific procedure 
• In sensitive cases, potentially litigant patients, t he consent procedure can be video recorded 

INFORMED CONSENT 
• Informed consent means that the consent of the patient taken after explaining about the complete 

detai ls of his illness, the procedure or treatment 
• Components of informed consent 

, Disclosure - about his illness, treatment options, costs, risks vs benefits, success and failure rates, 
alternate procedures, prognosis etc. 

,. Comprehen,;ion - in patient 's own language 
,. No influence over the decision - the patient should take his own 
,. Competence - patient's age and mental status (see below) 

Actual consent: Written and witnessed - The patient should write in his own handwriting that he 
has read and understood the nature of the procedure and its risks, before signing the printed 
consent form . Two witnesses should also sign to prevent any allegations in the future. 

Reasons for obtaining consent 
• To examine, treat or operating a patient without consent amounts assault as per law, even if it is done in 

good fa ith 
• If the doctor has failed to furnish adequat e information about a treatment or surgery, precautions, its 

risks to the patient , the patient may charge the doctor for negligence 
Legally valid consent 

• Consent is essential for every medical examination. 
• Consent should be free, voluntary, clear, informed and direct . 
• There should not be any misinterpretation of facts, influence, threat or compulsion (No force, fear or 

fraud - the 3 F's that constitute invalid consent) 

Exceptions to informed consent : 
• DOCTRINE OF EMERGENCY (S 92 IPC) (Cl 30 BNS) 

, Any harm caused in good faith, even without the consent of the person, is not an offence, if the 
circumstances are impossible to get consent. 

, E.g.: An emergency in which a comatose person following a road t raffic accident, is developing 
extradural hematoma that needs emergency surgery. 

,- If the relatives are not ava ilable, even without consent, the doctor can perform surgery in good 
faith to save his live (Law implies consent) 

• LOCO PARENTIS (IN TH E PLACE OF A PARENT) 
, In an emergency, when the parent or guard ian is not available, the person in-charge of the child 

can give consent 
, E.g.: When a child of a residential school needs emergency Appendectomy, t he Headmaster of 

t he school or W arden can give consent 

J 



MEDICAL LAW and ETHICS 

• THERAPEUTIC PRIVILEGE 

,. Full disclosure of remote complications and theoretical risks about a procedure may frighten the 
patient, who may refuse the treatment 

,. The doctor may withhold some information, disclosure of which may harm the patient 
psychologically which in turn makes him to refuse a treatment where the risk is truly little 

' But this information must be informed to the relatives and written consent should be obtained 
• THERAPEUTIC WAIVER 

' Patient with competency to give consent waives his right to the doctor I 
,. Any communication that is made in good faith is not an offence (S 93 IPC) (Cl 31 BNS) 

• Examination of Arrested Person at Police Request (S 53 CrPC) (Cl 53 BNSS) 
• MEDICOLEGAL POSTMORTEMS (S 174 CrPC) (Cl 194 BNSS) 
• TREATMENT OF PATIENTS SUFFERING FROM NOTIFIABLE DISEASE (in the interest of the community) 
• MASS IMMUNIZATION PROGRAMS BY THE GOVERNMENT 

Consent in Medicolegal cases 
• In crim inal cases, the accused or victim cannot be examined without his consent. 
• A female accused, or a victim should be examined only by or under the supervision of a female 

registered medical practitioner (Section 53-B CrPC) [Cl 51 BNSS - Examination of a female accused only 
by, or under the supervision of, a female registered medical practitioner; Cl 184 BNSS - Medical 
examination of a rape victim - shall be conducted by a registered medical pract itioner employed in a 
hospital run by the Government or a local authority and in the absence of such a practitioner, by any 
other registered medical practitioner, with the consent of such woman or of a person competent to give 
such consent on her behalf within 24 hours] 

• consent of the spouse is required for artificial insemination, for donation of sperm/ovum and 
contraceptive sterilization 

• The nature of il lness of a patient should not be disclosed to anyone without the consent of the patient 
(professional secrecy) 

• Pathological or Clinical autopsy needs consent from the legal heir . 

• Medico-legal autopsy does not need consent from the relatives 

• Consent is not a defence in professional negligence 

Consent and Age 
• A child below 12 years of age and an insane person cannot give valid consent. (S 89 IPC) (Cl 27 BNS) 

Consent is obtained from the parent or the guardian 
• For general physical examination: patient age > 12 years 
• Anything beyond routine examination (e .g. vaginal examination, rectal examination, CT scan, Surgery 

etc.) : patient age> 18 years 
• A person above l8 years of age can give valid consent to suffer any harm, which may result from an act 

not intended or not known to cause death or grievous hurt 
• A woman of 18 years and above can give valid consent for sexual intercourse 
• A woman of 18 years and above can give valid consent for Medical Termination of Pregnancy

MEDICAL LAW and ETHICS 31 . 

THE DOCTOR SHOULD NOT DISCLOSE/DISCUSS DETAILS OF DISEASE OF 
• Patient with anyone else without his consent 
• Major patient to their parents, even if the parents are paying fees 
• Major patient with anyone, even if the docto r is treating free of cost 
• Patient even when requested by a public or statutory body (except in case of not ifiable diseases) 
• Husband to his wife or wife to her husband without their consent 
• A servant/employee to his employer without his consent, even though the master is paying the fees 
• A trial prisoner in police custody, to the authorities, without the consent of the prisoner 

❖ Maintenance of secrecy of medicolegal/ postmortem examination is implied and the doctor shou ld maintain 
secrecy 

❖ While reporting a c?se in any medical journal, care should be taken not t o disclose the identity of the patient, 
un less he expresses consent 

THE DOCTOR CAN DISCLOSE/DISCUSS THE DETAILS OF DISEASE OF 
• A minor child, insane or an intoxicated person may be disclosed to the guardian 
• A government servant, to the government (after he is requested by the government to examine his 

f itness) 
• A convicted prisoner, to the authorities without consent 

7. PRIVILEGED COMMUNICATION** * ** 

• It is the bonafide statement of a doctor on a subject matter of public interest, made to concerned 
authority, as a part of his duty to protect the interests of the community (i.e ., in certain conditions, 
doctor can reveal patient' s information to protect the public) 

• These are exceptions to professional secrecy 

VENEREAL DISEASES 
• When a syphilitic patient or a HIV infected person is about to marry or 
• If husband/wife is sufferi ng from a venereal disease 
• When a person suffering from syphilis or gonorrhea is entering a public swimming pool 
• The doctor should inform the concerned authority in order to protect others 

NOTIFIABLE DISEASES 
• Notification of infectious diseases to public health authorities

8 . Difference\ betwf'en Civil and Criminal negligence 

Feature CIVIL NEGLIGENCE I CRIMINAL NEGLIGENCE 
Offence No specific or clear violation of law Specific violation of a particular criminal law 
Negl igence Simple absence of care and skill Gross negligence, lack of competence, 

inattention, disregard to life 
Conduct of the Compared to a generally accepted Not compared to a single test 
physician standard of professional conduct 
Damage Reversible/repairable damage Irreparable damage o r death 
Defences Consent, Cannot be cited as defences 

Contributory negligence 
Trial by Civil court or Consumer court Criminal court 
Complainant Sufferer party (patient, relative or doctor) Publ ic prosecutor (on behalf of state) 
Evidence Strong evidence is sufficient Guilt should be proved beyond reasonable 

doubt 
Burden of proof Lies with the patient Doctor has to prove innocence 
Punishment Liable to pay damages (monetary Imprisonment and fine (S 304-A IPC) (Cl 104 

compensation) BNS) 

CJ . COMPOSI f E N EGLIGENCE: 

• Also called percentage negligence 

• W hen the combined negligence of two or more persons/doctors' results in t he damage to the patient, it 
is called composite negligence 

• The persons causing such damage are known as composite tortfeasors 

• The tortfeasors have to pay fo r t he enti re damages and the injured person has the choice of proceeding 
against all or any of them 

• E.g. A pharmacist selling an expired drug and a doctor injecting it into the patient resulting in adverse 
effect s. Both the pharmaci~t and the doctor are negligent.

• Defence against negligence • Not a defence against negligence

11. VICARIOUS LIABILITY*** 

• Synonyms: Liability for the act of another or Principle of Respondent Superior (Let the Master answer) 

or Captain of the ship doctrine 
• An employer (superior) is held legally responsible for the negligent acts of his/her 

employees(subordinates) appointed by him 
• E.g: If a swab or instrument is left in the patient's body after surgery, the chief surgeon is responsible 

Conditions to be satisfied 
• Exist ing employer-employee relationship 
• Employee did what he was legally supposed to do (within the scope of his employment) 

• Negligence occurred while he was on job 

General rules 
• Usually, the chief/senior doctor is held responsible for the negligence of j unior doctors, interns and 

residents who are under his direct supervision and control. That does not mean the junior is not 

responsible. Both will be sued by the patient, but more liability on the chief doctor 

• For some surgeries, a surgeon may be associated temporarily with another with the establishment of 

employer-employee relationsh ip. Each one is responsible for the negligence of other 

• A surgeon is not responsible for the negligence of the anesthesiologist and the anesthesiologist is not 

responsible for the negligence of the surgeon (since both are independent competent experts) 

The doctor is vicariously liable under the following circumstances also 
• Doctor knowingly refers a patient to an incompetent doctor- referring doctor is liable 

• Doctor writing i llegible prescription and pharmacist dispensing wrong drug 

• Doctor instructing prescription over phone and pharmacist dispensing wrong drug 

• For the negligence of the nurse 

-



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

12. RES IPSA LOQUITUR*.,. 

• Res lpsa Loquitur - the thing speaks for itself 
• The negligence is so obvious that it speaks for itself 
• Res lpsa loqu1· tur 1· s one form of c1· rcumstant1·a  I ev1· d ence w h1. c h ·i n d1. ca tes that the most probable cause of 

an injury is the doctor's negligence 
• 3 ba5ic requirements to be satisfied 

)..- The act that caused the harm does not occur without a physician's negligence 
, The doctor had complete & exclusive control over the injury producing equipment or treatment 
-,, Patient was not guilty of contributory negligence 

• In a negligence suit, professional negligence of a doctor should be proved in the court by another expert 
physician 

• In Res lpsa loquitur, medical expert opinion is not required. Common knowledge of any person is 
sufficient to understand the negligence (Doctrine of common knowledge) 

• When the doctrine of Res lpsa Loquitur applies, the patient need not prove negligence; the doctor 
should prove that he is not negligent 

• Examples 
, Amputation of wrong limb or finger 
, Operating a wrong patient 
, Leaving swab or instrument inside patient's abdomen after surgery 
;... Ill effects of drug due to prescription of over dosage/wrong medicine 
;... Blood transfusion mismatch hazards 

13. N OVUS ACTUS INTERVENIENS 
• In Novus actus interveniens, the doctor is negligent. 
• Then a new act which is completely unrelated, unexpected and unforeseen action intervening or 

breaking the chain of events (Act of God) and further worsens patient's condition 
• The new act breaks the chain of causation. So, the doctor's negligence is no more the proximate cause 

of injury to the patient 
• This principle usually applies to cases of assault and accidental injury 
• Example: Doctor gives penicillin without test dose (doctor negligent) ➔ patient develops rashes ➔ 

patient is urgently referred to another hospital ➔ the ambulance meets with an accident (unrelated act) 
➔ patient dies 

14. 1THERAPEUTIC MISADVENTURE* 

• Synonym: Medical misadventure 
• A misadventure is an accident or disaster, in which an individual is injured or died due to some 

unintentional act by a doctor/hospital 

• Despite all proper care and precautions during treatment, the patient may suffer injuries 
• Types of misadventure 

.,,.. Therapeutic: during treatment 
, Diagnostic: during diagnostic procedures 
;... Experimental: when the individual has consented for an experimental study 

• A doctor is not responsible for injuries or death resulting from an adverse drug reaction (provided he has 
taken all necessary care such as the test dose, emergency resuscitation efforts etc.)

• D. iagnostic misadventure - a 11 erg1c reaction to cont rast media given before CT scan

• Sensit ivity tests to be perto rme d b e f ore giving inJect1·o ns likely to cause anaphyla.(·IS  

15. M EDICAL INDEMNITY IN SU RANCE 

• Synonym Medical Malpractice Insurance 

• An insurance plan to financially safeguard medical practitioners against lega l costs and claims for 
com pen sa t ion by patients in negligence suits 

• A contract between the doctor and an insurance company 

• The doctor pays premium to an insurance company 

• In cases of professional negligence of the doctor, the insu rance company pays compensat on c a,ms 

against the doctor and bears costs of legal proceedings 

Objectives 
• To protect the insured doctor 
• To arrange, conduct and pay for legal proceedings to defend the doctor 

• To arrange profess ional as~1stance 
• ro indemnify the insured doctor for the expenses ar1s1ng out of negligence, misconduct etc 

Organizations in India that offer indemnity insurance policies for medical practitioners . 

• Indian Med1colegal Consulting Services o r Pro fessiona l protection groups 

• IMA National Professional Protection Scheme 

• Professional Indemnity Policies by Nat ional General Insurance companies

21. [MAINTENANCE OF MEDICAL R-ECORD~ 

1ENUMERATE THE DUTIES OF DOCTORS IN MAINTAININ§ AND PRES!RVING MEDICAL RECORDS AS PER CODEj 
OF MEDICAL ETHICS 20~ --

• A good medical record serves the interest of the medical practitioner as well as his patients. 
• It is especia lly important for the treating doctor to properly document the management of the patient 

under his ca re. 

• Every physician shall maintain the medical records pertaining to his/ her indoor patients for a period of 3 
years from the date of commencement of the treatment in a standard proforma . 

Objectives 

• Monitoring of the actua l patient 

• M edica l resea rch 

• Medica l/dental or paramedical education 

• For insurance cases, personal injury suits, workmen's compensation case, criminal cases, and w ill cases . 
• For malpractice suits 

• For medical audit and st atisti ca l studies 



MEDICAL LAW and ETHICS 

Accessibility 
• All patients have the right to access their records and obtain a copy of those records . 

• Doctors and health care providers, who are directly involved in the care and treatment of the patient 
have the right to access. 

• Parents of a minor also have access to patient's medical records. 
Release of records 

• Medical records are the property of the hospital or patient's medical practitioner. It is a confidentia l 

communication of the patient and cannot be released without his permission. 

• Request for medical records by patient or authorized attendant should be acknowledged and documents 
should be issued within 72 hours. 

• Discharge summary, referral notes, or death summary must be given without any charge for all including 

patients who discharge themselves against medical advice. 

• Doctors are not under any obligation to produce or surrender their medical records to the police in the 
absence of a valid court warrant. 

• A summons to produce clinical records is a form of court order. Failure to comply is contempt of court 
and may be punished. 

22. DUTIES- OF A D-OC-TOR: 
___, 

• A physician shall uphold the dignity and honor of his profession 

• Maintaining good medical practice : The Principal objective of the medical profession is to render service 

to humanity with full respect for the dignity of profession and man 

• Maintenance of medical records : Every physician shall maintain the medical records pertaining to his/ 

her indoor patients for a period of 3 years from the date of commencement of the treatment in a 

standard proforma 
• Every physician shall display the registration number accorded to him by the State Medical Counci l / 

Medical Council of India in his clinic and in all his prescriptions, certificates, money receipts given to his 

patients. 
• Every physician should, as far as possible, prescribe drugs with generic names and he/ she shall ensure 

that there is a rational prescription and use of drugs 

• A Physician should expose, without fear or favor, incompetent or corrupt, dishonest or unethical conduct 
on the part of member s of the profession. 

23. DUTIES &RESPONSIBILITIES OF A DOCTOR TOWARDS THE STATE1 
• - - - -- -- _ _J 

• Notifiable diseases: Doctor should notify certain notifiable diseases and possible outbreak of an epidemic 

to the public health authorities 

• Poisoning cases : Doctor should assist the police in determining whether the poisoning is accidental, 

suicidal or homicidal. 
• Geneva Convention: The wounded or sick of the armed forces, ship-wrecked, prisoners of war or civilians 

of enemy nationality are to be treated by the physician without any adverse distinction based on sex , 
race and nationality 

• The physician shall observe the laws of the country in regulating the practice of medicine and shall also 
not assist others t o evade such laws. 

• A physician should observe the provisions of the State Acts like Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940; Medical 
Term ination of Pregnancy Act, 1971; Transp lantation of Human Organ Act 1994 etc. 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

24 . DUTIES OF A DOCTOR TO THEIR PATIENTSj 
• Exercise reasonable degree of skill and knowledge in treating a patient 
• Patience and delicacy should characterize t he physician 
• Attending a patient : Once having undertaken a case, the physician should not neglect the patient, nor 

should he withdraw from t he case w ithout giving adequate notice to t he patient and his family. 
• Furnish or prescribe proper and suitable medicines 
• Instructions: should give full instruct ions to his patients or their attendants regarding use of medicines 
• Warning: Doctor should warn patients of the side-effects of the prescribed drug 
• Prognosis: The physician should neither exaggerate nor minimize the gravit y of a pat ient's condition 
• Infectious diseases : In case of an infectious disease, the doctor should warn not only the patient, but also 

third parties who are close t o the patient . 
• For Children and disabled persons who are incapable of taking care of themselves, the doctor should 

arrange for their proper care . 
• Consent: A mentally sound adult (> 18 years) must be told of all the relevant facts in non-medical te

12. Extension Doctrine or Open conse~t 

• In some cases, the doctor cannot arrive at final diagnosis even after appropriate investigations 

• For example, in case of abdominal pain due to undiagnosed condition, the doctor can get extension doctrine 

i.e. consent for laparotomy and proceed - opening the abdomen, find the pathology and proceed to 

treatment according to cause 

• This is called Extension doctrine or Open consent

14. Mal~ngering• 

• Synonym: Shamming 
• Conscious, planned feigning or pretending a disease for self-gain or false allegation 

• Reasons for pretending: to avoid duties, to avoid hard work, to get leave from work, to avoid legal 

responsibil ity etc. 

• E.g.: application of the juice of Calotropis to produce artificial bruise

lmLNTlrlCATIONI 

• Identification Is the determination of individuality of a person based on certain characteristics 
• Total, Absolute or Complete identification - when the identity can be fixed with respect to their name, 

age, sex, address, occupation etc., 
• Partial or Incomplete identification - in putrefied and mutilated bodies and in skeletal remains, a 

forensic pathologist can help in determining features like race, sex, age, stature etc., from the 
morphology of the body parts. 

M edicolcgal importance ot identification 

• Civil

, To identify the accused and victim in criminal cases (assault, murder, sexual offences)

,. Decomposed, mutilated or skeletonized bodies

3. ~ ONADAL DYSGE~Eilij 
• Any congenital developmental disorder characte rized by a progressive loss of primordial germ cells on 

the developing gonads of an embryo 
• This leads to extremely hypoplastic and dys-functioning gonads mainly composed of fibrous tissue -

streak gonads 
• The accompanying hormonal failure also prevents the development of secondary sex characterist ics in 

either sex, resulting in a sexually infantile female appearance and infertility.

4 . CONC[Alf O SEX 

• Concealed sex is hiding one's se>< by wearing the dress of opposite sex. It occurs under the following 
circumstances. 

• Criminals conceal their sex, so that they ca nnot be identified. Simple physical examinat ion detects t he 
sex. 

• After committing a murder, criminals may mutilate the face and external genitalia t o hide the identity of
t  
he victim. Finding uterus or prostate detects t he sex 

• In advanced putrefaction, the external genitalia are bloated due to gaseous distension. Identifying th
u e

te  
rus or prostate which resist putrefaction for longer time detects sex 

• Transvestism Is sexual gratification is attained by wearing the dress of opposite sex.

S. rMIX ED DENTITION * 

• The development of human t eeth occurs in 3 stages 
,, Deciduous dentition 
,- Mi><ed dentition 
,- Permanent dentition 

• Deciduous or Temporary teeth 

,, 20 in number - 4 incisors, 2 canines, 4 molars in each jaw 

, lower central incisor is the f irst to erupt at 6 to 8 months of age 

,, Second molar is t he last to erupt between 20 and 30 months of age 

• Permanent teeth 

,, 32 in number - 4 incisors, 2 canines, 4 premolars, 6 molars in each jaw 
, First molar is t he first to erupt at 6 to 7 years of age 
, Third molar is the last to erupt after 17 years of age 

,, Successional permanent teeth - erupt in the place of deciduous teeth - permanent incisors, canines 

and premolars 
, Superadded permanent teeth - do not have deciduous predecessors - permanent molars 

• Mi><ed dentition 
, After t he eruption of first molar (6-7 years) the temporary teeth begin to fall off 

, Temporary canines are the last temporary teeth to fall off at 11-12 years 

,, In t his age group 6 - 12 years, both temporary and permanent teeth are present 

,, This is called mi><ed dentition 

Medicolegal importance of teeth 

• Age estimation 

• Race identificat ion 
• Identification - as they resist put refaction and environmental factors like fire for a long t ime t han other 

parts of the body 
• Se>< and blood group ca n be determined from the cells of pulp cavity 

• Dentures are used for ident ification 

• Bite marks are also used for identification 
• Habit of the person such as tobacco chewing - can be identified 

6. ~ USTAFSON'S CHANGES l!J THE tr_ETH** , 

~ ETHODS OF AGE E~TIMATION USING TEETH AFTER_21 VE~~ 

• Age est imat ion in adults after 21 years can be done based on the wear and tear changes of teeth -
Gustafson's method 

• Usefu l in dead persons only (teeth need to be extracted to study the changes) 

• Usually t he incisors or canines on the upper jaw are preferred. Molars are not useful 

• This is the most useful method to estimate age between 25 to 60 years

8. /DACTYLOGRAPHY****/ DERMATOGLY~ IC~ 
• Refers to t he scientific study of f ingerprints as a method of identification. 

• Also called Galton system, Oermatoglyphics, Fingerprint system 

• Fingerprint system was suggested by Sir Francis Galton 
• Fingerprint bureau was first established in Kolkata by William Herschel 

• It is the study of epidermal ridges and their configurations of fingers 
• They are formed during t he intrauterine life and are const ant throughout life 

• They are highly individualistic, i.e. they are different even in monozygotic twins 
• So, Fingerprint match is widely accepted as the most reliable evidence of identification 



VISION _F ORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

Types of fingerprints 

• LOOPS - most common type (66%) 

• WHORLS 

• ARCHES 

• COMPOSITE (mixed type)

Fingerprint characteristics 

• Ridge endings, ridge bifurcat ions, trifurcations, lake formation, broken ridges, short ridges etc., 

• Final ident ification is made based on comparison of these characteristics 

• In practice, 16-20 points of fine comparison are accepted as proof of identity

Bridge s Spur 

Eye or enclosure ---<::)-- Double bifurcation

Fingerprints encountered at the scene of crime 

• Visible print - impression made by a finger contaminated with blood, ink, dyes etc., 

• Plastic print - impression made on soft substances like soap, wax, cheese etc., 

• Latent print - impression formed due to sebaceous and sweat gland secretions. Not visible readily. 

Developed with reagents to make them visible 

Permanent impairment of fingerprint pattern occurs only in 

• Leprosy 

• Electrical injury 

• Radiation injury 

Fingerprint reader (FINDER) 

• A computerized automatic fingerprint reading system 

• It can read and compare fingerprint within seconds 

• Fingerprints of 8 fingers (except little f inger) are stored 



IDENTIFICATION 

Med1colegal importance 
• Fingerprints left at the scene of crime can fix the exact identity of the person and thus helps in 

identificat ion of the criminal 
• To identify unknown persons involved in accidents and decomposed bodies 
• Now-a-days increasingly used for biometrics in schools, colleges and offices 
• Identification in case of accidental exchange of newborns

10. TATTOO MARi<s-] 
• Tattoo marks are designs made on the skin by multiple small punctures with needles dipped in co loring 

material 
• They are permanent as it stains the dermis 
• Dyes commonly used - Indian ink, vermilion, indigo, Prussian blue 
• Usually tattoo marks are seen over arms, forearms and chest but can be seen anywhere 
• Dye injected into the dermis usually lasts longer, if it enters deeper layers, they are removed by 

phagocytes

Old, faded tattoo marks made visible by 
• Infra-red photography 
• Use of UV light 

• Rubbing the part and examining under magnifying lens 

Tattoo marks in decomposed bodies can be visualized by 
• Removal of epidermis makes tattoo clearly visible 
• Treatment with 3% H202 

• Treatment with 0.5% caustic potash 

• Erased tattoo - histological examination of the draining lymph node 

M edicolegal importance: 
• Identification of a person - tattoo marks provide information regarding race, religion, language, name of 

beloved person, mental makeup, dates of important personal events etc. 
• Social status of a person - by the kind of dye used, the type of design 
• Sexual orientations - blue bird design on the back of the hand is common among homosexuals 
• Drug addicts - in order to conceal injection marks, t hey imprint t attoos over those areas 
• Criminal gangsters - belonging to a particular organization w ill have simi lar pattern of tattoos on similar 

parts of the body 

11. BITE MARK~ 
• Bitemarks are impressions caused by human or animal bite 
• It is usually patterned abrasions or contusions. Rarely lacerations 

Medicolegal importance: 

• It can be caused offensively (accused biting victim) or defensively (victim biting accused) 
• In sexual offences, bite marks (love bites) are seen over neck, cheek, breasts, abdomen or thighs Love 

bites usually indicates consented sex 

• Bite marks can be seen in child abuse 

• Can be seen on inanimate objects like fruits, cheese, chocolate etc . 

• Examination of bite marks can provide evidence to identify the accused 

• In fresh bite marks, st eri le swabs are taken, and blood grouping and DNA profiling can be done

12. !FORENSIC ODONTOLOGY AND ITS MEDICOLEGAL IMPOICTANCj 

Forensic odontology (forensic dentistry) involves the proper collection, management, interpretation, evaluation 

and presentation of dental evidence for legal proceedings. It is simply defined as the application of dental 

knowledge to aid in the administration of justice. 

Identification of the deceased 
• Dental identification has proved to be extremely useful and reliable over the years, but is dependent on 

the availability, adequacy and accuracy of antemortem (AM) dental records that can be compared with 

the postmortem (PM) dental findings . 
• Teeth are one of the most resilient structures in the human body and can survive most conditions of 

nature that may destroy or change other body tissues. 
• For example, teeth (particularly the posteriors) often survive high temperatures, along with restorations 

and sometimes acrylic dentures may survive . 
• DNA can be extracted from the pulp chamber to crossmatch and identify a victim. 

• Amelogenin gene can be extracted from the pulp of a tooth and then analyzed using a PCR to determine 

the sex of the victim. 
• Ameloglyphics, or enamel rod patterns can be util ized for identification. Like fingerprints, these are 

unique to each individual. Unlike fingerprints, they are highly resistant, and cannot be intentionally 

burned or cut to change the pattern. 

Bite mark analysis 
• Bitemarks can occur during a variety of human activity including assault, domestic violence, rape, elder 

abuse, self-defense, sports, accidents, infanticide, or other homicide 

• Bite mark analysis focuses on identifying perpetrators by comparing dental records to a bite mark left on 

the victim or at the scene - for example, in food or chewing gum. 

• Bite marks may also be found on perpetrators themselves, left by the victim in an act of self-defense

15. ~ TYPING/ DNA FINGERPRINTING! 
• DNA finger printing w as developed by Dr. Alec Jeffreys in 1985 
• Based on the fact that each individual (except identical twins) has a unique DNA pattern. 
• CODIS - Combined DNA Index System is a DNA database system funded by the US FBI 

Autopsy samples for DNA analysis 

• Fresh bodies - blood preserved in EDTA 
• Muscle is the most ideal source for extracting DNA. 100mg muscle with NS or dimethyl sulphoxide 

(DMSO) solution saturated with sodium chloride as a preservative. 

• Skin - second best after muscle 
• Bone marrow - not preferred in fresh bodies; only when skeletal remains are available; Femur or 

humerus are preferable; no preservative needed. 
• Hair (10-20) picked using forceps without damaging the root (cut hair without root is not useful) 
• Tooth pulp 

Samples from living subjects 

• Blood preserved in EDTA- most common. 

• Buccal epithelial cells 
• Hair follicles with roots (plucked hair) 

• Saliva, Semen, sweat 
• RBCs - cannot be used as they do not have a nucleus

DELusioN1 
• It is a false belief in something which is not a fact, and which persists even after its falsity has been

• There is disturbance of thought process in delusion 

• It is a tip of iceberg - indicates a deep sea ted mental disorder

z. MENTAL HEALTH CARE (MHC) ACT 2017 

• Replaces the M ental Health Act, 1987 

• Provides for mental healthca re and services for persons with mental illness. 
Rights ot a Person with mental illness: 

• Right to access good quali ty menta l healthca re and t reatment from appropriate establishments 
• Right to communit y living 

• Right to protection from cruel, inhuman and degrading treatment 
• Right to equality and non-discriminat ion 

• Right to information 

• Right to confidentiality . 

• Right to access medical records 

• Right to persona l contacts and communication . 

• Right to legal aid 

• Right t o make complaints about deficiencies in provision of serv ices 

Central Mental Health (CMH) Authority and State Mental Health Authority : 

• To registe r, supervi se and manage a register of mental hea:th professionals (psychiatrist s, nurses, cl inical 
psycho logists, also psychiatric social workers ) & establ ishments. 

Mental Health Review Board: 

• To be set up mostly in every district as per the CMH recommendation and will be for a term of 5 years. 
Mental Health Establishment (MHE) Registration 

• Initia lly MHE get provisional registrat ion having validity for 12 months. 
• The authority shall conduct an aud it of al l registered M HE every 3 years. 

ADMISSION, TREATMENT AND DISCHARGE OF A PERSON WITH MENTAL ILLNESS (PMI) 

• Independent admission and discharge: 
, Every major person, shall have a right to make an advance directive in w ri ting, specifying the way 

he/she wishes to be cared/not cared for and treated/not treated for a mental illness; and ca n 
appoint nominated representative (NR) for his care and treatment. 

, Any major person who considers himself to be mentally ill and desires t o be admit ted to any MHE 
for treatment, may request the Medical officer in charge of the MHE. 

, The medical officer can admit him if he is satisfied that the severity mental illness requires 
admission, and he will be benefitted from admission and treatment. 

, This person shall be discharged immediately when he requests so. 
, If no request is made, he will be discharged within 30 days. 

• Supported admission up to 30 days: 
, When a nominated representative applies for admission, 
, the patient should be admitted if he has been examined independently by a psychiatrist and a 

mental health profess ional (MHP), 
,. on the day of admission or within 7 days and 
,. if both conclude that admission is necessary. 
, The admission will be limited to 30 days. 



r-----------~~....:.,,;.V;ISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY ~ 

• Supportt•d ;idmbslon beyond 30 days: 
.. If ,l PMI require!, admission and trea tment beyond 30 days, or 
·.. A perso11 di!>charged requires re-admission within 7 days, 
,. r he PM I should be e>< amined by two psychiatrists and can be admitted for a ma><imum period of 

90 days. 
'> Admission beyond the period of ninety days may be extended for a period of 120 days at the first 

instance and therea fter for a period of 180 days each time by the board. 
• Admbsion of minors: 

,.. A nominated representative or parent of the minor shall apply for admission. 
'). The minor should be e><amined by two psychiatrists independently and conclude that he has 

illness of severity that requires admission. 
• Ph'i_slcal re,;traint and Seclusion: 

'>. M ay only be used when it is the only means avai lable to prevent imminent and immediate harm 
to the PM I or to others. 

~ It should be authori zed by the psychiatrist in-charge . 

Separation of child from the mother 
• A child< 3 years and mother (receiving treatment at MH E) shall ordinarily not be separated. 
• If the treating Psychiatrist , is o f the opinion that there is risk of harm to the child from the w oman due to 

her mental il lness, the child shall be temporarily separated. 
• The decision shall be reviewed every 15 days during the woman's stay in t he MHE. 
• If separation e><ceeds 30 days at a stretch, it should be approved by the respect ive Authority. 

Emergency treatment: t o prevent, 
• Death or irreversible harm to the hea lth of the person. 
• A person inflicting serious harm to himse lf or to others. 
• Serious da;nage t o the properties belonging to himself or others. 
• Emergency treatment is limited to a period of 72 hours or till his mental illness has been assessed, 

whichever is earlier. 

Prohibited procedures: 
• Electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) as a form of emergency trea tment . 
• ECT without giving muscle relaxants and anesthesia. 
• ECT for minors 
• Sterilization for men and women 
• Chained in any manner or form. 
• Psychosurgery w ithout getting prior perm ission from the board . 

Decriminalization of suicide (suicide is not a crime) 
• Any person w ho attempts to commit suicide shall be presumed, unless proved otherwise, to have severe 

!:itress and shall not be tried and punished under S 309 IPC. (Section Deleted in BNS)

SHORT NOTES: 

1. Delusions*******: Refer to Essay Q. No. 1 

2. !HALLUCINATIONS**ttj 

• It is a disturbance of perception 
• Hallucination is a false sense of perception without sensory stimulus or object to produce it 
• Seen in schizophrenia, epilepsy, drugs, alcohol withdrawal etc. 

Types 

• Visual hallucinations: the person visualizes non-existent objects 
• Auditory hallucinations: the person hears voice or sound without any source 
• Olfactory hallucinations: false sense of smelling without a source 
• Gustatory hallucinations: without any food or drink, the person experiences different taste 
• Tactile hallucinations: sense of insects crawling over the skin 
• Psychomotor hallucinations: feeling of a movement of a part of the body, thought there is no such 

movement in reality 
• Lilliputian hallucinations: perceiving objects much smaller than they really are 
• Hypnagogic hallucinations: hallucinations that occur before sleep 
• Synaesthesia: stimulus perceived by a sense organ other than the one that actually perceives it. E.g.: 

seeing music, hearing colours etc 

3. ~MPULSE**! 
• Impulse is a sudden and irresistible force compelling a person to the conscious performance of some 

action without motive or forethought and preparation 
• A sane person is capable of controlling an impulse. 
• An insane person having no judgment and no reasoning power, and no capacity to understand the facts, 

may do things on impulse 
Types of impulses 

• Kleptomania: An irresistible desire to steal articles of little value and of no use to the person 
• Pyromania: Irresistible desire to set fire to things 
• Dipsomania: An irresistible desire for alcoholic drinks at periodic intervals 
• Oniomania: Irres istible desire for shopping 
• Mutilomania: Irresistible desire to injure and mutilat e animals. 
• Trichotillomania: irresistible desire to pull out hair 
• Satyriasis: Irresistible desire for sexual intercourse including perversions 
• Suicidal and homicidal impulses: e.g.: run amok in cannabis poisoning 



"~----~--~~-~~-~ VISION • FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

4. OBSESSIVE COMPULSIVE DISORDER 

• Obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD) is a diso rder in which people have> recurring, unwanted thoughts, 
ideas or sensations (obsessions) that make them feel driven to do something repetitively 
(compulsions) . 

• They usual ly occur in neurotic people, well able to discharge the ordinary responslbillti c> c, o f life> 
• Common Obsessions 

, Fear of germs or contaminat ion 
, Unwanted forbidden or taboo thoughts involving sex, re ligion, or harm 
, Aggressive thought s tow ards others o r self 
, Having things symmetr ica l or in a perfect o rder 

• Common Compulsions 
, Excessive cleaning and/ or handw ashing 
, Ordering and arranging t hings in a particular, precise w ay 
, Repeatedly checking on things, such as repeat edly checking to see if the door is locked or that the> 

oven is off 
, Compulsive counting 

• Many people without OCD have distressing thoughts o r repetiti ve behaviors. 
• How ever, these thoughts and behaviors do not typically disrupt daily life 
• For people wit h OCD, t houghts are persis tent, and behaviors are rigid 
• Diagnosis - requires t he presence of obsessions and/or compulsions that are time-consuming (more t han 

one hour a day), cause significant distress, and impair work o r social fun ctioning 
Treatm ent 

• M ed ications: SSRls 
• Psychotherapy: Cognitive behavioural therapy (Exposure and Response prevention) 

j PSYCHOPATHIC DISORDER, 

• Psychopaths or sociopaths have repeat ed or recurrent episodes of impulsive behavior not desirable and 
agreeable to t he society 

• They are neither insane nor mentally defect ive 
• They have deep rooted abnormalities of personal ity, and hence unable to conform to the convent ional 

st andards of behavior 
• The basic defect appears to be moral rather than psychological or neurologica l 

~t 1o lor 1 

• Few people develop psychopathic personality due to adverse environment during childhood, w hich 
taught them to adapt anti-social way of life in future 

• Others have deficiency and defects in personal education and t ra ining 
• In few people, excessive frustration is the cause 

' ,n(1c11 i.l pt ct 5 
• They have least sense of social responsibility 
• The activities are essentia lly anti -social 

• They learn very little or have least desire t o learn from their past experience to correct t hem selves

7 CIVIL RESPONSIBILITY OF A MENTALLY ILL PERSON* 
Competency as a witness 

• A mentally Iii person cannot be a competent witness 
• But in lucid interval, he can give evidence 

Management of property 
• A mentally ill person is unable to manage his property 
• A Dist rict court can appoint a manager to manage his property 
• The manager does whatever is best for t he management of his property with the knowledge and 

guidance of the court 
Business contract 

• If it is proved that, at the time of signing a contract, one of the two parties were of unsound mind, the 
contract becomes legally invalid 

• Mental illness developing after a legal agreement, does not invalidate the contract 
Consent 

• Consent given by a mentally ill person is legally invalid (5. 90 IPC) (Cl 28 BNS) 
Marriage contract 

• If it is proved that, at the t ime of marriage, one of t he spouses was insane, then the marriage is declared 
null and void (invalid) 

• Insanity developing later during married life, which was not present at the t ime of marriage, is a ground 
for divorce, but not a ground for nullity of marriage 

Testamentary capacity: Refer to next question 

8, ~STAMENTARY CAPACITY* 
• Testamentary capacity is the mental capacity of a person to make a valid will 
• A Will is a document detailing the disposal of property owned by a person which is prepared by him 

during his lifetime, but effective only after his death 
• The person making the will is ca lled a testator 

Eligibility to make a valid will 
• Age greater than 18 years 
• Sound mind at the t ime of making w il l (compos mentis) 
• An insane person cannot make a valid will. But during lucid interval, he can make a valid will

• An intoxicated person cannot make a valid w ill, unless he is certif ied by a doctor t hat his senses are in 
control 

• A deaf, dumb or a blind person ran make a valid w ill, 1f he can communicate effectively 
Condit ions 

• The person should make a wi ll voluntari ly 
• He should be 1n sound mind 
• He should have thorough knowledge of his property 
• He should not be under the inf luence of alcohol or drugs or any other force 

Procedure 

• The w ill must always be in w riting 
• Holograph Will - written by the testator 1n his own handwriting 
• It must be signed by two w itnesses, neit her of whom should be beneficiar ies 

9 . a) Mc Naughten's rule

CRIMINAL RESPONSIBILITY Of A MENTALLY ILL PERSON 
• A person may plead insanity to avoid 

, Conviction - if the accused was insane at the t ime of alleged crime 
, Trial - if the accused is insane and cannot pleat 
, Capital punishment - w hen t he condemned prisoner is insane 

• The law presumes that every person is of sound mind, and he is responsible for his actions 
• The defence has to prove t hat the accused is insane 
• The concept of crim inal responsibil ity is based on mens rea 
• Mens rea: criminal intent or guilty mind 
• Actus reus: criminal (physical) act 
• Crime = mens rea + actus reus 
• That means physical act alone does not make a person guilty, the mental component (criminal intent) is 

also equa lly important 
• So, an insane person who is unable to understand the nature and consequences of his act, is exempted 

from cr iminal responsibility 
Tests for determining criminal responsibility 

• Mc Naughten's rule: Every man is presumed to be sane, and responsible for his crimes, unless the 
contrary be proved. To establish a defence on the ground of insanity, it must be clearly proved that, at 
the t ime of committing the crir.ie, he was suffering from such a defect of reason, from abnormality of 
m ind, that he did not know the nature and quality of the act he was doing or that what he was doing was 
w rong . e 

• Section 84 IPC (Cl 22 BNS) states that, " Nothing is an offence which is done by a person, who, at the t,1'11 
of doing it, 15 

by reason of unsoundness of mind is incapable of knowing the nature of the act, or that he 
doing what is either wrong or contrary to the law" 

• Other tests 
Durham's Rule or The Product test 

, Curren's Rule 
, The American law Institute test 



FORENSIC PSYCHIATRY 

10. [MEDICOLEGAL IMPORTANCE OF LUCIO INTERVAL•' 

• Lucid interval is a period occurring in mental illness, during which all the symptoms of mental illness 
disappear completely. 

• It is common in mania & melancholia. 

• As the individual is able to judge his acts soundly, he becomes legally liable for his acts. 

• When a person in lucid interval commits an offence, he may take the plea of previous insanity. It is 

difficult to ascertain whether he was suffering from mental illness at the time of commission of crime 

• The period varies with individual and with time in the same person. So it is difficult to find out whether 

the act was committed during the period of lucid interval or not 

• A person in lucid interval can make a valid will. 

• He is competent to give evidence in a court of law 

11. ~ IFFERENTIATE BETWEEN TRUE INSANITY FROM FEIGNED INSANIT~* 

Feigned insanity: A sane person pretending like mentally ill is called feigned insanity 
Motives: 

• An accused feigns insanity to avoid trial 

• A convicted person feigns insanity to avoid punishment 

• Pol ice officer or army personnel feigns insanity to quit service 

• To avoid certain civil responsibilit ies like marriage, busines~ contracts

13. ,POST TRAUMATIC STRESS DISORDER] 

• Posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD) is a psychiatric disorder that may occur in people w ho have 
experienced or wit nessed a t ra umatic event such as a natural disaster, a serious accident, a terrorist act, 
war/combat, or rape or who have been threatened w ith death, sexual violence or serious injury 

Symptoms 
• Re-experiencing symptoms 

,- Flashbacks-reliving the trauma over and over 
, Nightmares 
, Frightening thoughts 

• Avoidance symptoms 
, Staying away from people, places, events, or objects that are reminders of the t raumat ic 

experience 
, Avoiding thoughts or feelings related to the traumatic even 

• Arousal and reactivity symptoms 
,- Being easily startled 
, Feeling tense or "on edge" 
,- Difficulty sleeping 
,- Angry outburst 

• Cognition and mood symptoms 

, Trouble remembering key features of the traumatic event 
, Negative thoughts about oneself or the world 
,, Distorted feelings like guilt or blame 
, Loss of interest in enjoyable activities 

Treatment 
• Medications: Antidepressants 
• Psychotherapy: Cognitive behavioural therapy, Prolonged Exposure therapy 



FORENSIC PSYCHIATRY 

14. AUTOMATISM] 

• A condition in which the conscious performance of an action is impaired to such extent that the person 
does not remember anything subsequently 

• Automatic conduct of a person (non-conscious act) 
• The person is not legally responsible for his actions 
• Post traumatic automatism - head injury, concussion 
• Post epileptic automatism - temporal lobe epilepsy 
• Poisons - carbon monoxide, alcohol, barbiturates 
• Others - hypoglycaemia, somnambulism, stress and emotional upsets 

VIVA QUESTIONS: 

1. ~ uros~ 
• A relatively mtld personality disorder characterized by excessive anxiety or indecision and a degree of 

social or interpersonal maladjustment. 

• Neuroses involve chronic distress, but they do not include delusions and hallucinations 
• Types: Anxiety neurosis, hysterical neurosis, phobic neurosis, depressive neurosis 

2. !111usio~ 

• Illusion is a false interpretation by the senses of an external object or stimulus which has real existence 
• E.g.: a person sees a rope and mistakes it for a snake 

3. [ breactionl 
• Abreaction is reviving and bringing into consciousness, the traumatic experiences of the past, which the 

patient has subconsciously 

4. Persecutory delusion 
Refer to Essay Q. No. 1 

5. ~ ctus reu~ 
• The concept of criminal responsibility is based on mens rea 
• Mens rea: criminal intent or guilty mind 
• Actus reus: criminal (physical) act 
• Crime = mens rea + actus reus 
• That means physical act alone does not make a person guilty, the mental component (criminal intent) is 

also equally important 

6, Mens rea: Refer previous question 

7. ~u~ 
• Fugue is a state of altered awareness during which the affected person forgets his identity, assumes a 

new identity, leaves homes and wanders 
• Seen in depression, hysteria and schizophrenia

• The nature of information collected usually includes 
, Biographical informat ion (age, marital st at us, occupation)

Secondary information (family history, police records, diaries) 

• It 1s also called psychiatric autopsy, retrospective death assessment , reconstructive evaluation and 

equivocal death analysis 

6 VIRTUAL AUTOPSV-(VIRTOPSY} , DIGITAL AUTOPSY 
• V1rtopsy 1s a word combining 'vi rtual' and 'autopsy' 
• It 1s a bloodless and minimally invasive procedure on the dead body to find the cause o f death 
• It employs imaging methods such as CT, MRI, and 30 surface scanning and reconstruction t echniques 
• V1rtopsy is a mult1d1sc1plinary technique that combines forensic medicine, pathology, radiology, computer 

graphic~ b1omechan1cs and physics 
• Vtrtopsy can be employed as an alternative to standard autopsies for broad and systemic examination of 

the whole body 
• Advantages 

, Less time consuming 
, Preservation of the body in a virtual form 
, Observer-independent (removes subjec tive error) 
, H1gh-prec1s1on, rnntaminat1on-free sampl ing. 
,. Forensic evidence 1s not touched or destroyed while conducting autopsy 
, Clean. bloodless v1sualt1at1on of the documentat ion 
,. Reduces the risk of infections contracted from blood or o ther tissue fluid s 
, Renders respect to rel1g1ous sentiments. 

• Disadvantages 
,. Cannot determine the color of internal organs and color changes. 
, Cannot determine all the pathological condit ions (e.g. inflammation) 
, Cannot determine the infection status of tissue 
,. Difficult to d1fferent1ate antemortem from postmortem wounds and post mortem artefacts. 
, Cost 
, No JUdtC1al val1d1ty (yet ) 

7 NEGATIVE AUTOPSY 

• No cause of death found in autopsy 

• Microscopic examination, toxicological analysis, histopathological examination and m icrobiological 
investigations are all negative. 

• Causes of negative autopsy: 
, Decomposit ion (most common cause) 

, Organ fa ilure . reflex vagal inhibit ion, cardiac arrhythmias, laryngeal spasm 

, Inadequate or m isleading history or symptoms prior to death like epilepsy et c. 

, Inadequate o r improper external examinat ion - missing needle pricks, snakebit e mark, electric 
burns; o r internal examination - air embolism, pneumothorax etc. 

, Insufficient laboratory examinat ions 

, Lack of skill and expertise of the doctor
