10. ~ IR EM_B _OL_IS_M,  
• Air embolism is an uncommon, but potentia lly catastroph ic, event that occurs as a consequence of the 

entry of air into the vasculature 
VENOUS AIR EMBOLISM (pulmonary air embolism) 

• When air enters the systemic venous circulation and travels t o the right ventricle and pulmonary 

circulation 
• Predominantly iatrogenic complication 

• Injecting more t han 100 ml of air into the venous system at rates greater than 100 ml/scan be fatal 

• Causes 
,. Cent ral venous catheterization , Hemodialysis 
, Penetrating & blunt chest trauma , Pulmonary barotraumas 
,. High-pressure mechanical ventilation , Decompression sickness (Caisson's 
,, Thoracocentesis disease) 
, Neurosurgical procedures conducted in the sitting position 
, Incised wounds of the lower cervical or clavicular regions involving jugular or subclavian veins 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

ARTERIAL AIR EMBOLISM 

• When a,r enters the arterial system 

• 2 ml of air in the cerebral circulation can be fatal 
• 0.5 ml of air into a coronary artery can cause cardiac arrest 
• Causes 

, Cardiopulmonary bypass 
, lntravascular interventions 
, Rarely after penetrating lung injury 

Autopsy findings 
• X-ray examination of the whole body may detect large quantities of air 
• Head should be opened first , and the surface vessels of brain examined for gas bubbles 

Demonstration of air embolism 
a. Before dissection of other thoracic organs, the pericardium is opened, heart is lifted upwards, and apeY le 

cut with a knife. Left ventricle is filled with froth if air is present in sufficient quantity to cause death 
b. The pericardium is cut, and the edges are grasped Sac is f illed w ith water and the heart is punctured wit h 

a scalpel and twisted Bubbles of air will escape 1f air is present 
c. Pyrogallol test: Air 1s brought in contact with alkaline Pyrogallol solution, which turns brown - indicates 

antemortem air embolism

13. EXAM INATION AND DISSECTION OF HEART DURING AUTOPSY1 
• The per icard ium is o pened and examined fo1 the presence of fluid 01 blood . 
• Then t ests for air embolism done 1f suspected (Refer Short Note No. 2) 

• Examination done for adhesions, discolorat ion, excess fat deposition and aneury~mal dtlatat1on 
• The heart is removed by cutt ing thr ough the roots of aorta and other great ves~el~ 
• It is washed and kept in the dissect ion table, with the apex facing the dissector and anterior surface 

upwards 

• The heart has to be dissected in the direction of blood flow I.e. right atrium ➔ right ventricle ➔ left 
atrium ➔ left ventricle 

• Right atrium is cut between the openings of SVC and IVC and examined 

• An Enterotome is introduced into the right atrium, cuts through the tncu~pid orifice and opens the right 
ventricle 

• The circumference of intact va lves can be measured by inserting specially maue graduated wnes 

• Similar procedure is followed for the left side and the left atrium and left ventricle!:> are opened 

• Now wash the heart, remove all the clots and w eigh the hea rt 

• The endocardium, valves are exam ined 

• The coronary ostia are examined 

• Seria l cuts are made at int ervals not m ore t han 3mm first Into the left coronary artery, then thr 
circumflex branch and t he an terior descending branch 

• The right coronary artery is also examined in a similar w ay

14 DISSECTION Of STOMACH DURING AUTOPSvj 

• Th e stomach Is separated from t he pancreas. 
• It 1s removed before evisceration by tying two ligatures each at the ca rdiac and pylo ric end!> . (double 

ligation) 
• The stomach is removed by cutting between the double ligatures at both ends. 
• It is routinely opened along the greater curvature. 
• The mucosa is examined for any sta ins, congestion, hemorrhage, desquamatlon, ulceration, sloughing or 

perforation . 
• The contents are noted in terms of quantity, nature, sta te of digestion, color and smell. 

15 . PRESERVATION OF SAMPLES FOR HISTOPATHOLOG~ 
• It is the microscopic study of the tissues of the deceased 
• It deals with the microscopic analysis of various changes at cellular/tissue level and thus helps in finding 

the cause of death 
• A portion of the affected area as seen by gross examination should be removed along with a portion of 

normal tissues at edge!> 
• Specimen rinsed to remove blood clots 
• Should be cut into pieces of 1 cm thickne!>s for proper preservation 
• Preservative: 10% neutral formalin or 95% alcohol 
• The volume of preservative should be 6 times the volume of ti ssue 
• The specimen is allowed to fix for several days before processing 

16 . EXAMINATION OF SKELETAL REMAIN~ 
• In medico-legal practice, many t imes, skeletal remains are received for autopsy. Careful examination may 

yield important information in all such cases 
• The bones are arranged in anatomic order and a skeletal chart is drawn, indicat ing which bones are 

present. 
• Human or animal skeleton: It is easy to say if the head, trunk or limbs are available. When p ieces of 

muscle are available, definite opinion can be given 
by precipitin test or anti-globulin inhibition test 

• One or more individual: Reconstruction of the skeleton is done and observed for disproportion in t he 
size of various bones, reduplication and articulation. 

, If the bones are suspected to be from more than one skeleton, short wave UV light can be used 
to separate them 

, It emits different co lor due to fluo rescence of organic and inorganic substances in t he bones 
• Race: can be determined from nasal bridge height, shape of nasa l apert ure and palate, teeth (incisors), 

the skull (cephalic index), pelvis and from features and indices of different long bones, 
• Age : Closure of the cranial sutures, erupt ion of teeth, ossif ication centers of bones, changes in the 

mandible, symphyseal surface o f the pubis, sacrum, and margin of t he glenoid cavit y of the scapula; 
ca lcification of laryngeal and sternal cartilages and hyoid bone are useful for age estimation . After t he 
completion of bony un ion, exact age cannot be determined 

• Stature: can be determined from long bones using d iffe rent formulae (Pearson, Trotter and Gieser, 
Dupertius & Hadden). To find the length of the bone, Hepburn Osteometric board is used . 



MEDICOLEGAL AUTOPSY 

• Sex: can be determined from the anatomical configuration of the pelvis, skull, mandible, diameter of 
head of femur and humerus, and measurements of femur, tibia, humerus and radius. Recognizable sex 
differences are present only after puberty 

• Identity: 

' Supernumerary ribs, congenital bony defects, malunited fractures, healed fractures or 
deformities of bone, are helpful 

,,. X-ray comparison of trabecular patterns 
-,. Superimposition technique 

• Cause of death: 

:, Fractures of the skull, cervical vertebrae, hyoid bone or ribs 
, Bones may be subjected to chemical analysis for the detection of metallic poisons (e.g. arsenic) 

• Type of weapon: depending on the nature of injury of the bones, the type of weapon e.g. hard blunt 
weapon, a light or heavy sharp cutting weapon, a pointed weapon or a firearm can be determined 

• Time since death: 
, No soft tissue attached, bone not completely dry or dry with putrid smell - death < 3 months 
,. Bone is dry with no putrid smell, normal color - 3 months to 1 year 
, After that time since death estimated by nitrogen content, amino acid content, UV fluorescence 

or Carbon dating of the bone 

17. A body was removed from the grave in front of a Magistrate as relatives alleged suspicion of foul play in the 
causation of death. After the Magistrate's inquest, an autopsy was performed. 
a) Mention the name given to the above mentioned legal procedure. 
b} Under which section of CrPC, the above mentioned legal procedure is performed? 
c) Mention the role of the doctor in conducting the above procedure. 

a) NAME OF THE PROCEDURE: EXHUMATION 
b) CrPC Section: 176 CrPC (Cl 196 BNSS) 
c) ROLE OF THE DOCTOR: 

-
EXHUMATION • 

• Exhumation is the lawful digging out of a buried body from the grave for medico-legal examination. 
Reasons for Exhumation: 
Criminal cases: 

• suspected homicide disguised as suicide and postmortem done. But relatives accuse that the 
postmortem is inadequate and appeal to Magistrate. 

• Suspected homicide disguised as natural death and body was buried without postmortem. 
• Death due to criminal abortion. 
• Death due t o criminal negligence. 
• To retrieve vital objects (e.g. bullet) from the body. 

Civil cases: for identification of the diseases in cases of 
• Inheritance of property 
• Insurance claims 
• Burial of wrong body by fraud or mistake. 



-------- ---==~ 
VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

Aut horization· 

• Executive Magistrate or Judicial Magistrate can give orders for exhumation (Police cannot order) 

• Execut ive Magist rates who can 1uthorize Exhumation, 
, District Magistrate (Collector), 

, Deputy Collector or Sub-Collector 

, Sub-divisional Magistrate (Revenue Divisional Officer) or 
, Tahsildar 

Procedure: 

• Preferably done in daytime 

• Magistrate, Police officer and the Medical officer must be present at the site 

• The grave is identified . 

• The soil is carefully removed, and the stages can be photographed . 

• The corpse, when found, should be photographed in the position in which it was found . 

• The corpse 1s then gently removed from the grave. 

• The body should be identified by the relatives . 

• Autopsy (first or second) done at the spot. 
• Materials and viscera preserved for chemical analysis: 

, S00g of soil from above, below and in contact with the body 
,. Tuft of hair - head, body and pubic region 
,. Bones - usually shaft of femur. 
,. Liver, stomach and intestines. 

• There is no time limit in India for exhumat ion. It can be done even many years after burial.

19 PROTOCOLS FOR CONDUCTION OF MEDICO-LEGAL AUTOPSIES IN CASES OF DEATH IN CUSTODY OR 

FOLLOW ING VIOLATION OF HUMAN RIGHTS AS PER NATIONAL HUMAN RIGHTS COMMISSION GUIDELINES: 

CUSTODIAL DEATHS/DEATHS DURING THE COURSE OF POLICE ACTION 

• Magistrate Inquest should be conducted in every custodial death 

• Magistrate enquiry should be completed within 2 months 

• Autopsy should be conducted by a panel of doctors 

• The post-mortem report ~long with videography and the Magistrate Enquiry report must be sent to the 
NHRC within 2 months of the incident 



MEDICOLEGAL AUTOPSY 

Precautions be taken before conducting post-mortem examination: 
• Clothing on the body of the deceased should not be removed by the police or any one 
• Clothes should be collected, examined as well as preserved and sealed by the doctor conducting 

the autopsy, and should be sent for further examination at the concerned FSL. 
• A detailed note regarding examination of the clothing should be incorporated in the postmortem 

examination report by the doctor conducting the autopsy. 
• In case of alleged firearms deaths, the dead body should be subjected to radiological examination 

(X-rays/ CT-Scan) prior to autopsy 
Guidelines for Video-filming and photography 

• At the time of video-filming of the post-mortem examination the voice of the doctor conducting 
the post-mortem should be recorded. 

• The doctor should narrate his prima-facie observations while conducting the postmortem 
examination 

• A total of 20-25 colored photographs covering the whole body should be taken. Some photographs 
of the body should be taken without removing the clothes 

• Photographs should be taken after incorporating post -mortem number, date of examination and a 
scale for dimensions in the frame of photographs itself. 

• Camera should be held at right - angle to the object being photographed . 
• Video-filming and photography of the post -mortem examination should be done by a person 

trained in forensic photography and videography. 
• Good quality digital camera with lOX optical zoom & min 10 mega pixels should be used 

20. HOW WILL YOU PROCEED AUTOPSY IN A CASE OF COVID-19 OR SARS COV-2 INFECTED CASES AND HIV 
INFECTED BODIES? WRITE ABOUT ROUTINE VISCERA AND SPECIAL VISCERA . 

COVID-19: 

• The WHO on 30th January 2020 declared the outbreak of COVID-19 as a Public Health emergency of 
International concern 

• The Doctors, Mortuary Technicians and other Mortuary Staff in Mortuary performing Autopsy are 
exposed to potentially high risk fluid and secretions, even after taking the highest precautions. 

• Hence, No Invasive Autopsy Technique should be adopted for forensic Autopsy 

• The death in hospital and under medical care due to COVID-19 is a Non-MLC case and doesn' t require 
postmortem examination and the required certification of death is being done by treating doctors. 

• Some of the cases of suspected COVID-19 death which are brought dead to hospitals are labeled by 
emergency doctors as MLC and body is sent to the mortuary. 

• Some of the cases are suicide, homicide or accident and maybe COVID-19 positive or suspected case of 
COVID-19. 

• After the inquest procedure, if any crime is not suspect ed, police have the authority (174 CrPC) (Cl 194 
BNSS) to waive off conduction of medico-legal aulopsy, even though labeled as MLC. 

UNIVERSAL PRECAUTIONS FOR COVID-19, HIV/AIDS, HBV: 
• Doctors performing autopsy should follow universal precautions and wear PPE kit. 
• The protective suit should include - disposable plastic apron, double gloves (heavy autopsy glove and 

surgica l glove), head cap, knee length gum boots, goggles and N95 face masks.

ESSAYS: 

1. Define death. What are the stages of death ? Add a note on Brainstem death. 
,---, 
DEATH 

• Death is defined as the permanent disappearance of all signs of life at any time after live birth has taken 
place. 

• Death is a process but not an event. 
Stages of death 

• Somatic or Systemic or Clinical death 
, Complete & irreversible stoppage of circulation, respiration & brain functions (Bishop's tripod of life) 
, All tissue and cells of the body are alive and functioning except for those damaged in the CNS. 

• Cellular or Molecular death 
, Death of ce lls and tissues individually 

Different t issues die at different rate. 
, The brain is the first organ to die - wit hin 5 minutes. 
, Various organs survive for various periods of time: skin, kidneys - 4 hours; cornea, muscles - 6 hours 

(muscles respond to electrica l stimuli up to 6 hours) 
Atria mortis 

• Latin: Gateways of death, Death's portal of entry 
• Since death could enter only by stoppage of these organs (heart, lung and brain), each were called at rium 

mort is.

CRITERIA FOR DIAGNOSING BRIAN STEM DEATH: HARVARD CRITERIA 
The ventilator is switched off for 10 minutes and t he following responses are noted. If all t he responses are 
absent, the person is put on venti lator and should be examined again after 6 hours. 

i . Spontaneous respiration (Apnea test) 
• 100% person oxygen given via ventilator for 10 minutes and 5% CO2 in oxygen for 5 minutes (to 

prevent hypoxic brain damage) 
• Then, t he patient is disconnected from t he vent ilator . 
• Carbon-dioxide concent ration increases t o a point at which it w ill st imulate respirat ion, if any 

cells are still alive in the respirator centre of brainstem. 
• Befo re doing an apnea test, coma due to t he follow ing condit ions should be excluded . 

, Primary hypothermia 
,. Depressant drugs (barbiturates, narcotics etc.,) 
, Alcohol intoxication 
, Relaxants (neuromuscular blocking agents) 
, Severe metabolic or endocrine disturbances 
, Hypovolemic shock 

i i . Spontaneous circulation 
iii . Pupillary reflex 
iv. Corneal reflex 
v . Ocular reflex 

• Oculocephalic reflex - eye is fixed and does not move w hen t he posit ion of t he head is changed 
(doll's eye phenomenon) 

• Vestibule-ocular reflex (Caloric t est ) - no eye movements when each ear is irrigated with cold water 
vi. Grimace reflex 
vii . Gag reflex 

vii i. Flat EEG 

Board of medical experts to certify brainstem death (BSD) 
i. M edical Administrator in charge of the hospital. 
ii. Authorized specialist (nominated by t he M edical administrator) 

i ii. A Neurologist or Neurosurgeon (nominated by the M edical administrator) 
iv. Medical officer treating the patient w hose brain-stem death has occurred.

3. ~ YNCOPE:1 
• Syncope (fainting) is defined as a transient, self-limited loss of consciousness w ith an inability to 

maintain postural tone that is followed by spontaneous recovery 
• It is due to acute global impairment of cerebral blood flow 
• Typical prodromal (presyncope) sym ptoms include dizziness lightheadedness or faintness, weakness 

fatigue, and visual and auditory disturbances

AUTHORIZATION FOR ORGAN DONATION AFTER BRAIN DEATH 
• Any donor (> 18 years of age) may authorize the removal before his death 
• The person lawfully in possession of dead body can authorize the removal of any organ including 

eye/cornea of the deceased person. 

TRANSPLANT COORDINATORS 
• As per the THOTA 2014 rules, they have been made mandatory in all organ retrieval and transplanting 

centers. 

• Transplant coordinators are being t rained by NDO and MOHAN foundation, 
• In a deceased donor program, the transplant coordinators coordinate brain death declaration, organ 

retrieval, and donation, help in form filling, and even help with the last rites of the donor. 

7 . SUDDEN DEATH* 
• Sudden death is defined as the death of a person, who is not known to have suffering from any 

dangerous disease, injury of poisoning, who dies within 24 hours after the onset of symptoms 
• Characteristics 

,.. The suddenness of death 
,.. The unexpectedness of death 

• Sudden cardiac death (SCD) is an unexpected death due to cardiac causes that occurs in a short time 
per iod (generally within 1 hour of symptom onset) in a person with known or unknown cardiac disease 

Autopsy in sudden death c.ases 
• Sudden death of young individuals usually arouses suspicion and autopsy should be suggested 
• Thorough external examination of the body should be conducted 
• Meticulous internal examination of all systems is needed 
• Each organ has to be weighed and gross examination findings are noted 
• If a disease in an organ is suspected, the entire organ is preserved in 10% formalin for Histopathological 

examination 
• Viscera should also be preserved for chemical analysis 

CAUSES OF SUDDEN DEATH : 
Diseases of _!_he cardiovascular system j50%) 
M ajor causes of sudden death in adults 

• Coronary artery disease 
• Hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy 
• Dilated cardiomyopathy 
• Myocardial infarction 
• Rupture of a myocardial infarction 
• Valvular heart disease 
• Arrhythmias (Ventricular fibrillation, Ventricular tachycardia, Long QT syndrome) 
• Rupture of aortic aneurysm 
• Myoca rditis 

❖ The strongest known predictor of sudden card iac death is significant left ventricular dysfunction of any 
cause.

8 ,SUSPENDED ANIMATION "' .. 

• Also called apparent death 
• Vital functions of t he body (heartbeat and respiration) are reduced to minimum so that they are not 

detected by routine clinical methods 

• The person appears clinically dead, but he 1s not dead since the brainstem is functioning 

• The metabolic rate is reduced so much, t hat the oxygen requirement of the cells is sat isfied through the 
oxygen disso lved in body fluids 

• The person can be res uscitated 1n by cardiac massage and artificial respirat ion 

• MLI · death should not be declared immediately. Efforts should be made to resuscitate the person 

Suspended animation is seen in the following cases 

Accidental Iatrogenic _ l J..:.. i!Vol~ 

• Drowning • Drug overdose • Open heart • Yoga practitioners 

• Electrocution (barbiturates, narcotics) surgeries 

• Hypothermia • Concussion • After general 

• Sun stroke • Shock anesthesia 

• Newborn infants • Insanity 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

9. fANAPHYLACTIC -SHOCK 
• Anaphylaxis is a common medical emergency and a life-threatening acute hypersensitivity reaction 
• It can be defined as a rapidly evolving, generalized, multi-system allergic reaction. 
• Without treatment, anaphylaxis is ohen fatal due to its rapid progression to respiratory collapse. 

• Common causes: 
,. M edications: Penicillin, local anesthetics, NSAIDS, immunotherapy etc. 
,. Foods. 
, Insect st ings 
, Pollens 

• Anaphylaxis is typically an lgE-mediated (type 1) hypersensitivity reaction that involves the re lease of 
numerous chemical mediators from the degranulation of basophils and mast cells after re-exposure to a 
specific antigen. 

Clinical features 
• Respiratory symptoms (dyspnea, wheezing, stridor, hypoxemia, inability to maintain patency, persistent 

cough) 
• Hypotension (systolic less than 90 mm Hg or a decrease of greater than 30% from baseline) 
• Skin or mucosa! layer (rash, pruritus, erythema, hives, swelling of the face, lips, tongue, or uvula) 
• Kounis syndrome (allergic angina) : This is myocardial infarction or 1schemia that can occur in the setting 

of anaphylaxis. 
Treatment 

• Airway management 
• Decontamination 
• Epinephrine, i.m. (dose : 0.3 to 0.5 ml of 1:1,000 concentration of epinephrine) 
• Supportive treatment: IV fluids, bronchodilators, ant ihistamines, corticosteroids. 

PM findings 

\ • Injections marks or sting marks may be present. 
• The sting area should be excised and frozen and sent for antigen-antibody reactions. 
• Cyanosis, froth in the nostrils and mouth. 
• Laryngeal edema (recedes soon after death) edema of epiglottis, t rachea and bronchi, 
• Lungs: pulmonary edema, congestion, petechial hemorrhages, hyperinflation of alveoli with 

D emphysema. 

Medicolegal Aspects 

• The patient's relative may sue a doctor for medical negligence following Anaphylactic deaths after 
administration of drugs or radio-contrast media. 

• Test dose should be administered before giving antibiotics and other drugs commonly known to cause 
al lergy. 

• Even when the test dose is negative, anaphylactic reactions may occur after administration of therapeutic 
dose (Medical misadventure)

VIVA QUESTIONS: 

1. laishop' s tripod of lif~ 

• The Cardiovascular system, Respiratory system and the Central nervous system are called the Bishop' s 
tripod of life 

• Complet e cessation of functioning of these systems results in death (initially somatic death which is 
followed by cellular death) 

2. laeating heart donor (Living cadaver) 

• A persist ent vegetative state where cortex is dead, but brainstem is functioning 
• The vital cardio-respiratory functions are maintained by heart lung machines for organ transplantation 

purpose 

3. Section 46 IPC [Cl 2(5) BNS)j 
• S 46 IPC defines death. 
• Death denotes the death of a human being unless the contrary appears from the context . 

4. Cause of Death! 
• The cause of death is the disease or injury that produces the physiological disruption inside the body 

resulting in death 
• E.g. Gunshot wound to the chest 

5. [Mechanism of Deathl 
• The mechanism of death is the physiological derangement that results in t he death. 
• E.g. Mechanism of death due to the gunshot wound (previous question) is hemorrhage/blood loss 

6. [Manner of Deathl 
• M anner of death is the way in which the cause of death ca me about 
• Manner of death can be classified in 3 ways: 

:,. Natural : as a result of aging, illness, or disease 
,,. Unnatural 

• Accident 
• Suicide 
• Homicide 

,.. Undet ermined 

-
7. [!'ardie_u ~ots. 

• Tardieu spots refer to Petechial hemorrhages 
• These are caused by an acute rise in venous pressure which causes over-distent ion and rupture of thin­

walled venules, especially in lax ti ssue such as eyelid, visceral pleura, epicardium 
• Petechiae are also seen plethoric and obese individuals dying of natural deaths, acute right heart failure 

with markedly increased venous pressure, Cardiopulmonary resuscitation . 
• Not seen in drowning

9 . !Presumption of death*

• Explained under Section 108 IEA (Cl 111 BSA) 
• When there has been no proof of existence o f a person in society for 7 years, the law presumes him to be 

dead. 

10. ,Presumption of survivorship: 
• Explained under Section 107 IEA (Cl 110 BSA) 
• When it is shown that person was alive within thirty years, the burden of proving that he is dead is on the 

person who affirms he is dead. 
• It deals w it h legal rights following death of two persons where the evidence of the order of their deaths is 

unknown . 
• Assume a husband and wife die in a single vehicle motor vehicle accident. Their wills specify different 

beneficiaries. The time sequence and order of their deaths is critical to determine who will be the ultimate 
beneficiary but that ca nnot be factua lly determined. 

• The presumption of survivorship says the younger of the two is legally presumed to have survived. 
• Therefore t he younger person's will shall govern . 
• This presumption gives legal certainty in an uncertain factual situation .

iALGOR MORT_!_S ~P_O~TMOR_IEM COOLING 

• It is the cooling of the body after death 

• After death, heat production is lost (due to cessation of cellular respirat ion) and therefore the body 
starts cooling 

• Heat is lost by 
,- Radiat ion (transfer of heat to the surrounding air) 

,- Convect ion (transfer of heat t hrough moving air currents) 
,- Conduction (transfer of heat by direct contact with another object) 

Measuring the temperature 

• Ideal place to measure core t emperature of a dead body is the rectum (except• f d ) 
in cases o so omy 

• A thermometer with a range of Oto so·c and 25cm long is used 

Rate of cooling 
• Initially cooling is slower because of the residual metabolic activity in the ce lls . 

• After that cooling is faster 
• Towards the end, cooling again becomes slower as t he temperature gradient between the body and 

atmosphere is less 

• Thus, the curve of cooling is sigmoid shaped (Sigmoid curve of cooling or Marshal's curve) 



,ZPOST-MORTEM CHANGES & ARTIFACTS 

Rate of cooling is affected by 
Difference in temperature between the body and the environment : 

• Larger the difference, faster the cooling rate 
• In tropical countries like India, the atmospheric temperature is higher than the body temperature during 

summer. So, calculation of time since death using postmortem cooling is not possible 
surface area of the body: 

• Larger surface area: speeds up cooling rate. 
• Children : increasing surface area causes rapid heat loss. 

Body weight 

• Larger body weight (due to excess fat): slower cooling and vice versa 
Edema 

• Slower cooling rate. 
Clothing 

• Thick clothes slow down cooling rate 
Air circulation 

• Higher humidity and rapid air velocity: rapid cooling rate 
Water: 

• Rapid cooling rate 
• More rapid in flowing water than still water

• Hypostasis of Viscera : 
, Heart: mistaken for Ml 
, Lungs: mistaken for pneumonia 
, Intestine : mistaken for hemorrhagic infarction, volvulus 

PM hypostasis may not be evident in 
• Dark skinned persons 
• Severe anemia 
• Severe hemorrhage 
• Body kept in cold storage immediately after death 

Color of PM staining 
POISON/CONDITION POSTMORTEM STAINING 

Carbon monoxide Cherry-red 
Cyanide Bright red 
Phosphorus, Copper Dark brown 
Nitrites, aniline, chlorates (KCI03) Bromates, Chocolate or copper brown 
Potassium bicarbonate 
Refrigerated bodies, hypothermia Bright pink 
Opium, Mummified bodies · Black 
Hydrogen sulphide Bluish green 
Clostridium perfringens septicemia Bronze or greenish brown 

Medicolegal importance 
• It is a sign of death 
• Time since death can be estimated 
• Indicates the position of the body after death 
• PM staining at two different sites indicates change in the position of the body after death 
• Cause of death in poisoning cases can be determined 

3. a) Enumerate the changes that occur in the body after death. Briefly describe Rigor mortis** 
b) Classify postmortem changes. Describe the muscular changes in a dead body. 
c) Define death. Enumerate the immediate, early and delayed postmortem changes. Discuss the factors

Postmortem changes: Refer to Essay Q. No. 1 

Muscular changes after death 
• Primary flaccidity 
• Rigor mortis 
• Secondary flaccidity 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

PRIMARY FLACCIDITY 

• All muscles show lose tension and show complete relaxation as the control from the higher centers is lost 

• As long as ATP is present in the cel ls sutficiently 
, Muscles are relaxed (ATP permits Actin -Myosin splitting) 
, Muscles respond to electrical stimulus (supravital reaction) 

• pH-alkaline 

RIGOR MORTIS or CADAVERIC RIGIDITY 

• Rigor mortis is the stiffening and shortening of muscles after death 
• Atfects all muscles of the body, both skeletal and smooth 
• pH-acidic 

M echanism 
• Muscle mass is made of bundles of long fibers 
• Each long fiber contains Myofibrils which are the contractile 

elements §.~,,1~ 
• Myofibrils are made of two proteins - Actin and Myosin 
• Actin inter-digitates into myosin to form actomyosin to cause 2 

(A) sarcomere normal length Z 
muscular contraction 

• Separation of actin and myosin needs ATP 
• At the time of somatic death, the muscle contains enough 

ATP to be in relaxed state 
• Irreversible fusion of actin & myosi n results in rigor mortis 
• This will persist till actin and myosin undergo autolysis 

(B) sarcomere contracte
• d
  

When ATP level falls to 85% then the muscle becomes rigid 
\ • When the ATP level falls to 15% th en the rigidity is 

maximum 

Order of appearance 

• Rigor mortis develops in all muscles simultaneously 
• But it becomes most evident in the smaller mu scles early (less reserve glycogen) 
• Myocardium is the first muscle to become rigid (within 1 hour) 
• Externally it first appears in the eyelids . 
• Nysten's rule (Proximo-dista l progression) Eyelids ➔ face ➔ jaw ➔ neck ➔ upper l i mbs ➔ thorax ➔ 

abdomen ➔ lower limbs 
• Rigor mortis of erector pilae muscles cause goose flesh or cutis anserina 
• Passes off in the same order as it appeared 

' India Temperate Countries 
Time of onset 1-2 hours 3-6 hours 
Whole body involved 4-6 hours 8-12 hours 
Duration Summer 18 - 36 hours 2-3 days 

Winter 24-48 hours 



r-MORTEM CHANGES & ARTIFACTS 

Rule of 12 or Rule of Dozen or March of Rigor (applicable for temperate climate) 

• First 12 hours after death: Rigor mortis affects the whole body 
• Next 12 hours: retained 
• Next 12 hours: passes off 

Conditions affecting onset and duration of Rigor mortis 
• Age: 

Extremes of age ➔ rapid onset 
0 Does not occur in fetus < 7 months 

• Body condition 
Lean bodies, children and old people ➔ rapid onset, duration shorter 

0 Fat bodies, adults ➔ onset slow, duration longer 
• Environmental temperature: 

° Cold and wet ➔ onset slow, duration longer 
0 Hot and dry ➔ onset fast, duration shorter 

• Cause of death: 
0 Asphyxia, pneumonia, nervous disease with muscle paralysis & dehydration ➔ slow onset 
0 Septicemia & poisoning ➔ rapid onset, may even be absent 
0 Excessive physical work before death, violent deaths (electrocution, lightning) tetanus, strychnine 

poisoning, convulsions before death ➔ rapid onset 

Medicolegal importance 
• A Sign of death 
• Time since death can be determined 
• Indicates the position of the body at the time of death 

Conditions simulating Rigor mortis 
• Heat stiffening (Pugilistic attitude) 

• Cold stiffening 
• Gas stiffening (Putrefaction) 
• Chemical stiffening (Embalming) 
• Cadaveric spasm 

5 ECONDARY FLACCIDIT'f1 
• During putrefaction, the enzymes produced cause autolysis and putrefaction of the muscle protein 
• The muscle goes for flaccid state, and this is called secondary flaccidity 

4. a) Enumerate the changes that occur in the body after death. Add a note on Decomposition 
b) Define death. Describe postmortem changes that occur in a dead body 12 hours after death 
Postmortem changes: Refer to Essay Q. No. 1 

\DECOMPOSITION\lnvolves two processes 
• Autolysis 
• Putrefaction 



I VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

AUTOLYSIS 
• Self-digestion of tissues due to lysosomes and their digestive enzymes (Hydrolases) 
• Without bacterial action (aseptic process) 
• Earliest autolytic changes occur in parenchymatous and glandular tissues and in the brain. 
• The earliest external sign is a whitish, cloudy appearance in cornea . 

PUTREFACTION 
• Disintegration of tissues by bacterial action 
• The chief destructive agent of putrefaction is Clostridium welchii (Cl perfringens) 
• lecithinase is the most important enzyme causing hemolysis, liquefaction of clots, gas formation and 

disintegration of tissue. 
• Proteins are converted to amino acids, carbohydrates to CO2 and water and lipids to fatty acids 

Color changes 
• First external sign - greenish discoloration of skin over the region of caecum 
• Color change appears 18 hours in summer and 36 hours in winter 
• First internal sign - discoloration in the under surface of liver 
• Color change is due to conversion of Hb to sulfa methemoglobin 
• Marbling of skin 

,, Hydrogen sulphide produced by the bacteria 
,, Met hemoglobin combines with H2S to form Sulph-methemoglobin 
,. Superficial blood vessels show greenish discoloration due to Sulph-methemoglobin 
,, The pattern of discoloration resembles branches of a tree 
,. It starts at 24 hours; becomes prominent in 36-48 hours 
,, First seen on shoulders, arms, thighs and lateral aspects of trunk 

• With time, the whole body shows, greenish, greenish black and black discolorations 

\ 

MARBLING - on chest & shoulders 
Development of Foul smelling gases 

• Proteins and carbohydrates are reduced to simpler compounds like amino acids, Hydrogen sulphide, 
methane, carbon dioxide, carbon monoxi~e mercaptans etc. 

• These gases produce a nauseating and disagreeable ammoniacal odor 
• Postmortem Blisters 

,. Collection of gases between epidermis and dermis 
, First seen on the lower surface of trunk and thighs 
,,, Time 36-48 hours 



• fOST-MORTEM CHANGES & ARTIFACTS ___~--~--~--~-

• BloJtinu 
,, Most marked In the head and face 
,, Eyes bulge from the sockets 
,, Tongue prot rudes out, lips swollen and discolored 
,, Abdomen distended, scrotum and penis bulge 

• Postmortem delivery 
,, Due to pressure of gases, the fetus in the uterus may prolapse along with the placenta 
,.. Usually occurs in 2- 3 days 

• Po~tmortem purge 
,.. Due to gas formation In the abdomen, the diaphragm is pushed upwards and compresses the 

lungs and heart 
,, The decomposit ion fluid and froth in the lungs are forced t hrough the mouth and nostrils 

• Skin slippage: Sk in of both hands and feet wi ll be found detached - degloving 
• Gas stiffening - due to formation of gas in the joints and tissues in 2-3 days 
• Separation of skull sutures children 3-5 days 

Colliquatlve Putrefaction 
• Th is is due to liquefaction of tissues 
• All viscera become soft, pulpy and converted into black masses 
• This Is ca lled colliquative putrefaction and starts 5-10 days after death 

• Prostate and virgin uterus resist putrefaction for a long time 

Order of Putrefaction of internal organs 
1) Larynx & trachea 8) Kidneys, Urinary bladd~ -

2) Stomach, intestines 9) Esophagus, Pancreas 

3) Spleen 10) Blood vessels 
4) Liver (Foamy liver) 11) Gall bladder 

5) Brain 12) Prostate, non-gravid uterus 
6) Gravid Uterus J 13) Skin, muscle, tendon, 
7) Heart, Lungs 14) Bones

• By studying t he different stages of thei r life cycle, time since death can be determined 

Conditions affecting putrefaction 
• Temperature and putrefaction 

,. Putrefaction begins above 1o•c 
,, Optimum temperature between 21 •c and 38°C 
,, Arrested below o•c and above 48°C

• Cause of death 
RAPID PUTREFACTION · .. I DELAYED PUTREFACTION 

• Peritonitis • Anemia, Debility, Wasting 
• Sepsis • Severe hemorrhage 
• Asphyxia • Carbolic acid poisoning 
• Intestinal obstruction • Strychnine poisoning 
• Abortion • Heavy metal poisoning (Arsenic) 
• Clostridial infections • Zinc chloride poisoning 

• CASPER'S DICTUM 
,, The ratio of decomposition in air, water and deep burial (soil) is 1:2:8 
,, It means, decomposition is 2 times slower in water and 8 times slower in deep burial when compared 

to the decomposition in air. 
,, A dead putrefies within 1 week when left open in air; 2 weeks when left in water and 8 weeks when 

buried in soil.

6. a) What are the postmortem changes? What are the methods to estimate time since death? • 
b) Enumerate the various changes taking place in the dead body after death. Discuss in brief, how these 

changes help us to estimate the "time since death" 
c) What is postmortem interval? Discuss in detail the various factors that are helpful in determining the 

postmortem interval. 

Postmortem changes: Refer to Essay Q. No. 1 

r - -

~~MORTEM INTERVAU( Time since death or Time of death) 
• The interval between death and the time of postmortem examination is ca lled the postmortem interval. 
• Exact time of death cannot be fixed by any method. 
• Only an approximate range can be derived. 

• The longer the postmortem interval, wider is the range of estimate (less accurate estimate of t ime) 
Importance 

• To know when the crime was committed. 
• Ascertaining time since death is the starting point for enquiries. 
• Time of death may exclude some suspects. 
• To confirm or disprove an allegat ion. 

Factors helpful in estimating the time since death can be categorized as 
• Corporal evidence (present in the dead body) 
• Environmental evidence (present in the surroundings of the body) 
• Anamnestic evidence (based on the deceased's daily habits, activities, movements) 



POST-MORTEM CHANGES & ARTIFACTS 

FACTORS USED IN THE DETERMINATION OF POSTMORTEM INTERVAL 
a. Changes in the eye 

• Eyeball becomes flaccid (due to fall in IOP): 4-6 hours. 
• Tache noire: 3-6 hours. 

• Cornea becomes hazy and opaque: 6- 8 hours. 

b. Algor mortis (Postmortem cooling) 
Time since death = No~mal body temperature - Rectal temperature 

The rate of temperature fall per hour. 

c. Livor mortis {Postmortem hypostasis) 
• Mottled patches: 1 to 3 hours after death 
• Patches coalesce to form uniform area of staining: 4 to 6 hours. 

• Fixation of lividity: 8 -12 hours. 
• Merges with color of decomposition: 36 hours 

d . Rigor mortis 
• Heart muscles - 1 hour 

• Eyelids - 2 hours 

• Full body - 6 hours 
• Begins to pass off - 18 hours. 

• Completely passes off - 24 to 48 hours. 

e. Decomposition 
• Greenish discoloration of the skin over the region of caecum (due to sulph-methemoglobin formation) 

the first external sign of putrefaction - 18 to 24 hours 

• Gas in stomach and intestines cause abdomen to distend (24 - 36 hrs) 

• Postmortem blebs: 36 - 48 hrs 

• Marbling: 36 - 48 hrs 

• Degloving: 2 - 5 days 
• Postmortem purge fluid from mouth and nostrils: 48 - 72 hours 

• The reddish green color of the skin may become dark green and almost black in 3 - 4 days. 

• Bursting of the abdominal cavity by 3 - 5 days 

• Adipocere: about 3 weeks - 3 months 

• Mummification: about 3-6 months 

• Skeletonization : 1 year (in earth) 

f . Gastric emptying time 
• The amount of stomach contents and the extent of digestion may help in estimation of time si nce death I 

if the time at which the deceased took the last meal is known. 

• Light meal empties in 1-2 hrs; medium sized meal empties in 3-4 hrs; heavy meal empties in 5-8 hrs 

• Head injury, mental stress or shock may completely inhibit gastric juice secretion, motility of stomach and 

opening of pylorus and undigested food may be seen even after 24 hours 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

g. Bladder content 

• Urine collects in the bladder at the rate of 1 ml/min 

• Bladder full, body warm and flaccid - death within 1 or 2 hours 

• Bladder empty, body cold and stiff - died previous night 

h. Hair and nail 

• Hair does not grow after death. 
• Length of beard hair measured. 
• A rough estimation of time since last shave is made. 
• Beard hair grows at a rate of 0.4mm/day . 
• Nails grow at O.lmm/day. 

i . Blood 

• Level increases after death: Lactic acid, Amino acid nitrogen, Acid phosphatase, Amylase, Glutamate­
oxalate transaminase, Lactate dehydrogenase 

• Level decrease after death: Sodium 

j. Vitreous humor 
• K concentration at the time of death: 5 - 8 mmol/I 
• Rise in K concentration: 0.17 - 0.238 mmol/hr 
• Linear rise in hypoxanthine levels 
• Chloride levels decrease 

k. Entomology 

• Flies (M. domestica and M. vicinia) deposit their eggs in about 18 - 36 hours (sometimes as early as 6 _ 
10 hours) 

• 12 - 24 hours: larvae are produced from eggs 

• 3 - 6 days: larvae become pupae 

• 8 - 12 days: pupae become adult flies 
• Complete life cycle from eggs to adult flies may take S - 6 days in summer and 8 20 d · · 

- ays in winter. 

I. Circumstantial evidence 
• Dates of mails and newspapers 
• Dated sales receipts 

• When the neighbors last saw the person 
• State of food in the kitchen/table 
• State of dress

5. Differentiate postmortem hypostasis and bruise 

Hypostasis: It is the bluish-purple discoloration of skin on the dependent parts of the body caused due to 
accumulation of blood in the toneless capillaries 

Bruise: It is caused due to rupture of blood vessels and extravasation of blood due to the application of blunt 
force injury

Cause Accumulation of blood inside vessels of Blunt force injury - rupture of blood

CAOAVERlf SPASM Refer to n~,ct Que~t•on 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

s. 'cADAvrn1c SPASM* 

• Synonyms: Instantaneous rigor, Cataleptic rigidity, Death clutch 
• Rarely seen 

• Seen at the time and immediately after death (no primary flaccidity) 
• Small groups of muscle which were already in a contracted state immediately before death, remains 

contracted after death, without passing through the state of primary flaccidity 
• Hence it is an ante-mortem phenomenon 
• Usually associated with violent deaths coupled with emotional disturbance at the time of death 
• Mechanism not clearly understood 

Examples 
• Drowning: grass and weeds caught in the hands during st ruggle, will be seen clutched in the hand after 

death 
• Suicidal cutthroat/gun shot: the weapon will be firmly grasped in the hand 
• Electrocution: the cable wire may be seen f irmly gripped in the hand in elect rocution cases 
• Rarely entire body will be cadaveric spasm (e.g.: in a beheaded soldier) 

Medicolegal importance 
• If it is seen in case of drowning, it strongly indicates antemortem drowning 
• In cases of gunshot injuries or cu- throat injuries, if the weapon is firmly grasped in hands, it indicates 

the suicidal nature (manner of death)

POST-MORTEM CHANGES & ARTIFACTS 

11. ADIPOCERE *I 

• Synonyms: Saponification or Grave wax 

• It is a modification of putrefaction. 
• It is due to hydrolysis and hydrogenation of body fats. 
• Body fats are converted into fatty acids (oleic, palmitic and stearic acids) 
• Commonly seen in bodies immersed in water or in damp, warm environment. 

Factors facil itating adipocere formation : 
• Abundantbodyfat 
• High moisture 
• Stagnant air 
• Warm temperature 
• Bodies covered with thick cloth and placed in coffins. 
• Bodies lying in deep stagnant water. 
• Clostridium welchii produces Lecithinase that facilitates hydrolysis and hydrogenation of fat. 

Features of adipocere 

• Prominently seen over face, breasts, abdomen, buttocks or limbs where fat is abundant. 
• Rarely entire body is involved. 
• It takes at least 3 weeks for adipocere formation. 
• Fresh adipocere is soft, moist, whitish, greasy (rancid butter) 
• Smells like ammonia (rancid odor) 
• Burns w ith golden yellow flame. 
• Body floats in water 

Medicolegal importance 
• If face is involved, Facial features are preserved and helps in identification 
• Wounds are also preserved well and helps in determining cause of death 
• Approximate time since death can be estimated 

12. MUMMIFICATION/ 
• It is a modification of putrefaction 
• Dehydration/drying, desiccation and shriveling of the cadaver occurs due to complete evaporation of 

water from the body 
• When there is no water, bacteria cannot act and hence decomposition is arrested 

Factors facilitating mummification 
• Absence of moisture in the air. E.g. Deserts 

• Dry or warm air currents 
• Chronic arsenic and antimony poisoning favors mummification 

Features 
• It starts at the exposed parts and then involves the entire body 
• Skin is dry, brittle, dark and t ightly adherent to skeleton 
• If a mummified body is not protected, it will become powdery and disintegrate 

• If protected, the body can be saved for many years 
• Mummification requires 3 - 6 months 
• A mummified body is odorless

Medicolegal importance 
• Body features are preserved and helps in identificat ion 
• Features of t he wounds are preserved 
• Face is distorted due to shrinkage of soft tissues 
• Approximate time since death can be determined 

13. \ARTEFACTsl 
.__•  Artefact is any change caused or feature ·in troduce d ·m a bo d Y a fte r death , which is likely to lead to 

misinterpretation of medico legally significant (antemortem) findings 
~rtefacts introduced after death and before autopSYj 
Agonal Artefacts 

• Regurgitation of gastric contents 

• Esophago-gastromalacia - due to auto digestion, stomach contents are spilled into thoracic cavity or 
peritoneal cavity; seen in persons dying of head injury 

Resuscitation Artefacts 
• Defibrillator application - ring like contusion in the chest 
• Cardiac massage - rib fractures, especially 3 - 7 ribs on the left side; contusion of the anterior chest wall 
• Central line - introduction of IV cannula leads to extravasation of blood in the neck tissues 
• Positive pressure ventilation - acute emphysema, subpleural blebs, tension pneumothorax 
• Laryngoscope - may injure lips, palate, tongue and teeth 

Artefacts due to handling of the body 
• Undertaker's fracture - subluxat ion of lower cervical spine due to tearing of intervertebral disc at C6 - C7 
• Postmortem abrasions - over bony prominences if the body is dragged 

Artefacts related to postmortem changes 
• Breaking of rigor mortis - rough handling breaks rigor mortis, resulting erro rs in est imating t ime since 

death 

• Rigor mortis of heart - simulates concentric hypertrophy of heart 
• Postmortem staining - may simulate bruises 
• Postmortem staining - cherry red color in CO poisoning, Brick red in HCN poisoning, dark brown in 

phosphorous poisoning 

• A groove with a pale floor- may be seen in the neck during decomposition, which simulat es a ligature 
mark (pseudo-strangulation), in fat and short necked persons 

• Bursting of abdomen and separation of skull sutures (in ch ildren) in advanced putrefaction should not 
be mistaken for injuries 

• Small round holes caused by maggots may simulate bullet ent ry wound 
Artefacts due to burns 

• Heat ruptures - may resemble lacerated or incised wounds 
• Heat hematoma - may simulate extradura l hemorrhage 

/Artefacts introduced during autops~ 

Skull fractures - due to partial sawing or using hammer and chise l and forcefu l pull of skull vau lt, fractures may 
be seen on the base of skull, especially middle cranial fossa 
Visceral damage - rough handling causes tears in brain, liver, lungs 
Hyoid bone fracture - when the tongue and neck structures are firmly grasped and pu lled

6. !Foamy liverl 
• Occurs in putrefaction, about 3-5 days after death 
• Due t o the action of Cl. perfringens and its enzymes and gas formation, bubbles develop in liver 

parenchyma 
• The liver is described as foamy liver with the appearance of honey-comb or Swiss-cheese 

7. Postmortem purge: Refer to Essay Q No. 4 

8. Rasper's dictum•! 
• The rate of putrefaction varies when it is placed in an open place, water or buried 
• Casper's dictum st at es that a body decomposes in air twice rapidly as in water and eight times as 

rapidly as in earth 

9. Marbling 
Refer to Essay Q No . 4 

10. Adipocere 
Refer to Short Note Q No. 11 

11. \Embalming•J 
• Embalming is temporarily preservation of human body (sometimes long-term) to delay decomposition 

and to m ake them suitable for public display at a funeral or transport to a dist ant place 

• The three goals of embalming are sanitization, presentation and preservation (or restoration) 

• Embalming fluid (contains forma lin, sodium borate and citrate, glycerin, sodium chloride, wintergreen 
water) is injected into the vascular system for preservation 

• It produces a chemical stiffening, similar to rigor mortis 

• Normal rigor mortis will not develop after embalming

~ MECHANICAL INJURIES 

~RASION~ 

• An abrasion is a wound caused by superficial damage to the skin or mucous membrane, not deeper than 
the epidermis 

• Caused by blunt force injury; except - scratch abrasion which is caused by a pointed object 

• They are simple injuries 

• Bleed slightly Tangential force 
• Heal rapidly without scar formation 

ABRASIONS - types 

• Scratch abrasion 
• Graze abrasion Damage to epithelium 

• Pressure abrasions ABRASION 
• Impri nt abrasions 

❖ Tangential force produces Scratch and Graze abrasions 

❖ Perpendicular force produces Pressure and Imprint abrasions 

Scratch abrasion 

• Synonym: linear abrasion 
• Caused by Sharp or pointed object not sharp enough to cause incised wound. 

• E.g. thorn, fingernails, tip of knife, pin 
• Remove a port ion of skin surface while passing across the skin and heaping up of denuded epidermis at 

one end 
• Clean area at the start of the wound and tags at the end 
• Helps in determining the direction of force from the presence of tags or heap of cells at one end 

Graze ~rasio~ 
• Synonyms: grinding or sliding abrasions 

• Most common type 
• Occurs when there is skin movement against some rough surface 

• Uneven, longitudinal parallel lines of abrasion are seen 
• Epithelium heaped up at the end of these lines which indicates the direction of force 

• Commonly seen in road traffic accidents 
• Friction burn (rug burn, rope burn, road rash, scuff abrasion) - a form of abrasion + heat burn 

";; It is caused by the friction or skin rubbing against a surface like road 

► Commonly seen in road traffic accidents 

Pressure--;b~ 

• Synonym: crushing abrasion 
• Sustained pressure of a rough object perpendicular to the skin, causing crushing of the superficial layers 

of the skin (Small force applied for a long time) 
• There is no force on impact, but sustained pressure after the object comes in contact with body surface 

• E.g. Ligature mark in hanging and strangulation, t eeth-bit e marks

p_ECHANICAL INJURIES 

2. classify mechanical injuries. Add a note on Contusions 

Classification of mechanical injuries: Refer to Essay Q No. 1 

~TUSIONj 
• Contusion or Bruise is produced by extravasation of blood into tissues duP to rupture of blood vessels 

caused by blunt trauma 

• Usually no damage to the skin 

• Contusion with abrasion is cal led abraded contusion 

• Can also be seen in internal organs - lung, heart, and brain are frequently affected 
• Rupture of capi llaries - petechiae 

• Larger and confluence of petechiae - ecchymoses 

• When a large blood vessel is injured - hematoma 

CONTUSIONS- types 
• Superficial bruise Intact skin 

• Deep bruise 

• Patterned bruise _-.-•-d--+---- Bruise due to rupture of 
• Ectopic bruise blood vessels in the 

subcutaneous plane 

SUPERFICIAL BRUISE 
• Subcutaneous or intradermal bruise 

• Visible immediately or soon after injury 
CONTUSION/BRUISE 

DEEP BRUISE 
• Delayed or Come-out bruise 
• Manifest externally only after severa l hours or days 
• Sometimes injury is produced before death and the bru ise is evident after death 

ECTOPIC BRUISE 
• Migratory or Percolated contusions 
• lnJury at one site, contusion at a remote site due to gravitation of blood 

,.. Fracture of anterior cranial fossa - bru ising around eye (Black eye) 
,.. Fracture of posterior cranial fossa - bruising over mastoid (Battle's sign ) 

,.. Fracture of Jaw - bruising in the neck 
,.. Fracture of pelvis - bru ising over th igh 
,.. Extra-peritoneal bleeding, ruptured ectopic pregnancy, Acute pancreatitis - bruising around umbilicus 

(Cullen's sign) 
,.. Retroperitoneal hemorrhage - bruising around flank (Grey Turner's sign ) 

PATTERNED BRUISE 
• Hammer - round bruise 
• Rod, st ick or wh ip - t ram line or railway-line bruise 
• Bruise produced by a cycle cha in

Factors modifying the size and shape of bruise 
• Age: Children and old people bruise more easily due t o delicate vessels 
• Sex: Women bruise more easily than m en 
• Condition of tissue: good muscle tone - less bruising 
• Type of tissue : 

► Vascular, loose tissue (e.g. face scrotum, vulva): more bruising as m ore blood ca n accumulate in 
loose t issue spaces 

► Firm, supported tissue (e.g. scalp, buttocks, palms, soles) - less bruising 
• Associated diseases: Bruise occurs readily in persons with diseases like arteriosclerosis, leukemia, 

hemophilia, scurvy, vitamin K deficiency etc. 

Medicolegal importance 
• Patterned bruise: help in the ident ification of w eapon 
• The age of the injury can be determined from the color changes.

3, Classify mechanical injuries. Add a note on Lacerations and their medicolegal importance** 

Classification of mechanica l injuries: Refer to Essay Q No. 1 

LACERATIONSi 
L -

• Lacerations are rupture or tears of skin and/or deeper tissues 

• Caused by a blunt force injury 

• It is an irregular, three-dimensional open wound 

Characteristic features 

• Margins - irregular, uneven, ragged with torn edges 

• Shelving - one margin overrides the other which indicates the direction of force 

• Bruising of the margins and underlying tissues seen 

• Pieces of tissues attached in between t he edges - tissue bridges 

• Hair bulbs and hair are crushed 

• Depth - varies w it h force and may contain foreign matter like dust, sand, paint of the vehicle involved 

etc. 
• Swallow tails - tearing at angles of lacerations at one end 

• Leaves a permanent scar on healing DEEP LACERATION 

LACERATIONS- types 
Skin - -

• Tears 

• Split lacerations Fat ---
• St retch lacerations ~ 
• Avulsed lacerations rvtusck 
• Cut lacerations 

TEARS 
• M ost common type of laceration 
• Caused by blunt force over skin having sufficient underlying fat and muscle 

• Caused by a heavy blunt object (lathi, iron rod) 

SPLIT LACERATION 
• Caused w hen blunt force is applied to skin directly overlying a bone with scanty subcutaneous tissues 

• Skin gets compressed between two hard blunt surfaces (weapon and bone) and splits 

• Edges appear to be clean cut (incised looking lacerations) 
• Commonly seen over bony prominences - scalp, lower jaw, iliac crest, shin etc . 

STRETCH LACERATION 
• When the skin gets stretched beyond its limits of elasticity, stret ch lacerat ion occurs 

• Caused by projecting parts of vehicle such as handle when the skin is stret ched 

• E.g. Fractured segment of a bone pierces t he skin and projects out

MECHANICAL INJURIES \ 
Medico-legal importance of tailing of wounds 

• Last part of incised wound 

• Indicates use of light sharp cutting weapon 
• Direction of force 

• Relative position of victim and assailant 

❖ Tailing of wound: incised wound 
❖ Fish tailing: stab wound 
❖ Swallow tai l: lacerations 
•:• Tail gating: road traffic accidents 
❖ Tail splashing: firearm injury 

Beveled cuts 

• Produced by an oblique strike of a sharp-edged weapon 
• One edge of the wound overhangs the other 
• Relative position of the assailant and the victim can be 

determined 

• Usually homicidal 

HESITATION/TENTATIVE/TRIAL/EXPLORATORY CUTS 
• Multiple, Parallel, Superficial cuts on either side of the main wound 
• Seen on the accessible and vital sites - neck, wrist, ankle, chest, abdomen 

• Usually in seen in suicidal cuts 
• Seen because the hesitant, scared person tries to test how much pa inful are the wounds 

• Seen at the initial part of the deep fatal wound) 
• Usually caused by a light sharp cutting weapon 
• A person committing suicide will expose the part and there will not be any cuts on the clothes 

Medicolegal importance of Incised wounds 

• Indicat es the nature of weapon used 
• Age of the injury can be determined 
• Position and character of injuries indicated the manner of inflict ion - suicide, homicide or accidental 
• Suicidal - multiple, parallel, superficial cuts on the accessible vital parts of the body 
• Homicidal - multiple, different directions, anywhere on the body, varying depth. 

• Cut injuries over face, nose, ears, genitals are usually homicidal 

• Cut injuries of nose, ear lobes - Grievous hurt (permanent disfiguration of face)

~ MECHANICAL INJURIES 

b) FEATURES HELPFUL TO DETERMINE NATURE OF INFLICTING WEAPONS 
Type of Force 

• Abrasions, Contusions, Lacerations and Fractures - Blunt weapons 

• Incised wounds, Stab wounds - Sharp weapons 
Dimensions 

• Two dimensional - Abrasions 

• Three dimensional - Contusions, lacerations, incised wounds and stab wounds 
Margins 

• Regular, clean-cut, well-defined margins - sharp weapon 

• Irregular, uneven and ragged margins - blunt weapon 
Shape 

• Spindle shape - Incised wound, Stab wound with double edged weapon. 

• Wedge, tear drop or triangular shape - stab wound with Single edged weapon 

Patterned injuries- pattern of the infl icting weapon seen on skin surface. 

• Patterned abrasion: Ligature mark in hanging and strangulation. 

• Patterned bruise: Tramline bruise caused by cylindrical rod . 

Other features 

• Fish tailing - stab wound with Single edged w eapon 

• Tissue bridges, Crushed hair bulbs, Swallow tails - blunt weapon 

• Avulsion - vehicle run-over. 

• Tai ling of wound - light sharp cutting weapon 

• Wide gaping wounds with contused margins - heavy sharp weapon

6. Classify mechanical injuries. Add a note on Stab wounds* 

Classification of mechanical injuries: Refer to Essay Q No. 1 

STAB WOUNDS 

• Also known as puncture wounds 
• Produced when a pointed weapon or object or instrument is forcibly thrust into the body 

• Penetrating wounds: the weapon causing punctured wound enters a body cavity 

• Perforating wounds: the weapon causing punctured wound enters a body cavity from one side and 

comes out fo rm other side (an entry wound and exit wound) 

FEATURES OF A STAB WOUND Puncture Wound 
Length 

• May be same as the breadth of the blade of weapon or [!] \ ·--. ··- )-Skin 
• Slightly smaller because of elasticity of skin causing ~ Fat 

shrinkage or ..,-- Muscle 

• Larger because of the lateral movement of the victim or 

assailant

Width 

• Width may not approximate with each other because of gaping 
• When the stab wound runs parallel to the cleavage lines, the shape will be produced accord ing to the 

type of weapon. 
• When it cut across these lines transversely or obliquely there will be gaping. 

Depth - greatest among the 3 dimensions 
• No relationship with the length of the weapon 
• Wound should not be probed to f ind the length in a living person as it may dislodge a blood clot and 

result in fatal hemorrhage. 
Margins 

• Regular and clean cut 
• Hilt mark - an abrasion caused the hilt of the weapon

• Du ring withdrawal of weapo n, the blunt end may show small splits on both 

sides - fish tailing 
• Double edged weapon - spindle shaped

J. classify mechanical injuries. Describe features of a chop wound. Distinguish between chop wound and 

lacerated wound. 

Classification of mechanical injuries: Refer to Essay Q No. 1 

fuoP WO~~D~ 
• Slash wounds 

• Caused by a relatively sharp and fairly-heavy weapon like axe, hatchet, chopper, sword etc. 

• Because of t he larger amount of force, chop wounds have features of both sharp and blunt force injuries 

• Abrasions and bruising seen at the margins and underlying tissues 

• Deep gaping wounds with destruction of underlying organs 

• Chop wounds over bone may have a groove or cut in the underlying bone 

• A chop wound over the head and neck is almost always fatal. 

• Chop wound over the extremities may cause complete or incomplete amputation of the fingers, or joints . 

Feature CHOP WOUNDS I LACERATED WOUNDS

Medico-legal importance 

• Usually seen on the exposed parts of the body like, head, face, shoulders and extrem ities 

• Relative position of the victim and assailant can be determined in beveled wounds 

• Type of the weapon - suggests the use of a heavy sharp cutting weapon 

• Manner - Almost always homicidal 

• Rarely accidental - propeller fans of ships and planes can cause chop wounds

15_§ RICATED WOUNDS* or SELF-INFLICTED ~OU~DS*j 

Fabricated, fictitious, forged or invented wounds are those which may be 

• Produced by a person on his own body (self-inflicted), or 

• Occasionally, caused by another person with his consent (self-suffered) 

Motive 

• To bring false charges against enemies 

• To pretend self-defense 

• To claim more compensation or draw attention and sympathy 

• To make simple injuries appear serious to claim more 

• To avoid hard duties (by police and soldiers) 

• By women to bring a rape charge against enemy 

• By prisoners, to bring a charge of beating or torture against officers 

• Feigned robbery by policemen, servants and watchmen acting in collusion with robbers to show that they 

were defending the property 

Characteristics 

• Usually caused by a light sharp weapon 

• Usually superficial wound - abrasions or incised wounds 

• Contusions and lacerations are rare 

• Wound are seen over accessible parts 

• Clot hes are spared 

• Tears in clothes do not correspond to those in the body. 

• Age of wound does not coincide with the history given by the person. 

• Old, linear scars suggest previous attempts 

16. :DEFENCE WOUNDaj 
• A defense wound is an injury received by the victim of an attack while trying to defend himself against 

the assailant 

• They are due to an instant response of the victim to defend the attack 

• Types of defence wounds 

► Active defence w ounds 

■ The victim actively grasps the weapon to prevent the attack. 

■ Wounds are seen over the palms, pal mar aspect of fi ngers, web-space between t he 

thumb and t he index finger 

► Passive defence wounds 

■ The victim raises his hands to prevent the attack 

■ Wounds are seen on the ulnar aspect of hand, forearms, interdigital areas 

■ Seen over the upper back when the victim tries to run away 

• Defence wounds may also be present on the feet and legs where a victim attempts defense while lying 

down and kicking out at the assailant 

• Defence wounds may be incised, stab, lacerations, contusions or abrasions depending on the type of 

weapon used 

• The presence of defense wounds is highly suggestive of homicide, attack from front and the victim was 

conscious at the time of attack

1. Classify mechanical injuries. Describe various types of skull fractures• 

Classification of mechanical injuries: Refer to Chapter 8, Essay Q No. 1 

~KULL F~ACTURES
1 

Fractures may be caused by 

• Local deformation: When the skull receives a foca l impact 

• General deformation: when the skull is compressed between 2 external objects 

~YPES OF SKULL FRACTUR~sj 

Fractures of vault of skull . Fractures of base of skull 

• Fissured fracture • Fracture of the anterior cranial fossa 

• Depressed fracture • Fracture of the middle cranial fossa 

• Comminuted fracture • Fracture of the posterior cranial fossa 

• Diastatic fracture • Ring fracture 

• Pond fracture • Hinge fracture / Transverse fracture/ 

• Perforating fracture Motorcyclist's fracture 

• Gutter fracture • Longitudinal fracture 

• Cut fracture 

FRACTURES OF VAULT OF SKULL 

Fissured fract ure 
• Synonyms: Linear, hair-line or crack fracture 

• Due to general deformation of the skull 

• Most common type 
• They are like ly to be caused by forcible contact with a 

broad hard surface or 
• Blows with an object with a relatively broad st riking 

surface. 

Depressed fracture 
• Syn: Fracture-ala-signature or Signature fracture or Mirror image fracture 

• Produced by local deformation of skull . 
• The fract ured part is driven inward into the skull cavity. 

• Caused by heavy weapons with a small striking surface. e.g. Hammer, stone 

• Their pattern often resembles the weapon or agent which caused it

Com I" J ed fracture 

• Syn : Cru~hrd or 'tcl"'ltr o r ,. , r 1r fr c c 

• Bone is broken into several pieces of different sizes 

• Usua lly a complication of depressed fracture 

• Causes. Fa ll from height , blow to the skull by a heavy weapon w ith broad striking surface

D1astdtic fra cures 

• Syn: Suture ' m f c ctl.. s 

• Fractures along skull sutures 

• Usually seen in children and young adults (sutures are not fused) 

• Usually along the sagittal suture 

_eond fr cture 

• Syn: Indented or Ping-pong fracture 

• Simple dent of the skull 

• Inner table is intact, outer table may show cracks around the dent 

• Seen in the skull o f infants 

• E.g. Obstetric forceps blade causing dent on the skull following a forceps delivery 

• Usually there are no internal injuries 

• Looks like a dent in the ping-pong ball 

Diastatic fracture Pond fracture 



~ INJURIES \ 
Penetrating and Perforating fractures 

• Caused by firearms or sharp pointed weapons 

• The weapon passes t hrough both tables of the skull 

• Srze and shape of the defect corresponds to thE cross section of the weapon 

• Fractves vVith entry and exrt wound - perforating fracture 

• Entry wound: outer table - punched out ; inner table - beveling 

• Exit wound: inner table - punched out; outer table - beveling 

Gutter fracture 

• Partial thickness of the outer table of skul l is removed by a tangential force to form a gutter 

• Can be caused by a bul et str king obliquely and not penetrating the skull 

• Usually accompanied by irregular, depressed fracture of inner table of the skull 

Cut fracture: Caused by re latively heavy sharp cutting weapons like chopper 

FRACTURES OF BASE OF SKUU 

Fractures of anterior cranial fossa 

• Caused by direct impact or contre-coup injury 

• lnJury to roof of orbit - Black eye or raccoon eye or panda's eye (a type of ectopic bruising) 

• If both eyes are involved - Spectacle hematoma 

• Escape of CSF and blood through nose 

Fractures of middle cranial fossa 
• Direct impact behind the ears or crush injuries of the head 

• Escape of CSF and blood through the ear when petrous part of tempora l bone is fractured 

Fractures of posterior cranial fossa 
• Bruising behind the ears and over mastoid process is seen - Battle sign 

Ring or Annular fracture 
• Fissured fractures around foramen magnum 

• Seen in fall from height when the oerson lands on feet or buttocks or heavy blow on the top of the head

2 . Classify mechanical injuries. Add a note on Coup and Cantre-coup injuries. 

Classification of mechanical injuries: Refer to Chapter 8, Essay Q No. 1 

COUP & CONTRECOUP INJURY: Though the mechanism of product ion of these injuries is not completely 
understood, it can be explained by 

• Shear (gliding) strain - produced by linear motion of the head. 
• Angular rotation - rotational force 
• Suctional force - produced by vacuum creation 

1COUPINJU~ 
• Injury to the skull and brain occurs at the site of impact 
• Injury may occur both with static and moving head 
• An impact on the stationary head causes the skull to move immediately in t he direction of force, but the 

brain lags behind for a brief period 
• The base and tip of parts of brain impinge on the rough surfaces of base of skull like roof of orbit and 

petrous apex 
• The skull hits the brain at the site of impact - resulting in coup injury 
• This type of coup injury is an acceleration injury 

CONTRE-COUP INJU_ifrj 
• Injury to the skull and bra in occurs on the side opposite to the site of impact 
• Do not occur when the head is fixed 
• When a moving head hits a stationary object, skull is suddenly brought to a stop by the impact 
• But the brain, which continues to move forward for a brief period, impinges against the skul l, resulting in 

contre-coup injury 
• Th is type of contre-coup injury is a deceleration injury 

Feature Coup injury Cantre-coup injury 
Site At t he site of impact Opposite to t he site of impact 
Mechanism Due to direct impact Due to vacuum creation and suction force 
External injury Contusion/Laceration of No external injuries 

scalp; skull fractures 
Severity ~ild Severe 
Occurs when Head is f ixed Head is moving 
Blow to the head More severe Less severe 
Common site Occipita l lobe Frontal lobe

4. A young man of 25 years allegedly assaulted by some persons was brought to the emergency in unconscious 

state. On examination, there is bleeding scalp wound on the right side. Just during CT scan, within half an 

hour, the man regained consciousness and wanted to tell something. 

a) How will you proceed to record the dying declaration? 

b} Duties of emergency medical officer in the management of this case 

c) In case of death, what are the probable autopsy findings determining the cause of death? 

a} General rules to record the dying declaration: Refer to Chapter 1; Essay Q No. 4 

b) Duties of emergency medical officer in the management of this case: 

• Complete Head to toe examination and assessment 

• Assess the prognosis using the Glasgow coma scale, 

• Stabilization of the patient 

• Maintenance of ABC - Airway, Breathing, Ci rcu lation 

• Any bleeding wounds are dressed. 

• Patient sent for CT scan . 

• Neurosurgery consultation should be arranged. 

c) Autopsy findings: History and clinical findings suggest Epidural (Extradural} Hemorrhage (EDH) 

• Lacerated wound on the right temporal/parietal region of the scalp 

• Fissure fracture of the temporal bone 

• Extradural hematoma - biconvex lens shaped hematoma on removing the vault of the skull . 

• Associated sub-arachnoid hemorrhage may be seen. 

• Contusions and lacerations of parts of cerebrum 

• Diffuse Cerebral edema. 

• Herniation of brain - usually Subfa lcine herniation 

• Kernohan's notch - in uncal herniation, brainstem is pushed against the contral ateral tentorium, 

resulting in the compression of cerebral peduncle. 

• Duret hemorrhages - tear drop hemorrhages in the brain stem

5. Two final year MBBS students were riding a bike after a party met with an accident on the rood. The rider fell 
on the road with his head striking the divider of the road. He sustained abrasions on his left upper and lower 
limbs. The rider was unconscious for a few seconds and then recovered. Then he was taken to a nursing home 
by the pillion rider. The rider complained of pain in the left temporo-parietal area. The doctor examined him 
and admitted him to the hospital. CT scan showed opacity in the left temporoparietal region. The next day, 
the boy became unconscious and died within an hour. 
a) What could be the most probable injury that caused death of the rider? 
b) Describe the mechanism and postmortem findings of the injury that caused the death. 
c) Explain various types of skull fractures with relevant diagrams. 

a) EXTRADURAL HEMATOMA: 
• The most probable injury that caused death is Extradural hematoma. 
• The clinica l features are typica l of lucid interval due to Extradural hematoma. 
• Init ially, the concussive force that caused the head injury results in an altered level of consciousness. 
• After some time, the person regains normal consciousness. 
• The EDH continues to expand until the mass effect of the hemorrhage, results in increased intracranial 

pressure and aga in decreased level of consciousness. 
• The period of normal consciousness between the periods of altered consc iousness is called lucid interval. 

b) MECHANISM OF THE INJURY: 
• Injury to the skul l causes rupture of blood vessels. 
• Blood starts collecting in the extradural space. 
• More blood collection ➔ dura mater gets stripped further and further from the skul l ➔ more damage to 

blood vessels ➔ more bleeding. 
• EDH develops slowly over several hours. 

POSTMORTEM FINDINGS IN EDH: Refer to Essay Q No. 4 

c) SKU LL FRACTURES: Refer to Essay Q No. 1

REGIONAL INJURIES 

• Source of bleeding: usually venous bleeding - bridging veins (a vein that connects the cortical surface of 
the brain to a dural sinus) 

• Subdural hematoma is the most common type of traumatic intracranial mass lesion. 
• Most common intracranial hemorrhage in boxers 
• Acute subdural hematomas occur most commonly over the cerebral convexity in the parietal region 
• Lucid interval is seen in one-third cases 
• Death may occur if the bleeding exceeds SO ml 
• CT scan 

;... SDH is not limited by sutures 
',, SOH shows concavity towards brain (EDH - convexity towards brain) 
Y Acute SOH (< 72 hours old) - hyperdense, crescent-shaped mass (concavo-convex mass) 
', Subacute SDH (3 days to 3rd weeks) - isodense 
-,. Chronic SDH (after 3 weeks) - hypodense

7. A 30 years old male is brought to the trauma center with history of head injury. He does not remember the 
incident. After a couple of hours he loses consciousness, and his condition progressively deteriorates, and he 
dies. Answer the following : 
a) What is the likely diagnosis? 
b) Mention the source of bleeding? 
c) What is the medico legal importance of this condition? 
d) What is the emergency procedure that needs to be done? 

a) Diagnosis: Extradural/epidural hemorrhage 
• Brought with the h/o head injury 
• The person is in a state of post-traumatic amnesia due to concussion (Traumatic brain injury) 

b) Source of bleeding: Refer to Essay Q No. 3 
c) Medicolegal importance: Lucid interval: Refer to Short note Q No. 2 
d) Emergency procedure that needs to be done: 

• Epidural hematoma is a surgical emergency. Any delay in surgery results in herniation of brain and 
permanent brain damage as the hematoma grows in size 

• craniotomy or Burr hole evacuation and drainage should be performed to evacuate the hematoma 
• If the patient diagnosed with EDH is unconscious and cannot give consent for surgery, consent of spouse, 

legal heir or relatives can be obtained. 
• If spouse, legal heir or relatives are not available, or even without consent, the doctor can perform 

surgery in good faith to save his live (Doctrine of Emergency- Section 92 IPC) (Cl 30 BNS)

PEDESTRIAN FALLING ON THE GROUND AFTER BEING HIT BY CAR 

PRIMARY IMPACT INJURIES 

• Due to the fi rst impact between the vehicle and the pedestrian 

• The injuries depend on the position of the victim (whether he was crossing the road, or he was hit frof11 
back or front) 

• If a person is struck from behind, it leads to spiral fracture or wedge-shaped fracture of the bone below 
the point of impact - bumper fracture 

• In adults - usually seen on the legs (# tibia - bumper#) 



JR EGIONAL INJURIES 

• In chi ldren - usually seen in head or thighs 

(# femur - bumper#) 

• Impact on the hips may cause fracture of 

the vertebra and pelvis 

• Patterned abras on or bruising produced 

by bumper, rad iator grill , head lights etc. Bumper Bumper 
may be present which may help in I 

identifying the offending veh icle 1111 Ill - Wedge shaped 
fracture 

SECONDARY IMPACT INJURIES 

• After the primary impact, the victim is 

scooped up and thrown over the bonnet, 
windshield or roof of the car (if primary 

impact was below the centre of gravity and 

if it is a moderate to high veloc ity impact) 

• Head injuries are common. 

• Fracture of ribs, injuries to shoulders, back of chest are also injured . 

• If a person is hit from the front, secondary impact injuries are seen on chest and abdomen. 

• Rupture of aorta, liver or spleen may be seen. 

• Seen on the same side as the primary impact injury. 

• Secondary impact injuries are rarely seen in children, as their centre of gravity is at a lower level. 

SECONDARY INJURIES OR TERTIARY IMPACT INJURIES 

• Injuries caused when the victim falls on ground or any other object after thrown by the vehicle 

• Graze abrasions - as the person is dragged on the road 

• Skull fracture -as the head strikes the ground 

• Coup and centre-coup injuries in the brain 

• Found on the parts opposite to the prim ry impact 

Waddell's triad 
• The classical pattern of injuries seen in pedestrian children in road traffic accidents 

• The injuries include - fracture of shaft of femur, chest or abdominal injuries and contralateral head injuries 

RUN OVER INJURIES 

• The person may be run over by the same of some other vehicle and the following injuries may be caused 

,.. Flaying or avulsion injuri es 

,.. Tyre-tread marks 
, Multiple rib-fractures resulting in flail chest 

,.. Crushed head, limb

VEHICULAR OCCUPANTS' INJURIES 
INJURIES TO THE UNRESTRAINED DRIVER 

• Steering wheel impact injury or Horn boss fracture 
r Transverse fracture of the sternum, fracture of ribs 
,. Contusion and rupture of heart 
,. Rupture of aorta 
,. Laceration of liver, spleen and kidneys 

• Head injuries - due to striking against windshield 
• Sparrow foot marks or Dicing injuries - Multiple punctate lacerations or superficial cuts of the face due 

to shattering of the windscreen glass into multiple small cubical fragments w ith relatively blunt edges -
seen in front seat occupants 

• Fracture & posterior dislocation of the hip due to impact against the dashboard - Dashboard dislocation 
• Whiplash injury 

INJURIES TO THE RESTRAINED DRIVER 
• As the driver is restrained by the seat-belt, steering wheel impact injury and head injury do not occur 
• Fractures of ankles, knees and hip are seen 
• Head injuries and whiplash injuries are rare

• Small intestine avulsions, lacerations of intestine, omentum and mesentery, contusions and perforations 

of intestine may occur due to acute flexion . 
• Rupture of spleen, liver, pancreas, caecum and bladder due to compression between the belt and vertebrae 

• Transverse fracture of L2 or L3 

10. A 26 year old male was found dead on the road-side and was brought for autopsy with an alleged history of 
Road traffic accident {RTA). 
a) What are the common injuries seen in RTA? 
b) Describe the different types of abrasions usually seen in RTA?

laboratory findings?

b) DIFFERENT TYPES OF ABRASIONS SEEN IN RTA: Refer Essay Q No. 8 

• Patterned abrasions 

• Graze abrasions 

c) EXCLUDING HOMICIDE: 

• If a person is killed by some other means (e.g. stab injury) and body disposed on roadside, primary impact 

and secondary impact injuries will be absent. 
• Run-over injuries and tyre marks will be post-mortem in nature. 

Hit and Run: 

• The driver of a vehicle kills a person accidentally and leaves the scene to avoid legal proceedings.  
• Examination of the scene, collection of gross and trace evidence (e.g. tearing of clothes, grease marks, oil, 

broken lamp, windscreen glass, metal fragments, paint particles, etc.) can be helpful. 
• Matching studies of blood, hair, dirt, grease, pint, oil, rust etc. found on the victim and the vehicle in 

quest ion will be helpful.
