2. LUCID INTERVAL **_l,  
• Lucid interval is seen in 

,,,.. Extradural hematoma 
,,, Subdural hematoma 
,,, Insanity 
-,., Heat stroke 
,,, Postictal phase after a seizure in epileptic patients 

Lucid in~erval in EDH 

• Initially, the concussive force that caused the head injury results in an altered level of consciousness 
• After some time, the person regains normal consciousness (concussion resolves spontaneously) 
• The EDH continues to expand until the mass effect of the hemorrhage, results in increased intracranial 

pressure and again decreased level of consciousness 
Medi..olegal importance 

• The person is legally responsible for his acts 
• Can make a valid will 
• Can be a witness and give evidence in court of law 

3 . CONCUSSION OF BRAI~ 

• Synonyms: Stunning brain shock or Commotio cerebri or Mild traumatic brain injury (MTBI) 
• Traumatically induced physiologic disruption in bra in function lasting for few minutes to 24 hours with 

or without loss of consciousness 

• Without any demonstrable organic brain lesion . 
• Usually results in relatively temporary impairment of neurologic function . 
• Occu rs due to accelerating or decelerating brain injury . 

Clinical features 
• Confusion 
• Loss of consciousness (immediately after head injury) 
• Memory loss (retrograde amnesia ) 
• Alteration of mental state or personality 
• Focal neurologic deficits 
• Lucid interval

/ . '.PUNCH DRUNK SYNDROME! 

• Syn : Boxer's encephalopathy, Dementia pugilistica, Chronic traumatic encephalopathy, Pugilist's 

Parkinson syndrome or Goofy's syndrome 

• A condition seen in boxers and alcoholics 

• As a resu lt of repeated cerebral concussions due to repeated blows to the head 

• Punch-drunk syndrome has also been recognized among American footballers, rugby players, and 

wrestlers. 

• In the initial stage there will be deterioration of speed and incoordination 

• Later they develop weakness in the lower limbs, hand tremors, and mental dullness. 

• Final stage - characterized by slurred speech, dementia, ataxia, broad based gait and Parkinsonism like 

facial appearance, loss of memory, outbursts of anger 

• Patient develops tangles and threads in his or her brain that are similar to those found in the brains of 

Alzheimer's patients. 

• In its later stages, patients develop symptoms of dementia and Alzheimer's disease. 

Autopsy findings 

• Healed meningeal hemorrhage 

• Diffuse axonal injury 

• Focal ischemia 

• Thinning of corpus ca llosum 

8. WHIPLASH INJURY*] 

• It is caused by violent acceleration or deceleration force 

• Commonly occurs in the front seat occupants of a vehicle 
 

Mechanism of injury 

When the vehicle comes to a sudden stop (deceleration) due to collision 

..!, 
Produces a forward thrust 

..!, 

Heavy head continues to move forward (sudden acute hyperflexion of spine) 

..!, 
Head strikes the wind screen 

..!, 

Acute hyperextension of the spine as the head is thrown backwards 

..!, 

This double movement is called whiplash 

·=· When a vehicle is hit from behind, there will be sudden hyperextension followed by hyperflexion. This double 

movement can also cause whiplash injury 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOG'f 
I 

RESTING IM PACT HYP EREXTENSION HYPERFLEXIO N 

Effects of whiplash 
• Fracture d islocation of upper cerv ical vertebrae - usually Atlanta-occipital 
• Fracture dislocation of lower cervical vertebrae (CS-6) 
• Fracture dislocation in the upper dorsal spine 
• Contusion and laceration of spinal cord may occur without associated cervical vertebral fracture 
• Milder injuries may result in persistent headache, neck pain, shoulder pain, back pain etc. 
• Infantile whiplash syndrome: viol ent shaking of child holding at chest level results in subdural hematoma 

and intraocular hemorrhages

• Motor cyclist's fracture or Hinge fracture : Due to heavy impact on one side of t he head, transverse fracture 

occurs across the floor of the middle cranial fossa and the skull is divided into two halves 
• Tail-gating or Under running injury 

,. Usually occurs in darkness 
,- Due to sudden and unexpected stoppage of the large vehicle moving in front 
,. The motor cyclist drives into the back of a large veh icle 
,. Injury to head, neck and shoulder

11. What are the objectives of postmortem examination in road accident victims? 
• To find the cause of death. 
• To find the manner of death. 
• To establish the identity of the victims. 
• To identify the driver 

• To reconstruct the position of the victims prior to the accident. 
• To differentiate injuries into primary impact, secondary impact and secondary injuries. 
• To identify the vehicle in hit and run cases. 
• To determine the significance of previous injuries in cases of delayed death .

' FIREARM & EXPLOSION INJURIES 

• All skin wounds shou ld be dissected out carefully w it h surrounding tissue and preserved in glass conta iners 
containing rectified spirit 

• The bullet should be removed with fingers or rubber tipped forceps and not with toothed metal forcPps 
It should not be washed as it would remove any residues. It should be sent to the Forensic science 
laboratory for identification and finding the weapon of firing. 

b) 8 ems = close range. (Refer t o Previous quest ion for diagrams and explanations) 

Purpose of rifling 

• Rifling imparts a spin or rotat ion to the bullet, and it acquires a gyroscopic steadiness during fl ight 
• Rifling enhances speed, range and accuracy 

• Rifl ing increases penetrating power of the bullet 
• Rifling prevents wobbling or tumbling end-over-end of bullet due to w ind/air resistance 

MLI of rifling 

• Rif ling marks can help in identification of the weapon used in a crime. 
• All spent bullet s should be handled with great care to avoid adding other marks to them 
• They should be handled with gloved hands and w ith plastic rubber toothed (not metallic) forceps.

Firearm definition & Classification: Refer to Essay Q No. 1 

WOUNDS PRODUCED BY A SHOT GUN 
The components of explosion and their effects on the wound 

• Flame (up to 30 cm) - Burning, scorching, singeing 
• Smoke (up to 45 cm) - Blackening, fouling or smudging 
• Unburnt gun powder particles (90 cm) - Tattooing or stippling 
• Pellets move as single mass (up to 2 m) - Single entrance wound 

• Wad (up to 5 m) - abrasions 

~0NTACT SHOT 
• Explosive type of injury with gross destruction of tissues 

• On the head 
, Large, irregular wound 
, The skul l and brain will be shattered 
, Extreme mutilat ion of the face occurs. 
, Eyes blown out of sockets 

• On the chest and abdomen: Large, circular or oval wound 
• Flame, smoke and soot are driven into the wound 
• The shot (pellet s) enter t he body en masse 
• Wound may be cherry red color due to t he presence of ca rboxy-hemoglobin 

• Muzzle imprint may be present

4. RICOCHET BUL' .-.. . 

• A Ricochet bullet Is the one that strikes an Intervening object fi rst, before striking the object targeted 

• It ric.ochets and rebounds and then hits the target 

• R1coc.het ng Is very rare 

• Bullet rT'ay be deformed or flat tened after ricocheting 

• Occur~ n inferior quality firearms and low velocity bullets 

Appe "~ c, r"' 

• S,ze - larger than the bullet 
• Shape - keyhole, elongated or triangular with irregular margins 

• Burning, blackening and tattooing are absent in the entry wound 

• As the bullet loses ,t,; gyrating movements abrasion collar Is not seen 

• The bullet may be deformed and morf' superf1C1ally placed an the body

I VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGy 

:\l lvP,, IN A CA~[ OF DEATH DUE TO FIREAR~ 
• Immediately after the dead body is received, clean cotton gauze dipped in rectified spirit is used to mop 

tht' hands and preserved . Then they are sent to the Forensic science laboratory to detect primer residue 

• Whole body X-ray to be done to localize the bullet/pellets 

• Punctures, stains on clothing should be noted and photographed 

• External injuries on the body should be noted and photographed 

• Note the effect of flame, smoke and gunpowder on clothes 

• All wounds should be photographed 

• All skin wounds should be dissected out carefully with surrounding tissue and preserved in glass containers 
containing rectified spirit 

• The bullet should be removed with fingers or rubber tipped forceps and not with toothed metal forceps 

• It should not be washed as it would remove any residues 

• The doctor should mark his initials at the base with a sharp pointed instrument to facilitate its identification 
in court 

• The bullet should be sent to the Forensic science laboratory for identification and finding the weapon of 
firing 

6. \ATYPICAL FIREARM WOUNDS/ PECULIAR_EFFECTS OF F-IREARM~ 
Bullet bruise: Bullet just hits the skin only and drops down to the ground, without any penetration or wound of 
entry, producing a bruise alone at the site of impact. When the bullet is ejected with lesser velocities (unjacke~ed 
bullets, bullet smaller than gun caliber), this is caused. 

Bullet graze (bullet slap): bullet hits the skin at an angle producing an abrasion or a laceration, without any 
penetration or wound of entry. Causes - poor quality bullet or poor aiming 

Ricochet bullet: Refer to Short Note Q. No. 4 

Single Entrv and Multiple Exit Wounds: A bullet after entering into victim's body gets broken into several pieces 
by hitting against any bone and then each fragment ejects out like an individual missile (secondary missile) thus 
producing multiple wounds of exit 

Multiple W~unds of Entry and Exit, but a Single Bullet Recovered: A bullet passing through a victim sitting with 
knees t~uching the chest, wounds of entry and exit of the bullet may be seen on back, chest, knees, etc. 
respectively 

Dum Dum bullet 
• Also called as mushroom bullet 
• The bullet in which nose is open and covered with lead jacket 
• When it strikes the target, the lead jacket expands and 

produces a large entrance wound 



FIREARM & EXPLOSION INJURIES \ 
Tandem bullet or Piggyback bullet 

• Tandem - one behind the other 

• May be due to defect in weapon or faulty ammunit ion 

• First firing - bullet fails to come out 

• Second firing - second bullet comes out carrying the lodged bullet 

• Both may enter the body th rough the same entrance wound or different wounds 

STRIKING BUUET LODGED BULlll 

_--,,i_ tiiiiiii. 
GUN TANDEMBUllETS 

Frangible bullet 

• This type of bullets breaks into multiple pieces when they enter the body 

• After recovery of the bullet, matching with a test bullet becomes difficult 

Souvenir bullet 
• Bullet present inside the body for a long time without causing any symptoms 

• A dense fibrous capsule usually surrounds the bullet 

• Lead poisoning may occur due to slow absorption of lead from lead bullets 

Yawning bullet 
• Travel in irregular fashion at an angle to the line of force 

• As the velocity of the bullet is very much reduced, penetrating power is also less 

• May produce a keyhole entry wound when it strikes tangentially 

Tumbling bullet 
• Rotates 180° in end on end (tumble) during its motion 

• As the velocity of the bullet is very much reduced, penetrating power is also less 

• A superficial lacerated wound may be caused

VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

Kennedy phenomenon 
• Surgical alteration or sut uring of gunshot wounds 
• After surgica l alteration, it cannot be evaluated as entrance or exit wounds 
• Named after the assassination of John Kennedy, former US President 

Rayalaseema phenomenon 
• It is an artefact 
• An individual is killed by stab injury and t hen a bullet is kept inside the wound 
• This is done to mislead the investigation 
• Some cases have been reported from the Raya laseema district of Andhra Pradesh

• It is the study of firearms and ammunition 
• Proximal (Internal) ballistics: study of firearm mechanism and the movement of projectile inside the firearm 
• Intermediate (External) ballistics: study of motion of a projecti le after being ejected from t he firearm t ill the 

time it hits the target 
• Terminal (Wound) ballistics: study of injuries produced by firearms

13. jwounding-p~f b~II~~ 

• It is directly related to the kinetic energy at the moment of impact (E=mv2/2) 
• Thus, t he wounding power depends mainly on the velocity of the bullet 

14. Abrasion collar• 
Refer t o Short Note Q . No. 1 

15. ~ontusion collarl 
• It is a ring of contusion around the entry wound 
• It is caused by the friction of bullet which causes rupture of tiny blood vessels 
• Not see n always 
• Diagram: Refer to Short Note Q. No. 1 

16. ~ rease collarl 
• Lubricant (grease) used for the barrel, is carried by the bullet when it is fired 
• When the bullet enters the skin, the lubricant is wiped of its surface due to the spin of the bullet 

• Not seen always 
• Mostly seen on clothing as a black deposit 
• Diagram: Refer to Short Note Q. No. 1 

17. ~hored (supported) exit woundJ 
• If the skin at the exit wound is supported (belt, waist band of trousers or if the body leans against the wall, 

chair, door) this type of wound is seen 
• Near-Circular exit wound surrounded by abrasion 
• Resembles wound of entrance, but it is devoid of burning, singeing and tattooing

27. Bullet embolism 
• Bullet or fragments may enter systemic circulation and may block a major artery supplying a vital organ 

28. Balling or Welding of shots 
• The pellets of a shot gun are converted into a compact mass 

• On firing, they move as a single mass w ithout dispers ion, and produce a single large entrance wound 
• Occurs due to faulty manufacture, very o ld amm unitions 

29. Billiard ball effect 
• Occurs in smooth bore f irearms 

• Due to extensive dispersion of lead shots because of the pellets striking each ot her in flight 
• Range of firing cannot be determined accurately 

30. Chilled shots 

• Lead shots are basica lly soft, but hardened by tin and antimony 
• Hence, they are also called hardened shots 

31. Tests for detection of firearm residues 

• Harrison and Gilroy test 
• Neutron activation analysis 
• Atomic absorption spectroscopy(AAS) 
• Flameless AAS 
• Dermal nit rat e (paraffin test)

2. A beheaded dead body of a male subject was recovered by side of a railway track with multiple stabs over 

chest, and abdomen. The line of traumatic severance was 1 inch above suprasternal notch without evidences of 

vital reaction. The stabs were showing a significant zone of vital reaction. The head was found 50 feet away 

from the body. Autopsy findings revealed multiple petechial hemorrhagic spots over subpleural surface and 

interlobar fissures of lung. Nail beds and lips were showing bluish discoloration. 

a) How will you establish whether the head was separated ante mortem or postmortem in nature? 

b} Write down the pathophysiology behind the formation of petechial hemorrhage and bluish discoloration. 

c) Whether the head belonged to the some individual. 

d) Define stab wound. Why the length of blade of a weapon may not correspond always with depth of stab 

wound over chest and abdomen? 

a) ANTEMORTEM OR POSTMORTEM: 

• The line of traumatic severance of the head does not show evidence of vital reaction - which suggests 

that t he injury is postmortem. 

• Stab injuries show vital reaction . 

• Thus the person was killed by stab injures and the head was severed after death. 

• Differences between antemortem and postmortem injuries : Refer to Essay Q no: 1 

b) PATHOPHYSIOLOGY OF PETECHIAL HEMORRHAGES: 

• Petechia are small pin-point collections (0.1 to 2.0 mm) of blood. 

• Impaired venous return ➔ Increased venous pres5u re ➔ distension and rupture of venules ➔ petechiae 

• Petechia are usually seen on the skin, sclera, conjunctivae, eyelids, forehead, and mucosa of mouth. 

• They are also seen in the brain, visceral pleura (in the interlobar fissures) and pericardium. 

• Petechia were considered classica l signs of asphyxia. 

• They are also seen in obese individuals dying of natural ca uses, acute right heart failure with markedly 

raised venous pressure and cardiopulmonary resuscitation . 

b) PATHOPHYSIOLOGY OF BLUISH DISCOLORATION: 

• Oxygenated blood is bright red in color. 

• After death, blood becomes purplish blue when oxygen in given up by hemoglobin (reduced Hb) 

• When the concentration of reduced Hb is> 5 mg/dl, cyanosis is evident. 

• After 24 hours of death, cyanosis will appear in any dead body. 

c) WHETHER THE HEAD BELONGED TO THE SAME INDIVIDUAL: 

• When the severed head is placed over the neck, the traumatic line of severance will approximate. 

• Can also be confirmed by DNA matching 

d) STAB INJURY: 

• Definition : Refer to Chapter 8; Essay Q No. 6 

• Depth of the stab wound refers to the length of the blade of the w eapon . 

• Knife partially thrusted: Depth of the wound is usually less than the length of the knife . 

• Knife fu lly thrusted on unyielding surfaces like chest wall - depth of the wound is equal to the length of 

the blade. 



I VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOG~ 

• Knife fully th rusted on yielding surfaces (abdomen, breast, buttocks, thigh) 
,,, Depth of the wound may be more than the length of the blade. 
,,, Because the force of thrust presses the tissues underneath. 
, In such cases, the hilt mark may be seen on the externa l wound . 

Yielding of Abdominal wall -
abdominal wall normal position

4. Define Torture. Enumerate the types/methods/techniques used for torture. Explain the medical findings in a 
case of torture. Discuss the ethical and legal issues related to torture. 

World Medical Association's (WMA) Declaration of Tokyo: defines torture as the deliberate, systematic or 
wanton inflict ion of physical or mental suffering by one or more persons acting alone or on the orders of any 
authority, to force another person to yield information, to make a confession, or for any other reason. 

United Nations Convention against Torture: defines torture as the deliberate infliction of severe pain or suffering q 
on a powerless v ictim. The treatment must be inflicted for a specific purpose, such as forcing the victim to 
confess, provide information, or to punish them 

Physical Torture methods: 
• Beating - most common method 
• Finger torture- Pencil or similar objects are placed in between two fingers, and then squeezed hard or 

twist ed 
• Burns and scalds - Cigarette burns are the most common variety. Victims are made to sta nd in the sun or 

near fire for hours. 
• Cold torture- Victims are made to lie on cold, damp floor or ice slabs 
• Irritant torture- Ir ritants like chili powder, common sa lt are applied on the delicate parts of the body 

(eyes, genitals) or open wounds. 
• Suspension torture- Victim is suspended by his wrists (la bandera), ankles (murcielago), arms or hairs 
• Parrot's perch - Hanging the victim from a stick between knees and arms bound tightly together 



;JIJlrJ -------
VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOG~ 

• Crushing injuries - The most common method is, with the victim seated or lying on the back and a Ghotna 
(a wooden pole used to pound spices and grains) to be ro lled up and down the fron t of the thighs with 
one or more individuals standing on it 

• Operating table torture (el quirofano)- Victim is made to lie on a table with upper half of the body 
unsupported 

• Hogtie torture - Victim is tied in an abnormal position and left for hours 
• Electric torture (Cattle prod) - Electric shock is inflicted on to the se nsitive parts such as nipples, genital<. 

or all over the body. 
• Dry submarine - When victims head is t ightly covered with a plastic bag 
• Wet submarine" - When victims head is immersed in water, urine, vomit etc. 
• Foot whipping, falanga or bastinado - beating on the soles of a person's bare feet . 
• Telefono- Simultaneous beating of both ears w ith palms of the hand 
• Saw horsing -victim is made to stradd le the wooden stick or triangular wooden box w hich is pointing 

upwards with additional weight suspended from their foot . 

Mental Torture methods 
• Socia l Deprivation - Solitary confinement in a dark environment. 
• Deprivation of basic needs - Withholding food, water, toilet facilities, clothing. 
• Prolonged sleep deprivation 
• Mock execution - the victim might be blindfolded, transported to remote place, made to dig their grave, 

followed by holding a gun to their head and pulling the trigger, often shooting near but not touching the 
victims 

• Threats and humiliation- Direct threat, or threat directed towards family m embers, relatives, friends etc 
along with humiliation. 

Sexual Torture methods 
• Victim may be forced to undress in front of others and photographed in humiliating positions. 
• Using instruments - Infliction of injuries to private parts of m ale and female victims 
• Sexual torture using animals- in form of rape by trained dogs and monkeys. Spiders, ants, rats and li za rds 

are introduced into the vagina or anus. 

Consequences of Torture 
• Physical consequences - early or delayed in the form of severe pain, hemorrhage, infection, scars, 

mutilation, disfiguration etc. 
• Psychological consequences - anxiety, depression, phobia, sleep disturbances, alcohol/ drug abusP, P0st· 

traumatic stress disorders, suicidal t endencies etc. 
• Social consequences - social stigma, unemploymc·n t, negativity in social activities 

ROLE Of DOCTORS: Examination and Treatment of Torture victims 
Examination of Torture Victims 

• Torture victims should be exa mined accordin g to the Istanbul protocol. 
• Doctors should obtai n a complete history about different m ethods of torturP . 
• Doctors who examine torture victims should be aware o f peculi. ar types o f scars and d f . . 

f' ·orm1t1es ca used b, 
specifi c types of torture. 



L FIREARM & EXPLOSION INJURIES 

• Some torture methods leave no scars (e.g. electric current causing pain) But ca reful examinc1lIon may 

reveal marks of electric probe marks in the skin . 

• Abrasions, contusions, lacerations and fractures may be in different stages of healing . 

• Bruises or aseptic necrosis of the fosues of the soles . 

• Rupture of tympanic membrane . 

• Bruises of abdominal wall, wrist, arms and neck . 

• Dependent edema and petechial hemorrhages due to pro longed stand ing . 

• Scrotal or perinea! hematoma in saw horsing . 

• Sexually transmitted diseases, injuries to the breasts, external genitalia, vagina, anus o r rectum due to 

sexual abuse. 

• All injuries should be recorded in detail and marked on body diagrams. 

• Photograph of injury should be taken w ith a scale placed near the injuries. 

• X-rays and CT scan should be taken to identify fractures and visceral inju ries. 

Management of Torture Victims 

• A careful and detailed clinica l examination is necessary to derive a complete picture. 

• A doctor should be very sensitive and patient while dealing with torture victim. 

• Doctor should empathize with the patient and the family. 

• Doctor should try to win the patient's confidence, as assessment of psychological trauma is very important. 

• Doctor should avoid situation or objects that remind the victim about torture event. 

• Physica l and psychological treatment should be provided simu ltaneously. 

Ethical aspects of torture 
• As per the Declaration of Tokyo (World Medica l Associat ion, 1975) the doctor shall not 

• Parti ci pate or be accomplices in the practice of torture 

• Provide means, instruments, substances or knowledge designed to faci litate the practice of torture 

• Be present during any procedure involving cruel or inhuman activities. 

• Use any procedure that might alter personality or consciousness, for the purpose of diminishing his 

physical and/ or mental resistance, in the course of police investigation of any other kind 

Legal aspects of torture 
• s 330 ANDS. 331 IPC (Cl 118 BNS) dea l with voluntary causing hurt and grievous hurt fo r the purpose of 

extorting confession 
• As per the World Conference On Human Rights, 1993 freedom from torture is a right that must be 

protected under all circumstances.

7 ) Fracture or dislocation of a bone or tooth

• Which endangers life or 
• Which causes the sufferer to be in severe bodi ly pain or 
• Unable to follow his ordinary pursuit s for a period of 20 days (15 DAYS as per BNS 114)

3. DOWRY DEATHS• 

• Dowry deaths are the deaths of young women (primarily in Indra) who are murdered or driven to suicide 

by continuous harassment and torture by husbands and in-laws ,n an effort to extort an increased dowry 

• Dowry deaths occur either by murder of a married woman or she herself commits suicide, unable to bear 

the harassment or cruelty for not giving dowry 

• The usual defence in most dowry cases rs that the woman committed suicide or death occurred 

accidentally due to bursting of stove while cooking 

• In cases of dowry death, Magistrate's inquest is conducted 

• The postmortem examination should be carried out by a panel of two doctors preferably 

Section 304 8., IPC (Cl 79 BNS) 

(1) Where the death of a woman is caused by any burns or bodily injury or occurs otherwise than under 

normal circumstances within seven years of her mar iagc and rt Is shown that soon before her death she 

was subjected to cruelty or harassment by her husband or any re lative of her husband for, or in 

connection with, any demand for dowry, such death shall be called " dowry death", and such husband or 

re lative shall be deemed to have caused her death 

(2) Whoever commits dowry death shall be punished with imprisonment for a term which shall not be less 

than seven years, but which may extend to imprisonment for life 

4. Weapon identification by examination of injury 

EXAMINATION OF A WOUND 

Type of wound 

• The type of wound should be noted 

• Abrasion, contusion, laceration, fracture or dislocation are caused by blunt weapons 

• Incised wounds and stab wounds are caused by sharp weapons 

• Penetrating or perforating - sharp pointed weapons or firearms 

Shape of the wound 

• Irregular - blunt weapon 

• Linear with clean margins - sharp cutting weapon 

• Triangular or one edge pointed and other edge rounded - single edged knife 

• Fusiform or spindle shaped - double edged weapon 

• Circular - firearm injury 

Margins 

• A lens should be used to examine the margins accurately 

• Abrasions and contusions around margins - heavy blunt weapon 

• Clear cut margins - sharp cutting w eapon 

• Abrasion collar, contusion co llar, grease co llar - firearm injuries 

Foreign bodies 

• Glass pieces, w ood piece, broken tip of knife, bullet, pellets or wads indicate the nature of weapon which 

caused injury

INJURIES DUE TO HEAT, COLD, LIGHTNING & ELECTROCUTION 

CAUSES OF DEATH FROM BURNS 

Immediate causes 

• Primary (Neurogenic) shock: Due to pain or fright. 

• Asphyxia: Suffocation due to inhalation of CO, CO2, cyanide etc. 
• Carbon monoxide poisoning 

• Injuries: due to falling off the building to escape. 

• Laryngospasm and edema of glottis caused by smoke or heat 

Delayed causes 

• HYPOVOLEMIC OR SECONDARY SHOCK 
, Most common cause of death 

► Usually occurs within 24-48 hours. 
• ARDS - Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome 

• Toxemia due to absorption of metabolites from burnt objects 

• Septicemia - most common cause of death, occurring after 4- 5 days 

• Infective complications - gangrene, pyemia, tetanus, pneumonia 

• Renal failure 

• Bleeding from ulcers - Curling's ulcer (seen in 20% cases of burns) at the 1st part of duodenum 

Remote cause 
• Malignancy may develop from a burn scar - Marjolin's ulcer 

2. Classify Burns. Add a note on the salient post-mortem findings in Burns. 
Burns classification: Refer to previous question 

POST-MORTEM FINDINGS IN BURNS 

EXTERNAL FINDINGS 
• Burnt clothing should be carefully removed and examine:d for the presence of kerosene, petrol etc. 

• Site distribution and extent of burning are noted 
I 

• Singeing of hairs of scalp, face, eyebrows and eyelashes 

• Face - distorted and swollen. Tongue - protruded and tip burnt 

• Pink froth - may appear at the mouth and nose due to irritation of the air passages by smoke 

• Crow's feet - burns or soot may be absent at forehead, angles of eyes and nasolabial folds. It indicates 

the closing of the eyes and the resultant wrinkling owing to smoke irritation 

• Postmortem hypostasis and rigor mortis cannot be assessed 

• Degloving of hands and feet 

• Blisters may be seen 

• Heat ruptures 
,- When the heat applied is very great, the skin contracts and splits 

, These are called heat ruptures 
, It may simulate incised or lacerated wounds 

, Usually seen over fleshy areas, like calves and thighs, and over extensor surfaces and joints. 

, They cannot differentiate antemortem from postmortem burns 



=-----------. 
VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

• Pugilist ic attitude 
, Due t o heat st iffening of muscles caused by coagulation of muscle proteins. 

,. The lower limbs are flexed at the hips and knees, the upper limbs are abducted at the shoulders, 

flexed at the elbows and w rists and held out in front of t he body, the fingers are hooked like claws 

and head slightly extended 

, Flexor muscles being bulkier t han extensor, cont ract more and a position of generalized flexion is 

adopted. 
, Commonly adopted by boxers and it is also ca lled boxing, fencing or defense attitude 

,. It occurs irrespective of whether t he person was alive or dead at the time of burns and hence it 

cannot differentiate antemortem from postmortem burns 

INTERNAL FINDINGS 

• Heat hematoma 
, Resembles extradural hemorrhage 

► It consist s of soft, friable clot, chocolate color, honeycom b appearance, 

• Heat fractures of skull may be seen 

• Brain - congested and edematous 

• Soot (carbon) particles may be present in the larynx, trachea and bronchioles - surest sign of 

antemortem burns. 

• Lungs: Congest ed and edem atous 

• Esophagus, stomach and intestines may also contain soot part icles 

• Curling's ulcer (seen in 20% cases of burns) at the 1st part of d uodenum after 3 days 

• Internal organs become shrunk, firm, hardened and cooked by the effect of heat - puppet organs 

• CO levels in the blood will be more than 10% 

• The blood is cherry-red in color . 

3. A 20 year old newly married female was brought dead to hospital with alleged history of accidental burns. 

During autopsy dermo-epidermal burns are present all over the body except for the right lower limb. 

a) Who should conduct Inquest in this case? Give reasons.

4. A lO-year old female domestic maid presents to the emergency with burns over both lower limbs. Her 

employer gives a history of spillage of boiling water over her body parts while she shakes her head in 

disagreement. The burnt areas also look suspicious and not consistent with the history given by the employer. 

a) How will you confirm whether the injury is due to spillage of hot liquid and not by flame or use of 

corrosive? 

b} If the burns are confirmed to be due to corrosive, how will you manage it? 

c) What shall be your medico-legal duties in such a case? 

a) FLAME BURNS or SCALDS or CORROSIVES: Refer short note Q. No. 9, 10 and 11 

Burns due to Flame or Hot liquid or Corrosive can be differentiated easily based on physical features. 

b) M ANAGEMENT OF CHEMICAL BURNS: 

• Prompt wound irrigation is the most critical aspect in limit ing the extent of dermal burns from exposure 

to caustic substances. 

• Irrigation of both acid exposures and alkaline exposures within several minutes decreases the pH change 

in the skin and the extent of dermal injury. 

• Prevent contaminated irrigation solution from running onto unaffected skin. 

• Remove contaminated clothes. 

• After initial decontamination, (by irrigation), the patient must be treated as a typical burn patient. 

• Based on the degree of injury, ensure adequate fluid resuscitation 

• Take precautions to prevent compl ications (hypothermia, infection, rhabdomyolysis). 

c) MEDICOLEGAL DUTIES: 

• Note the preliminary particulars: Name, age, sex, address, occupation, accompanying person's name, 

• Note 2 identification marks . 

• Carefully elicit history.  

• Admission and treatment in a secured ward - no attenders allowed 
 

• Maintain records properly. 

• Collect and preserve samples (Clothing, washes of wound) 

• If the patient is serious, make arrangement for dying declaration. 

• Inform police under Section 39 CrPC. (BNS Cl 33)

2. PUGILISTIC AmTUDE4 

• Synonyms: Heat rigor, Boxing or Fencing or Defence attitude 

• This phenomenon is seen in cases with extensive burn injury(> 6S°C) 

• Due to exposure to heat, the muscle proteins are coagulated irreversibly 

• This resu lts in contraction of muscle fibres 

• The flexor muscles have more bulk & hence flex ion is seen in all parts of the body 

• The legs are flexed at the hips and knees 

• Arms are flexed at elbows and wrists and held out in front of the body 

• Fingers are hooked like claws 

• Contraction of paraspinal muscles causes marked Opisthotonos 

• This defence attitude is commonly adopted by boxers (pugilists) during fight 

• Hence this is called Pugilistic attitude 

• Pugi listic attitude does not differentiate ante-mortem from post -mortem burns 

• Formed due to coagulation of muscle proteins and is seen in both ante-mortem and post-mortem burns

10 ~CALDS' 
• Scald is an injury result ing from the application of moist heat 

• Liquid above 60 Q( or steam ca n cause scalds 

Degrees of scalds 

• First degree: redness 

• Second degree: blisters 

• Third degree: necrosis of dermis 

Clinical features 

• Injury is limited to the area of contact 

• Redness occurs immediately 

• Blisters are seen within few minutes 

• Splashing and dribbling are seen 

• Soddening and bleaching of skin seen 

• Singeing of hair absent 

• No blackening or charring 

• Heal without scarring or contractures

12. ~ EAT CRAMPSj 
• Synonyms: Miner's Cramps, Stoker's Cramps, Fireman's Cramps 
• Seen in persons working in high temperature and when sw eating is profuse 
• Vigorous exercise also results in loss of electrolytes through profuse sweati ng in hot w eather 
• Results from loss of water, sodium and chloride from the body through sweat 
• If the person consumes more water without electrolytes, heat cramps may develop 
• Severe and painful cramps of muscles of arms, legs, abdomen 
• Treatment - i.v. saline causes ra pid relief 

13. HEAT PROSTRATION\ 

• Synonyms: Heat Syncope, Heat Collapse, Heat Exhaustion 
• It is a circulatory disorder resulting from exposure to high t emperature 
• Failure of cardiovascular system to compensate for hypovolemia 
• Person standing in the sun sweats profusely, becomes pale, his BP falls and collapses suddenly due 10 

pooling of blood in the lower limbs 
• Skin- cold, pupils-dilat ed, pulse - w eak and thready, slow respiration 
• Temperature is subnormal 
• Treatment - rest with head down position, replacement of water and electrolytes 



L INJURIES DUE TO HEAT, COLD, LIGHTNING & ELECTROCUTION \ 
14. ~AT HYPERPYREXIA/ HEAT STROKE 

• A life-threatening medical emergency 
• Results from failure of heat regulating center in hypothalamus 
• Predisposing factors - high temperature, heat waves, increased humidity, minor infections, old age, 

alcoholism, thyrotoxicosis 

• Very high body temperature (> 41 °C) 

• Sudden onset with collapse and loss of consciousness 
• The skin is dry, hot without sweating 

• Constricted pupils, rapid pulse and breathing 

• Delirium, coma, convulsions, multiple organ failures 
• Treatment: rapidly cooling the body w ith ice water till the rectal temperature falls below 102°F 
• PM appearances: Degeneration of Purkinje cells - characteristic 

15. FROST BITE 

• Injury caused due to freezing of tissues due to cold exposure 
• Occurs when the tissue temperature drops below 0°C {32°F) 
• Ice crystal formation distorts and destroys the cellular architecture 
• Vascular endothelium is damaged, stasis progresses rapidly to microvascular thrombosis. 
• Finally, thrombosis, dermal ischemia, and superficial necrosis appear 
• Usually occurs in extremities, tip of nose, ear lobules 
• Blisters can also be seen 

16. PARADOXICAL or RECIPRO~~l UNDRESS~GJ 
• Refers to a hypothermic person as a result of cold exposure, taking off his clothes just before deat h 
• He is found dead in an extremely cold environment, but paradoxically without clothes 
• This condition is seen in accidental hypothermia in old persons 
• Prolonged exposure to severe cold results in the failure of thermal regulatory mechanisms 
• This causes t he failure of the vasoconstriction of the arterioles of the skin resulting in flow of blood from 

the body core to the skin 
• Increased blood flow to the skin gives an exaggerated sensat ion of warmth 
• The person feels hot and undresses 

- --
HIDE AND DIE SYNDROME 

• During terminal stages of hypothermia, the victim is disoriented 
• He tries to protect himself from the cold by hiding in corners, cupboard or abandoned room 
• The person may be found dead naked amidst disturbed furniture, pul led over drawers, and emptied 

cupboard which may ri se suspicion about murder fo r the motive of robbery 
• When the person is exposed to severe cold outdoors, he may try to burrow into the snow, bush or soil to 

escape from cold 
• Ultimately t he victim succumbs to hypothermia 
• This is cal led hide and die syndrome 

Mll : 
• Victim found naked amidst disturbed furniture may rise suspicion about sexual offence 
• Visit to the crime scene by an experienced forensic pathologist rules out foul play

18. LICHTENBERG'S FLOWERS 
• Synonyms: Filigree burns, Arborescent marks, 

Lightning marks 
• The cutaneous manifestation of lightning 
• When the effect of lightning is very superficial this 

phenomenon is seen 
• They are multiple, superficial, thin, tortuous, Fern­

leaf like pattern of erythema on skin 
• Commonly seen over the shoulders, chest or flanks 
• Pattern resembles the branches of a tree 
• They may result from staining of tissues by Hb from 

lysed RBCs or rupture of capillaries resulting in 
ecchymoses, along the pathway of current. 

• The usually disappear in few days if the person 
survives

• They are black necrotic spots in the stomach mucosa

Refer short note Q. No. 17 

5. ~sh burns or Spark burn~ 
• Produces by intermittent contact with current 
• Occurs when there is an air gap between metal and skin and the electricity jumps or arcs, from one spot 

to another 

• Exogenous burns 
• Multiple burnt or punched out lesions - when the arc dances over a large surface area of the body -

crocodile skin effect or crocodile flash burns 

6. '.fu"~ent peailiJ 
• Occurs in electrocution 
• Small balls of molten metal, from the electrode, may be carried deep into the t issues 

• These are called current pearls 

7. Electrical Metallization] 
• The site of the entrance in electrocution may be diagnosed histo logically by seeing deposition of metal 

particles on the skin. 

• This is ca lled metallization. 
• This is due to volatilization of the metal and the same being driven into the skin. 

• The same phenomenon is also seen in lightning. 

• It can differentiate the point of entry from exit 
• But cannot differentiate antemortem and postmortem electric burns

IJlcHANICAL ASPHYXIA 

~~ 
• Hanging is a form of asphyxia caused by suspension of body by a ligature which encircles the neck, the 

constricting force being the weight of the body 
Classification of Hanging 

• Based on the position of the knot 

► Typical hanging: if the knot is present at the mid-occiput 
,, Atypical hanging: if the knot is present at any other position 

• Based on the degree of suspension of the body 

-,, Complete hanging: if the body is completely suspended, so that no part of the body touches the 
ground 

► Partial hanging: if any part of the body touches the ground. Partial hanging may be in standing, 
kneeling or lying down position 

• Based on the manner of hanging 
>-' Suicidal hanging 
► Homicidal hanging (lynching) 
► Judicial hanging 
, Accidental hanging 
, Autoerotic hanging (Sexual asphyxia) 

) TYPICAL HANl__ )TYPICAL HANGI

CAUSES OF DEATH IN HANGING 
Immediate causes 

• VENOUS CONGESTION 
,. Due to the compression of jugular veins by the ligature 
,- Causes stoppage of cerebral circulation 
,- Results in rapid rise of intracranial pressure and unconsciousness 
-,, Tension required - 2 to 4 kg 

• ASPHYXIA 
,- Due to the compression of laryngeal and tracheal lumen 
,- Resulting in prevention of air entry to the lungs 
',- Tension required - 15 kg 

• ASPHYXIA+ VENOUS CONGESTION - most common cause of death in hanging 
• CEREBRAL ANOXIA 

',- Due to the pressure on the carotid arteries in the neck 
',- Resulting in deprivation of blood supply to the brain 
',- Tension required to occlude carotid arteries - 5 kg 
-,, Tension required to occlude vertebral arteries - 20 kg 

• REFLEX VAGAL INHIBITION 
',- Due to the stimulation of carotid body receptors 
',- Instantaneous death with no asphyxia! signs 

• FRACTURE DISLOCATION Of CERVICAL VERTEBRAE 
► Occurs if there is long drop of the body (as in Judicial hanging) 
► Fracture dislocation usually occurs in C2·C3 level (Hangman's fracture) rarely at C3-C4 leve l 

Delayed causes 
• Aspiration pneumonia 
• Pulmonary edema 
• Brain infarction 
• Hypoxic encephalopathy 
• Brain abscess 

2. a) Classify asphyxia/ forms of death. Describe the postmortem findings in a case of death due to hanging"'* 
b) Classify Mechanical asphyxia/ deaths. Write in detail about the types, causes of death and post-mortem 
findings of hanging. 
Classification and types of Hanging: Refer previous question. 

!POSTMORTEM FINDINGS INH ANGWGj 
EXTERNAL FINDINGS: NECK 
Ligature mark 

• Principle external finding in hanging 

• A type of Pressure abrasion 

• Oblique in position 

• Inverted 'V' shaped 

• Apex of the inverted Vis usually absent as the knot usual ly does not touch-the body 
• Usually located at or above the level of thyroid cartilage . Below the thyroid cartilage in partial hanging 



~ MECHANICAL ASPHYXIA 

• More pronounced on the opposite side of knot 

• Does not encircle the neck completely (incomplete or faint over the nape of neck) 

• Base of the ligature mark - Hard and parchment like 

• Present both in antemortem hanging and postmortem suspension 

LIGATURE MARK IN HANGING 

: \ Knot corresponds to apex of V 
""~0t . ~l.-..,.__- Inverted V shaped 

Oblique in orientation 
~ bove thyroid cartilage 

Formation of Ligature mark depends on 
• Composition of ligature material 

• Width 
• Mode of application 
• Weight of the body 
• Duration of suspension 
• Position of knot 

❖ Faint ligature mark: broad material (e.g., saree), soft, lesser duration of suspension 

❖ Prominent grooved ligature mark: thin and hard ligature material (e.g., rope, cable), longer duration of suspension 

OTHER EXTERNAL FINDINGS: 
Face 

• Pale in vagal inhibition, spinal injury 

• Congested in asphyxia and venous congestion 

■ Petechiae (Tardieu spots) noted over forehead, face, temples 

Eyes 
• Protruded 
• Tardieu spots seen on the conjunctiva 

■ Le Facies sympathique - due to the unequal pressure on the cervical sympathetic chain by the ligature, the 

eye on the same side will remain open and the pupil is dilated. There will be drooping of eyelid on the other 

side (ante-mortem sign) 
Tongue: Swollen, blue, protruded, bitten 

Salivary dribbling (ante-mortem sign) 
• Seen from the angle of mouth, opposite to the side of knot 

• Due to the stimulation of submandibular salivary glands by the ligature 



.,, VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY J 

Neck: Stretched, elongated rarely 
Genitals 

■ Penis engorged due to congestion 
■ Seminal emission seen due to relaxation of sphinct er 
■ Vagina engorged, blood stained fluid discharge 
• Urine and feces may escape 

Postmortem staining 
■ Seen over forearms, hands, genitalia, lower abdomen, legs and foot 
• Glove and stocking distribution of PM staining 
• Cyanosis: Blue discoloration of lips, fingertips, nail beds, tip of nose, ear lobules

4. A young woman of about 20 years was f ound hanging from ceiling of her room with a dupatta and feet 
dangling from the ground, in her rented accommodation by her landlord today morning at 7 am. She was 
fully clothed, but her clothes were turned inside out. She was seen last alive by her landlord at about 8 pm 
the previous night when she had left for party. Police were called and the body with a ligature intact, was 
shifted to the mortuary by 8 am. The body is presented for postmortem examination at about 10:30 am. You 
are the doctor conducting the postmortem examination. 
a) What findings on the body will help you arriving at the probable tim e since death in this case? Give the 

specific time limits of each for this particular case. 
b) Enumerate the specific samples that you should collect in such a case. Give reasons in support far the 

same. 
c) Define hanging. Which type of hanging is present in this case. 
d) How will you remove the ligature material from the neck of the victim? 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

a) TIME SINCE DEATH: Refer to Chapter 7; Essay Q. No. 6. 
b) SAMPLES TO BE COLLECTED & PRESERVED: 

• Ligature material with knot intact 

• Clothing - to examine for blood stain, seminal stain, foreign matter.  
• Viscera - t o check if she was intoxica ted with drugs or alcohol. 
• Fingernail scrapings - for DNA typing; if she was sexually assaulted . 
• Blood - alcohol or drugs 

• Vaginal swabs - to look fo r spermatozoa . 

• Blood stains on the body - for DNA typing 
• Seminal stains on the body - for DNA typing 

c) TYPE OF HANGING IN THIS CASE: Refer to Essay Q. No. 1. 
• It is a case of complete hanging since her feet did not touch the ground. 
• As her clothes were turned inside out, this could be Homicidal hanging which can be confirmed based on 

other circumstantial evidences and postmortem findings. 
d) REMOVAL OF LIGATURE MATERIAL: 

• Before removing the ligature, the position of the knot should be not ed 
• To remove the ligature, it has to be cut away from t he knot. 
• The cut ends should be t ied with a twine/thread. 
• Labelled and sealed before handing it over to the police.

5. a) Define and Classify asphyxia. Add a note on the Postmortem findings in ligature strangulation.

strangulation by ligature. 

Classification of asphyxia: Refer to Essay Q. No 1 

CAUSES OF DEATH IN STRANGULATION 
• Venous Congestion 

, Due to the compression of Jugular veins by the ligature 
, Causes stoppage of cerebral circulat ion 

,, Results in rapid rise of intracranial pressure and unconsciousness 
• Asphyxia 

, Due to the compression of laryngeal and tracheal lumen 
,, Result ing 1n prevent ion of ai r entry to the lungs 



MECHANICAL ASPHYXIA 

• Asphyxia + Venous Congestion 
• Cerebral anoxia (more common in strangulation) 

,. Due to the pressure on the carotid arteries in the neck 
',,> Resulting in deprivation of blood supply to the brain 

• Reflex vagal inhibition 
',, Due to the s~imulation of carotid body receptors 
,. Instantaneous death with no asphyxia! signs 

• Fracture dislocation of Cervical Vertebrae - rare 

POSTMORTEM FINDINGS IN LIGATURE STRANGULATION 
~-·.. EXTERNAL FINDINGS 
Face 

• Congested 
• Petechiae (Tardieu spots) noted over forehead, eyelids and temples (more numerous than 

hanging} 
• Blood-stained frothy discharge from the ears, nostrils and mouth is a common feature 
• Evidence of abrasions, fingernail scratch marks, and contusions over the face, arms and other 

parts of the body 
Eyes 

• Wide open, protruded 
• Subconjunctival hemorrhages 

Tongue: Swollen, blue, prot ruded, bitten 
Genitals: Involuntary discharge of urine and feces - more common than in hanging 
Injuries: In homicidal st rangulation, t here may be associated injuries in the body like abrasions in t he 
forearms, back of elbows, shoulders etc. 
Cadaveric spasm: sometimes, pieces of cloth, button or hair of the assailant may be firmly grasped in the 
hands due t o cadaveric spasm 
Cyanosis: Blue discoloration of lips, fingertips, nail beds, tip of nose, ear lobules 

EXTERNAL FINDINGS: NECK 

Ligature mark 
■ A type of Pressure abrasion 
• Transverse, ci rcular in posit ion 
• Usually located, at or below the level of thyroid cartilage 
• Encircles the neck completely, more prominent on the front and sides, than on the back of the neck 
• Base of t he ligature mark - soft and reddish 
• I rregu Ia r or cres Cent .IC scratches may be found on the skin of the neck as a result of the victim's 

attempts to pul l the ligature away from the neck

INTERNAL FINDINGS: NECK 
• BLOODLESS DISSECTION OF NECK: neck should be examined after the removal of brain and viscera 

from the chest and abdominal cavities. This method should be followed in all cases of suspected neck 
violence 

• Subcutaneous tissues and muscles under the ligature mark - bruising seen 
• Thyroid cartilage fracture - may be seen 
• Hyoid bone fracture usually not present (as the level of ligature is below the level of hyoid) 
• The rings of trachea may be fractured when considerable force is applied. 
• Mucous membrane of the pharynx, epiglottis and larynx usually show areas of hemorrhagic infiltration 

General internal findings: 
• Congestion of viscera 
• Petechial hemorrhages in the viscera 
• Fluidity of the blood 

MEDICOLEGAL ASPECTS OF STRANGULATION 
• Strangulation is mostly homicidal, followed by accidental. 
• Accidental strangulation 

, Accidental strangulation may occur in uterus, when fetal movement causes the umbilical cord to 
encircle the neck. 

, While playing children may get strangled by ropes, wires, hammocks . 
.,,,, Children also get strangled by the automatic closing of electric windows in cars 

• Suicidal Strangulation - rare. Spanish Windlass technique (suicidal strangulation)- A stick or a rod is 
introduced underneath the ligature and the rod twisted to tighten noose.

7. A 23 years old lady has been brought dead to the casualty with a history of strangulation. What will be the 
duties and responsibilities of the Casualty medical officer? What are the postmortem findings you will look for 

to substantiate the history? 

DUTIES AND RESPONSIBILITIES OF THE CASUALTY MEDICAL OFFICER: 

• Medical Officer has to confirm death 

• M .L.C. details are to be entered in Brought dead register/ separate Accident Register (AR) booklet for 

brought dead cases. 

• Intimation to the nearest police regarding death is given 
• Intimation shall be given to an Officer not below the rank of Sub Inspector of Police & acknowledgment 

obtained 
• The body is sent to the Mortuary for Autopsy. 
• Wounds need not be examined. 

Postmortem findings you will look for to substantiate the history: Refer to Essay Q. No. 5 

8. A forty years old man's body is brought for postmortem examination. An oblique ligature mark is present over 
the neck. Dried salivary stain is noted over the right angle of mouth. Hyoid bone is intact. 
Answer the following: 
a) What is your probable opinion regarding cause of death? 
b) Difference between hanging and strangulation? 
c) How will you differentiate ante mortem hanging from postmortem hanging? 

a) Cause of Death: Hanging 
• Ligature mark is oblique 

• Dried salivary stain on the right angle of the mouth (one of the characteristic feature of antemortem 
hanging) 

b) Differences between Hanging and Strangulation: Refer to Essay Q. No. 6 

c) Differences between antemortem Hanging & postmortem hanging: Refer to Essay Q. No. 3 

9. The dead body of a 25 year old lady, married 2 years ago, was found suspending from a ceiling fan in her 
house. Police was informed by her husband and in-laws. The body was sent for postmortem examination 
suspecting it to be a case of homicide. 

a) What findings in the neck would suggest that the Jody died of ligature strangulation? 
b) What findings in the crime scene would suggest that she was killed before the body was suspended? 
c) Under what section {s) of /PC, the husband and in-laws, can be booked, if this case turns out to be a 

suicide? 

d) What are the postmortem findings and ideal autopsy samples to be preserved in this case? 

a) NECK FINDINGS SUGGESTIVE OF LIGATURE STRANGULATION: Refer to Essay Q. No. 5 



-MECHANICAL ASPHYXIA 

b) CIRCUMSTANTIAL EVIDENCE OF HOMICIDE: 

• Strangulation is mostly homicidal, unless proved otherwise . 

• Gagging of mouth . 

• limbs tied . 

• When there are more than one turn of ligature, each firmly tied around neck, homicide is certain . 

• Entanglement of clothing or hair with the ligature . 

• Abrasions - due to movement of the ligature around the neck . 

• Fingernail marks - either of victim's attempt to remove the ligature or of assailant's to secure the 
ligature. 

• Other injuries on the body 

• Usually occurs in open places . 

• When it occurs inside a room, it is not closed from inside . 

• Signs of struggle: disturbed furniture, disturbed bed sheets, torn clothing etc. will be present . 

c) IPC sections: 
• Since she woman is married for 2 years only, the husband and in-laws will be tried under S 304-B IPC (Cl 

79 BNS) (dowry death)

• Nail marks - irregular or crescentic scratches found on the front and sides of the neck. 
• Round or oval contusions of size 1-2 cm (six penny bruise) caused by the pressure of f ingertips may

violence.

• Mechanical occlusion of the nostrils and mouth 
• Smothering agent may be hand, pillow or a cloth 

HOMICIDAL SMOTHERING

pillow etc. 

• Post-mortem findings

~ AGGINGJ 

• Gagging is a method of c1sphyxial death in which a soft cloth or some other foreign object is thrust into 
the throat and obstructing air passage 

• Even if the cloth is small, it absorbs saliva and swells 
• Slowly it completely blocks the air entry 

CHOKI@ 

• Choking is a method of asphyxia caused by entry of a foreign body into the air passage and causing 
obstruction at the level of larynx or trachea 

• It is always accidental 
• Commonly seen in children who play with small objects like an eraser, peanut, coins, buttons etc by 

putting in their mouth 
• Also seen in adults under alcohol intoxication and elderly people suffering from neurodegenerative 

disorders - they may aspirate food particles 
1Cafe_C orona r~ 

• Also called Food asphyxia 
• This is the accidental entry of a large bolus of food or meat into air passage obstruct ing the larynx. 
• The cause of death is reflex vagal inhibition due to choking resulting in cardiac arrest. 
• Seen in patients who are under the influence of alcohol. 
• Suppression of gag-reflex due to alcohol or drugs makes the individual susceptible to Cate Coronary 
• M ay happen when a well-nourished person dying suddenly and unexpectedly in restaurants and cafes, 

shortly after having food, without any signs of respiratory distress. 
• Death appears to be due to heart attack. Hence the name cafe coronary (coined by Dr. Roger Haugen) 
• First aid: Heimlich's maneuver 
• At autopsy, a large piece of food bolus is found obstructing the larynx. 
• High level of blood alcohol found in most cases

/POSITIONAL (POSTURAL) ASPHYXI~ 
• Inability to breathe adequately due to abnormal position of the body. 
• Neck twisting with kinking or compression of trachea and/or elevation of tongue into the posterior 

hypopharynx. 

• When the chest is prevented from expansion by internal pressure (body upside down and abdominal 
organs press on the diaphragm) 

• Jack-knife position - thighs and knees are brought against the chest. 

• Restraint asphyxia - Hobble Restraint is defined as binding an individual's wrists together behind his 
back, binding his ankles together, then bending his knees and tying his bound wrists and ankles together 
above his back (Hog-tying) 

pVERLYING OR OVERLAYING/

• Always accidental

[BURKINGj

• His companion pulls the victim by leg and drags him in the floor

15. Define drowning. What ore the types of drowning? Discuss the pathophysiology of fresh-water and sea water 
drowning. Add a note on the causes of death in drowning 

~OWNING 
• Drowning is a form of asphyxia caused by entry of water or any fluid into the air passages caused by 

complete or partial submersion into water or other fluid 

DROWNING 
TYPICAL or ATYPICAL 

WET DROWNING DROWNING 
Fresh water 
drowning Dry drowning 

Y Salt-water Immersion syndrome

  Hydrocution

Shallow water 
drowning 

TYPICAL or PRIMARY or WET DROWNING 
• Water is inhaled into the lungs 
• Death is due to pulmonary edema, cardiac arrest or ventricular fibrillation 

DRY DROWNING 
• Water does not enter the lungs 
• Death is due to immediate and sustained laryngeal spasm which is init iated by the sudden entry of water 

into the larynx 

IMMERSION SYNDROME or HYDROCUTION or SUBMERSION INHIBITION 
• Seen in temperate and cold countries 
• More common in children 
• Death is due to cardiac arrest due to vagal inhibition 
• Vagal inhibition due to the stimulation of the vagal nerve endings caused by 

Sudden entry of
0  c
 old water into ear, nasopharynx or larynx 

,, Water striking the epigastrium while diving 
c Cold water stimulating the nerve endings of the body

• Predisposing factors 
:: Young swimmers 
c Under the influence of alcohol 
2 Excitement and zmotions 
:: Overeating prior to swimming 

• Instantaneous loss of consciousness occu rs with no classical signs of drowning 

• Usually diagnosed by exclusion 

SECONDARY or NEAR DROWNING or POST IMMERSION SYNDROME 
• If the victim survives for 24 hours or more, he develops secondary complications of drowning 

• Common complications are pneumonia, sepsis, ARDS, hypoxic brain damage 
• The extent and duration of Cerebral ischemia determines mortality and morbidity 

SHALLOW WATER DROWNING or SUBMERSION OF THE UNCONSCIOUS 
• Epileptics, unconscious people and people under the influence of alcohol may get drowning in shallow 

water 
• Since there are no struggl ing movements, churning is absent and hence classical features of drowning are 

not seen 

PATHOPHYSIOLOGY OF DROWNING 
FRESH WATER DROWNING 

• Fresh water contains 0.6% NaCl 

• Fresh water causes denaturation of the surfactant lining of the alveolar wall and jeopardizes oxygen 
exchange 

• Fresh water enters circulation through the semipermeable alveolar lining 
• This results in increased blood volume 

• Entry of water into the circulation causes hemodilution 
• Hemodilution leads to 

,, Hemolysis 

, Hemoglobinuria/hemoglobinemia 
, Hyperkalemia (due to RBC lysis) 
,, Hyponatremia 

• Death occurs due to cardiac arrest as a result of 

,, Ventricular fibrillation (due to hyperkalemia) 

,, Increased cardiac workload (due to increased blood volume) 
,, Myocardial ischemia (due to hemolysis and hypoxia) 

• Rapid death in 4 - 5 minutes

\sAt"TWATER DROWNING! 
• Sea water contains > 3% NaCl 
• Sea water dilutes or washes away the surfactant 
• Water from circulation enters alveolar spaces resulting in Pulmonary edema 
• Blood volume decreases ➔ Hypovolemia ➔ Shock 

• Hemoconcentration occurs 
• Hypernatremia, Hypermagnesemia 
• Relative increase of RBC count, Crenated appearance of RBCs 
• Death due to heart failure, Pulmonary Edema 

• Slow death

CAUSES OF DEATH FROM DROWNING 
• Asphyxia - due to entry of fluid into the lungs 
• Laryngeal spasm - due to entry of fluid into the larynx 
• Vagal inhibition - due to stimulation of vagal nerve endings 
• Injuries - Head injuries, Fracture dislocation of cervical vertebrae 
• Ventricular fibrillation 
• Post-hypoxic encephalopathy

d) GITTLER'S TEST (CHLORIDE TEST) 

• In freshwat er drowning, (freshwater contains lower chlor·ide t han blood) Chloride content of the left 
heart decreases. 

• In sea water drowning, (seawater contains higher chloride than blood) Chloride content of the left heart 
increases. 

• ~orma lly, chloride content is equal in t he left & right chambers of the heart (600mg%) 
• Greater than 25mg% difference indicates death due to drowning 
• This test is obsolete now.

10. A dead body was reportedly floating in a nearby lake and subsequently recovered by the police and sent for

d} Mention the additional steps you will take in this case, apart from the routine to aid in investigation. 

a) POSTMORTEM CHANGES TO CORROBORATE HISTORY GIVEN BY THE POLICE: As per history given by the 
police the t ime since death would be less than 6 hours. The following postmortem changes may be present 

• Changes in the eye 
, Flaccid eyeballs (due to fall in IOP): 4-6 hours. 
,, Tache noire: 3-6 hours. 
,, Cornea becomes hazy and opaque: 6- 8 hours. 

• Algor mortis (Postmortem cooling) 
, Reduced body temperature 

• Livor mortis (Postmortem hypostasis) 
, Mottled patches: 1 to 3 hours after death 
,, Patches coalesce to form uniform area of staining: 4 to 6 hours. 

• Rigor mortis 
,, Heart muscles - 1 hour 
,, Eyelids - 2 hours 
,, Full body - 4 to 6 hours

c) MANNER OF DEATH: 
• Accidental drowning 

► Common in non-swimmers, children 
► People with h/o epilepsy, under the influence of alcohol or drugs, coronary artery disease may fall in 

water bodies and die 
► Swimmers while diving may sustain head injury resulting in unconsciousness and die of accidental 

drowning 
• Suicidal drowning 

► Drowning is a common method of suicide by women in India 
► Usually they are fully dressed. 
► Swimmers usually tie their hands and feet before entering into water. 
► Some may tie heavy stones or objects to their body and dive into water 
► Few may comsume poison or cut their throat before entering into water.

► Very rare in adults 

I ► Hands and feet may be tied. 
► Nail marks may be present on the neck due to strangulation or throttling before drowning. 
► Other signs of struggle or marks of violence (bruises) on the body are seen. 

SHORT NOTES: 

1. ~IGNS OF ANTE MORTEM HANG IN~ 
• Cyanosis of face, ears and lips 
• Protrusion of tongue 
• Salivary dribbling mark 

► Seen from the angle of mouth, opposite to the side of knot 
► Due to the stimulation of submandibular salivary glands by the ligature 

• Le Facies sympathique - due to the unequal pressure on the cervical sympathetic chain by the ligature, the 
eye on the same side will remain open and the pupil is dilated. There will be drooping of eyelid on the other 
side 

• Intimal tears of carotid artery 
• Vital reaction in the tissues under the ligature mark

Hyoid bone fractures in hanging 

• Fractures of hyoid bone are usually seen in the age group above 40 years 
• In individuals < 40 years, hyoid bone is cartilaginous, and fractures are uncommon 
• Usual fracture site - at the greater cornua at the junction of inner two thirds and outer one thirds 

Significance of hyoid bone 
• Ante mortem fracture of hyoid bone is diagnosed by the evidence of hemorrhage around the fracture sit e 
• Microscopically, extravasation of RBCs seen in the fracture site 
• In hanging, hyoid bone fractures are seen in 30% of the individuals above 40 years 
• Hyoid bone fracture is more common in manual strangulation 
• Hyoid bone is delicate and may fracture during removal of neck structures during autopsy 
• Hence, if suspected, X-ray or CT-scan can be taken before autopsy to diagnose ante mortem fracture 

3, ~UDICIAL HANGING1 
• Legal death sentence in India is executed by hanging until death 
• The face of the person is covered with a dark mask 
• The knot is usually placed under left ear or chin (Atypical hanging) 

• Sub-mental position of the knot - most effective 
• The person is made to stand on a platform with trap door which opens downwards 
• When the bolt is drawn, the trapdoors open and the person drops down 
• There will be a long drop of at least 10-15 feet . The drop is calculated based on the build and weight of 

the person. 
• At the end of the drop, violent jerking causes fracture diSlocation of spine at C2-C3 level (hangman's 

fracture) or less commonly C3-C4 level resulting in injury to the spinal cord which results in death. 

• Extensive laceration of neck structure~ are sometimes seen. 

• Tears of sternomastoid muscle are often seen 



- - --- - --- - - ----~~~ 
VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXIC~ 

• Amussat's sign: Transverse intimal tears of carotid arteries - in cases associated with long drop (e.g. 
judicial hanging) 

• Asphyxia signs are not marked in judicial hangings. 
• Doctor must be present to certify death. 

4. Differences between antemortem hanging and post-mortem suspension: Refer to Essay Q. No. 3 

5. g><UAL ASPHYXIA** or AUTOEROTIC DEATHS*! 
• Synonyms: autoerotic asphyxia, asphyxiophilia, hypoxiophilia, Kotzwainism, masochistic asphyxia, 

sexual hanging 
• Usually seen in young males with abnormal sexual behavior like masochism or transvestism 
• Partial reduction of blood supply to the brain may cause sexual pleasure and hallucinations of erotic type. 

Methods 
• Partial hanging is the most common form - asphyxiation by placing a noose around his neck and pulls the 

other end of the rope with a pulley type arrangement. Accidental death may occur due to tightening of 
ligature or failure of the pulley to release the pressure on neck. 

• Electrical stimulation of genitalia 
• Covering of face in a plastic bag 
• Glue sniffing 

Circumstantial evidence 
• Closed room 
• Padding under the ligature 
• Undressed or dressed like a woman 
• Pornographic literature 
• Drugs like cocaine, alcohol 
• Masochistic injuries - cigarette burns, clamps to breasts and genitalia 
• Semen ejaculated 
• May be practiced in front of mirror or recorded in video camera

8. /MUGGINGj 
• Also called - arm lock, strangle hold 
• Mugging is a method of homicidal strangulation 
• Strangulation is caused by compressing the neck in the elbow bent or knee bent 
• The victim is usually attacked from behind 

Post-mortem appearances 

• No signs of violence or strangulation present in the neck_ because it is compressed by the soft muscles 
of the arm or leg 

• Signs of struggle present sometimes - nail marks around the neck 
• Internally- signs are minimal or absent

• They are present in natural w ater sources, more abundantly in stagnant water 

• There are more than 15000 species 
• Drowning ➔ Diatoms in lungs ➔ ruptured alveolar walls ➔ left heart ➔ Systemic circulation ➔ brain, 

bone marrow, liver 
• If the same species of diatoms is detected in t issues and water from suspected source, it confirms death 

due to drowning 
• Diatoms can be demonstrated by histological examination of lung tissues or acid digestion of bone 

marrow

Starvat ion is due to complete or partial deprivat ion of regular and constant supply of food required 

maintaining human li fe 

Typ , d ~•, --v:it rn 
Acute starvation (total fasting) - sudden and complete withholding of food 

Chronic starvation (malnutrition) - gradual withholding of food 

Clinir .,1 f, t J <-' •, n ~t ,r : (develop in a characte ristic chronologica l order) 

Loss of w ell-being and hunger, hunger pains, and craving for food 

• Apathy and fa tigue. 
Weight loss, more rapid in the fi rst 6 months of starvation than afterward. 

• Pigmentation, cachexia, and hypothermia 
Extreme lethargy, intellectual disabilities, and loss of self-respect 

Hunger edema. 

• Reduced resistance to infections in general, and development of diarrhea and tuberculosis, or other 

intercurrent infections.

IMPOTENCE 
• Impotence 1s the inability of a person to perform t he act of sexual int ercourse 

STERILITY 
• Sterility in male: inability to impregnate a female 
• Sterility in female: inability to conceive 

INFERTILITY 
• Failure to conceive (regardless of cause) after 1 year of unprot ected and regular intercourse 

❖ An impotent need not be sterile and vice versa, but both condit ions can coexist

t,...IMPOTENCE & STERILITY \ 
Functional impotence 

• First night impotence or bridegroom impotence: Due to fear, anxiety and timidity 

• lmpotentia quoad persona (quoad hanc): Impot ent t o a part icular female 

• Sexual aversion disorder: Aversion to all females 
Other causes 

• Endocrine dist urbances: Diabet ic neuropathy, Hypopituitarism, Hypothyroidism 

• CNS: hemiplegia, paraplegia, tabes dorsalis, spina bifida, meningomyelocele 
• Atherosclerosis 

• Beta blockers, Alpha blockers, CNS depressants, amphetamines, Selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors 
(SSR ls), diuretics 

• Substa nce abuse : alcohol, morphine, cocaine, heroin, cannabis 

• Chronic poisoning: Exposure to poisons, e.g. lead, arsenic, pesticides or aphrodisiac agents 
CAUSES OF STERILITY IN MALES 

• Age: very old and very young may be sterile 

• Bi lateral cryptorchidism 

• Surgical removal of testis after puberty 
• Endocri ne disturbances: Addison' s disease, Hypopituitarism, Hypothyroidism 

• Klinefelter' s syndrome 
• Infections of t estis: Mumps, tuberculosis 
• Trauma to the t estis, vas deferens

Age

Age

ARTIFICIAL INSEMINATION 

• Introduction of semen into the vagina, cervix or uterus by means other than by coitus to bring about 
pregnancy is called asation 

Types 
• Artificial Insemination Homologous (AIH) 

, Husband's semen is used 
• Artificial Insemination Donor (AID) or Heterologous artificial insemination 

, Semen of some other person is used 
• Artificial Insemination Homologous Donor (AIHD) 

, A mixture of husband's semen with the donor semen (Pooled semen) obtained from the sperm 
bank is becoming popular 

INDICATIONS FOR ARTIFICIAL NSEMINATION 
Women 

• Absent or non-patent fallopian tubes 
• Hostile cervical mucus 
• Refractory endometriosis 
• Unexplained infertility 
• Widows or unmarried women desiring for children 

Men 
• AIH

, Due to congenital anomalies (epispadias or hypospadias) husband is unable to depos

l IMPOTENCE & STERILITY \ 
Biological aspects 

e Semen is obtained by masturbation in a sterile glass container after 3 days of abstinence 

• It should be used within 2 hours of collection 

• 1 ml of semen is deposited above the internal os by a sterile syringe, at or about the time of ovulation 

Precautions 

• Doctor should obtain signed request from the wife and husband 

• Written informed consent from both the husband and wife 

• It is better to obtain the consent of the donor and his wife (but not mandatory) 

• Identity of the donor must not be revealed 

• The results of insemination and details of the recipient and her husband should not be revealed to the 

donor 

• The donor must be mentally & physica lly healthy; should not have any hereditary/familial diseases 

• The donor should have healthy children 

• The donor must not be a relative of either spouse 

• The donor should be fertile, and his age should be< 40 years 

• The donor should be of the same blood group as that of the recipient' s husband 

• Rh compatibility between the donor and the recipient should be tested 

• The donor should give a written declaration that he will not claim parenthood for the child in future 

• The detailed clinical records should be maintained

• Also called lmpotentia quoad persona or Psychological impotence

4 Nocturnal Penile Tumescence Testin~ 
• Normally, a man has 5-6 erect ions during the REM sleep 
• If these erections are absent, 1t 1nd1cates neurological o r vascular pathology 
• If these erections are present It indicates psychogenic impot ence 
• Thus, this test helps to distinguish psychogenic impot ence from other causes

R1.:pture of hymen 
Hymen usually gets ruptured (posteriorly at s, 7 or 6 O clock positions) during fir st sexual intercourse 

Accidenta l - fall on a projecting object 

• Masturbation with large objects 

• Foreign body insertion (sola pith insertion into the vagina of very young gir ls to render them suitable for 

sexual intercourse) 

• Careless insertion of sanitary tampons with projections 

• Surgery for imperforate hymen 
• Infectious diseases: ulceration in d1phther1a 

Med1colegal aspects of hymen and virginity 
• Annular type - may appear intact even after sexual intercourse has taken place 

• Fimbriated type - may appear torn even in the intact state 
< The ruptured segments of hymen gradually become thicker and smaller and appear as small fleshy 

pyramidal projections - carunculae hymenales (hymenal tags) 

• After vagina l delivery, the hymenal tags may disappear or attain irregu lar thick margins - carunculae 
myritiformis

6. ipSEUDOCYES!S*] 
• S yn: Spurious, False, Feigned, Hysterical or Phantom pregnancy 
• A woman believes and feels that she is pregnant though she is in fact not pregnant 

• Usually seen in 
, Woman nearing menopause. 
, Young woman who strongly desire a child. 
, Those with psychological or hormonal disorder .. 

• Patients may present with all the subjective symptoms of pregnancy. 
• Abdominal enlargement may be due to abnormal deposition of fat, ascites or an ovarian tumor 

• Breast changes are sometimes present 
• Woman may imagine fetal movements 

. d ly the condition may continue for full period of pregnancy 
• If  not diagnose ear 

r·,ence false labor pains which cease abruptly if she is told that she is not pregnant 
• Few cases may expe

• Age of the fetuses different; varies b/w 1- 3 months 
• Both fetuses will grow 

• One fetus is more matured than the other 
• Fetus papyraceus (Fetus compressus) - one of the fetus dies early in pregnancy, and get compressed b

the growth of the oth 1

10 IN VITRO FERTILIZATION {TEST TUBE BABY) 

• In vrt ro fe1t rl11 .i t1011 (IVI) Is a w mplex srries of procedures for couples su ffering from infertility wherein 
mature er,g'> .JI l ' fN trl11ed by <,peI m rn a lab, whrch rs incubated and monitored t hroughout the process of

Indications

• Severe male factor infertility 
• Unexplained infertility 

Steps: 

• Ovulation inductio n with clomiphene/FSH/GnRH

• Cultured trll 4 ce ll/8cell or biastocyst stage 
• Embryo transfer (into the uterus 1 cm below the fundus) .

11. THEASSISTED REPRODUCTIVE TECHNOLOGY {~GULATION) A_0~ 021 
Purpose of the Act 

• To regulate and supervise the Assisted Reproduct ive Techno logy (ART) clinics and t he ART banks 
• To prevent of misuse of ART services 
• To ensu re safe and ethical practice ART serv ices 

Provisions of the Act 
The National Assisted Reproductive Technology and Surrogacy board 

• Lays down a Code of Conduct to be observed by persons working at ART clinics and banks. 
• Sets minimum standards of physical infrastructure, laboratory and diagnostic equipment and expert 

manpower to be employed by clinics and banks. 
• Supervises t he funct ioning of the National Registry . 
• Liaison with t he State Assisted Reproductive Technology and Surrogacy boards . 

The National Assisted Reproductive Technology and Surrogacy Registry 
• The Registry shall act as a central database in the country through which the details of all ART clinics and 

banks, including nature and types of services provided by them . 
• f very ART clinic and bank must be registered under the Nat ional Registry. 
• No person/ clinic/bank shall render ART procedures unless they are registered w ith the Registry. 
• The Registration must be renewed every five years. 
• Registration may be cancelled or suspended if an entity contravenes the provisions of the Act . 

Duties of ART clinics and ART banks 

• Ensure that commissioning couple, woman and donors of gametes are eligible to avail the ART services .
•  
 Maintain detailed records of all donor's oocytes, sperm or embryos used or unused, for at least a period 

of ten years. After that, records can be t ransferred to a central database of the National Registry 
• Not more than 3 oocytes or embryos may be placed in the uterus of a woman during the treatment 

cycle. 

• A woman sha ll not be trea ted with gamet es or embryos der ived from more than one m an or woman 
during any one treatment cycle. 

• A clinic shall never mix semen from two individuals for the procedures . 
• The clinic shall not use ovum t hat are derived from a foetus, in any process of in-vitro fe rtil ization . 
• Sex selection is prohibited .

offences and Penalties · 

• Any couple involved in commercial surrogacy shall be punished 

,- First offence: imprisonment up to s years & fine up to Rs 50,000 
,- Subsequent offence: imprisonment up to 10 years & fine up to Rs 1,00,000. 

• Any person, organization or clinic involved in exploitation of surrogate mothers or children born th rough 

surrogacy, shall be punished with imprisonment for up to 10 years and fine up to Rs 10 lakhs. 

14 PRECI_PITATE DELIVER'f 

• When all the three stages of the labour occur in very quick succession, so that delivery occurs suddenly or 

very rapidly, it 1s ca lled preci pitate labour 

• Commonly seen in multiparous women 
• Extremely rare in primipara 

• The delivery occurs suddenly and rapidly without the knowledge of the mother 

• Occurs due to hyperactivity of uterus and lack of tone of cervix 

• The delivery may occur when the mother is trying to pass stools or during t he periods of unconsciousness 

during epilepsy etc 

• The child may die of 
:,.. Subdural hemorrhage due to fall on the floor 
,, Drowning if it falls into a lavatory pan 
,- Hemorrhage from the torn end of the umbilical cord 

Autopsy findings 
• Foreign bodies li ke mud, sand may be seen on the head or body of the foetus 

• Ca put succedaneum and moulding of the head are absent 

• Fissure fractures of the skull 
• Blood, meconium, vaginal mucus may be seen in the air passages 

Medicolegal importance 
• The mother m ay be accused of infanticide, though the cause of death is due to injury because of 

precipitate labour 
• The child m ay be ki lled by other means, and the mother and her relatives may claim death due to 

precipitate labour 

15. SIGNS OF RECENT DELIVERY IN THE LIVING* *!

General appearance 

• Pale and exhausted for 2-3 days 
• Dark colored pigmentation over the lower eyelids 

• Pulse & body temperature are slightly increased 

Breasts 
• Breast s are full en larged and tender with nodular feeling 

• Prom inent surface veins 

• Nipples - enlarged 
• Areola _ Large and dark with prominent Montgomery's tubercles. 

• Colostrum or milk may be expressed 
• Colostrum is a strong evidence of delivery within a few days
