'!JY_}. QUESTIONS 

1. False virgin• /Pseudo virginj 

• In some cases, the hymen remains intact even after sexual intercourse 
• They are called False virgin 
• Occurs when the hymen is 

, fleshy and elastic 
, t hick, tough with big central opening 
, situated higher up in the vagina 

• They are identified by other features such as gaping of labia majora, enlarged clitoris, dilated vagina, absence 
of rugosity in vagina, pendulous breasts with enlarged nipples 

2. Fecundation ab extra 
• Conception is possible without penetration of vagina by penis 
• When sperm is deposited in the vulva (or even on the inner aspect of thighs), insemination can occur due to 

the passage of sperm from external genitalia to the uterus 

---- -- ;-J 
3 Sectio~ 416 CrPC(BNS Cl 457)1 

• S 416 CrPC : If a woman sentenced to death is pregnant, High court shall commute death sentence to life 
imprisonment 

4 Affiliation cases 
• In case of an illegitimate child, the woman may allege a man to be the father 
• Then she files a case in the court to fix paternity 
• This is called affiliation case 

-
5 Suppositious chi ldren 

• Suppositious (fictitious) child is the one produced by a woman claiming to be her own child, whereas real fact 
is not so 

• A woman may pretend pregnancy and delivery and later produce a living child 
• Or she may substitute a male chi ld for a female child 
• Or she may substitute a living ch ild for an aborted child 
• Legal issues _ inheritance of property, blackmailing a male, claim for higher compensation 

6 Posthumous child 
• A child born after the death of its father is ca lled posthumous child 
• Legal issues involved are_ legitimacy, inheritance of property, defamation et c. 

7 Atavism 
• The child does not resemble parents but resemble grand-parents or great grand-parents. 
• This is ca lled atavism

RAPE 

(S q ;or 375 PC, .lOl.s Amendme t) (Cl 63 BNS) 
A man is said to commit rape if he, 
(a} Penetrates his penis, t o any extent, into the vagina, mouth, urethra or anus of another person or makes the 

person to do so with him or any other person; or 
(b} Insert s, to any ext ent , any object or a part of the body, not being the penis, into the vagina, the ureth ra or anus 

of another person o r makes t he person to do so w ith him or any other person; or 
(c} Manipulat es any part of the body of another person so as to cause penetration into the vagina, the urethra or 

anus of another person or makes the person to do so w ith him or any other person; o r 
(d) Applies his mouth to t he penis, vagina, anus, urethra of another person or m akes the person t o do so wit h him 

or any other person 

Under the circumstances falli ng under any of t he following seven descr iptions 
(1 ) Against her person's W' I 
(2) W it hout her con~ 

(3) W it h her consent when her consent has been obtained by putt ing her person or any person in w hom she is 
interested in fe1r f de1t~ or of hurt 

(4) With her consent, when the man knows that he is not her husband and that her consent is given because she 
believes that he is another m an to whom she is or believes to be lawfully married 

(5 ) With her consent, when, at t he time of giving such consent, by reason of unsoundness of mind or intoxication 
or t he administ rat ion by him, personally or through another of any stupefying or unwho lesom e substance, she 
1s unable t o understa nd t he nat ure and consequences of that action t o which she gives consent 

(6) With or w ithout her consent, when she Is under ~ighteen years of age 
(7) When she Is unable t o communicate consent

VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

Gener~I ~amination 
• General demeanour: distressed, shocked, intoxicated, agi tated, tearful, co-operative or aggressive 

• Record vital parameters - BP, pulse, temperature 

Clothtnr, 
• Not attempts should be made to undress her 

• Ask the victim to stand on a large clean, white paper and then undress herself 

• Collect all the materials that fall on the paper 

• Collect stains (blood, semen, sa liva, ur ine) if present on the clothes 

• If the sa me cloth was worn during the incident, cloth should be preserved for trace evidence collection 

Extragenital 1nju1 ies 
• Examine fingernails for any damage or any materials underneath which has t o be scraped off 

• Signs of struggle: 
,.. Abrasions (nail scratches) may be seen on the face, breast s, back of elbow s 

, Contusions may be seen on the arms due to forceful restraint of the victim (six penny bruise) 

• Love bite marks (suction petechiae) - due to suction; seen over neck, breast s, face or shoulders, teeth 

impressions are not usually seen 

• True bite marks - deep impressions on the teeth due to forcible bites; usually seen over breasts, neck, 

lower abdomen or thighs 

• Note the injuries on body chart/sketches and photographs should be taken with scale (picture)

GENITAL EXAMINATION 

• Victim should be examined in the lithotomy position 
• Comb pubic hair to det ect presence of foreign hair 

• Matted pubic hair with blood/semen should be cut with scissors 

• External genitalia: abrasions, contusions or lacerat ions may be seen on the vulva 
• Internal examination 

, Hymen - ruptured or intact ; note the position of tears 
,,. Glaister-Keen globes 

o Glass rod bent at 150° at one end which has a spherical head at t he tip. 
o The spherical glass head is gently passed through the hymenal aperture and then eased 

forwards. 

o Now, the hymenal edges are slightly everted . 
o This procedure does not cau se any pain or injury. 
o When Glaister-Keen globes are not available, Foley's catheter can be used. 

,,. Due to penile penetration in a virgin, hymen rupture usually occurs posteriorly at the 5 - 7' 0 
clock positions 

, Vagina - note irjuries, discharges, foreign bodies 
,,. Collect washings of posterior fornix and vaginal swabs 
, Vaginal swabs should be examined immediately for the presence of spermatozoa 

• Genital injuries may be minimum or absent - if the woman is an adult and a deflorate woman used to 
sexual intercourse 

• In very young girls(< 8 years) - hymen is deeply situated, and the vagina is too narrow for penetration, 
hymen is usually intact. Severe bruising of labia may be seen. Lacerations of the fourchette and 

perineum may be seen

SPECIMEN TO BE COLLECTED FOR LABORATORY INVESTIGATIONS 
• Clothes 
• Hair 

,. Few scalp hairs cut close to root 
,. Pubic hair combings, Avulsed pubic hair. 
,. Loose hair, present anywhere on the body. 
,. Matted pubic hair. 

• Blood for 
,. Blood grouping 
,, DNA profile 
,. Alcohol and drug detection 
,. Venereal diseases 

• Swabs 
,. From bite marks for saliva 
,. Vaginal swabs and washings of posterior fornix 
, Urethral swabs for venereal disease 

• Scrapings 
,. Dried blood stains 
,. Dried seminal stains 
',, Fingernail scrapings - for blood and tissue of the accused 

• Foreign bodies present anywhere on the body 
• Blood for grouping, serology, alcohol 
• Urine for drug screening and alcohol 
• Sanitary tampons if worn after intercourse. 
• All the materials should be collected, air-dried, stored in appropriate containers, sealed, labelled and 

despatched to the Forensic Science Laboratory

Genital examination: 
• Hymen - completely destroyed. 
• Vaginal orifice - dilated. 
• Mucous membrane - wrinkled and thickened with complete loss of rugosity. 
• Complete penetration in such women may occur without any evidence except presence of seminal fluid. 
• Absence of injuries in such women does not rule out penetration .

WILL VS co 'SENT FO'R SE"UAL It ~rncouRSE· 
• Will is the psychological desire and abil ity to do something that you intend. 

Consent is the permission or acceptance to do someth ing. 
If either w ill or consent or both are absent, sexual intercourse is considered as rape 

• Both will and consent absent: An accused person kidnaps a lady and has forceful intercourse with her 
• Will absent; Consent present: Employer asks sexual favour from a lady employee to get promotion. She 

accepts (consents) unwillingly (will absent) . Amounts to rape. 
W ill present; Consent absent: An illiterate woman who does not have a child years after marriage comes 
to a priest. Priest says she can have children by having intercourse w ith him. For the desire of children, 
she accepts (will present ). But consent is obtained by misconception Amounts to rape.

~ sEXUAL OFFENCES 

S 377 IPC' 
• S 377 IPC deals with unnatural sexual offences. Sexual intercourse against the order of nature is a crime. 

• On 06/09/ 2018, Supreme Court decriminalized a part of S 377 IPC 
• The Supreme court ruled that Consensual unnatural sex (Lesbian, Gay, Anal coitus, Buccal coitus) 

between persons above 18 years is not a crime ) 

• But Section 377 remains in force relating to sex w ith MINORS, NON-CONSENSUAL sexual acts and 
BESTIALITY.

• Necrophilia 

• Troilism • Trafficking 
• Necrophagia 

• Frotteurism • Indecent assault 
• Fetishism 

E_)(AMINATION OF_THE ACCUSED IN A CASE OF ALLEGED RAPE 
• Examination conducted under Sections 53 & 54 CrPC (Cl 51,52 BNSS) 
• The examination should be ca rried out without delay because minor injuries may faoe rapidly 

Preliminaries 
• Requisition for examination of accused from the Invest igating Officer/Magistrate 

• Identification of the accused by tlie police and accompanying person 

• Consent: informed consent from the accused or guardian (if he is < 18 years) 

• Brief history about. the incident should be recorded 

General examination 
• Genera l behaviour and the mental state of the person should be observed 

• Clothing: to be examined for disarrangement, damage, tears, loss of buttons, stains et c. 

• Collect stains (blood, semen, saliva, urine) if present on the clothes 

• Nail scrapings should be examined for the presence of epithelial cells 

• Injuries on the body (inflicted by the victim} - abrasions, bruises, nail marks, bite marks, avulsion of 

scalp hair should be noted 

GENITAL EXAMINATION 
• Developmental state of the external genitalia t o be noted 

• Injuries may be present on the external genitalia due to victim's resistance to prevent penetration 

• Forceful penetration into a non-lubricated vagina causes tears in the frenulum 

• Dried blood or seminal stains may be present on the external genitalia 

• Matted hairs may be seen 

• Smegma under the prepuce 
,. Smegma is a thick cheesy secretion with a disagreeable odour, containing desquamated 

epithelial cells and smegma bacilli (Mycobacterium smegmatis) 
,. Smegma usually gets rubbed off during sexual intercourse and it requires 24 hours to for again 

,. Absence of smegma is not conclusive evidence of sexual intercourse as it can be cleaned during 

bath 
,. Presence of smegma is evidence that the person did not have sexual intercourse or complete 

penetration within 24 hours

9 A 16 year old girl was brought to the hospital with complaints of vague lower abdominal pain. On examination, 

the doctor finds that she is pregnant with 8 weeks. In such case, 

a) What is the legal responsibility of the doctor? 

b) If the parents of the girl do not wish to pursue the matter legally, what should the doctor do? 

c) How can the doctor help the investigation officer in collecting appropriate evidence in th is case? 

a) LEGAL RESPONSIBILITY OF THE DOCTOR: 

• Legal responsibility of the doctor is to report to the police 

• A 16 year year old girl becoming pregnant - comes under aggravated penetrative sexual assault of 

POCSO act. (Refer to Short note Q. No. 14) 

b) PARENTS OF THE GIRL DO NOT WISH TO PURSUE THE MATTER LEGALLY: 

• Even though the parents do not want ot pursue the matter legal! it is the responsibili ty of the doctor to 

report to the police 

• If the doctor fails to report, as per POCSO act, the punishment is 6 months imprisonment 

c) How can the doctor help the investigation officer in collect ing appropriate evidence in this case? 

Refer to Short note Q. No. 14) 

SHORT NOTES: 

1. ACQUAINTANCE RAPE 

• Acquaintance Rape is rape that involves people who know or are familiar with each other . 

Acquaintance rape is the most common type of rape . 
• 
• However, it is less li kely to be reported than stranger rape . 

• Acquaintance rape is a broad category that includes all rapes except those perpetrated by people 

previously unknown to the rape victim. 

• Common types 
,. Date rape 

,, Marital rape - when husband attempts sexual intercourse with wife against her will/consent 

• Examples of acquaintances include someone the victim is dating, a classmate, co-worker, employer, 

family member, spouse, counselor, therapist, religious official or medical doctor. 

When rapes involve college students, the t erm "campus rape" is sometimes used . 
•

s. a) Genital examination findings in a virg in rape victim•

RAPE ON VIRGIN 
General examination 

• Gait - the girl walks with difficulty because of pain 
• Signs of struggle_ usually absent in children because they eit her don' t know what is happening or they 

are unable to offer resistance 
Genital examination 

• Attempt to separate the thighs may cause severe pain. Anaesthesia (general/local) may be requ ired for 
examination 

• Labia majora and minora: tender, red and inflamed 

• Hymen: 
,- Rupture of hymen occurs with the first intercourse which is the main evidence of rape in virgin. 
,- Tears due to penile penetration extends up to the margins; several tears point to first 

intercourse. 
,. Tears usually occurs posteriorly on the sides, in 4° or 8° clock position 
,- Soon after the incident, the margins of the torn hymen are sharp and red w hich bleed on touch 
,- After 3-4 days, edges are congested & swollen; by 1 week, they get healed but they do not unite 
, In very young girls(< 8 years) - hymen is deeply situated, and the vagina is too narrow for 

penetration, hymen is usually intact. Severe bruising of labia may be seen. Lacerations of the 
fourchette and perineum may be seen 

• Vaginal canal: bruising, tears, bleeding may be seen 
• Fourchette - tears during first intercourse 
• Posterior commissure: lacerated in children 
• Perinea! lacerations may be seen in children 

Diagram: Refer to Essay Q. No. 1 

6. RAPE ON DEFLORATE WOMAN 
• In deflorate women, even without childbirth 

, Hymen is completely destroyed 
, Vaginal or ifice is di lated 

, The mucous membrane is thickened and wrinkled. 
• Complete penetration of penis can occur in these women without any local signs or injuries 
• Only evidence for sexual intercourse is the presence of spermatozoa in the vagina 
• If the woman offers resistance, local injury may be seen in the vulva like small abrasions and light bruising 
• Signs of struggle may be evident like bruising over forearms, shoulders, buttocks, nai l marks etc .

7. TWO FING~R TEST: AN ILL_9GICAL,J_!,LEGAL_AND UNSCIENTIFIC TEST. COMMENT ON lj 

• T.h e.  two-finger te_s t'  carried out b Ya med 1' cal p-rac-titio-ner-, inc-lud-es a v-aginal exam•in ati·o n o f a II ege d rape 
v1ct1ms to_  check 1f she is sexual! Y act ·1 ve or not by penetration of fingers. 

• The medical practitioner inserts t wo 1·in gers .i nto the vagina of a rape survivor to f-ind ·1 f the hy men ·is  t orn 
or not; and also to test the laxity of the vagina. 

• If the vaginal is lax and freely admits two f ingers, along with ruptured (healed) hymen t he woman is 
considered as " habituated to sex." 

Illogical 

• The two-finger test is based on the incorrect assumption that a sexually active woman cannot be raped . 

• A woman's sexual history is wholly immaterial while adjudicating whether the accused raped her.  
Illegal 

• This test brings back the trauma of a woman in rape cases. 

• The medica l evidence of past intercourse is used to cast doubt on the rape allegation, either to suggest a 
survivor lied about the rape, to imply t hat the rape was not harmful, or to suggest the moral impropriety 
of the survivor and therefore her lar.k of entitlement to justice. 

• On a moral and ethical level, the test is a clear violation of privacy and dignity, where the woman is 
subjected to the pain of insertion even after going through a horrific experience. 

Unscientific 
• The test has no scientific backing and does not provide any definite information. 

• The test thrives on the archaic concept of a hymen determining whether a woman is sexually act ive or 

not . 
• Hymen can also rupture due to regular physical activit ies and exercises, and not necessarily because of 

sexual intercourse. 
• In children, the hymen being located deep inside vagina stays intact during penetrat ion and sexual 

assault. 
• Laxity of the vaginal muscle also depends on the psychological state of a person. For example, in the case 

of a distressed person, like a rape survivor, the muscle will appear to be tensed . 

Court rulings: 
• In 2013, the Supreme Court, stated that the test "violates the right of rape survivors to privacy, physical 

and mental integrity and dignity under Article 21 of Indian Constitution. 
• In 2018 the United Nations Human Rights, UN Women, & the WHO also called for a ban on the two-f inger 

I 

test so as to eliminate violence against women and stated it as medically not mandatory. 

• In 2022, the Madras High Court directed the state to ban the two-finger test. 
• Again in 2022, the Supreme Court of India reiterated its opposition to the use of the 't wo-finger test ' in 

rape and sexual assault cases.

,. Examination shall be made only by, or under the supervision of, a female registered medical

l4. e~cso ACT] 
ffiOTECTION OF CHILD FROM SEXUAL OFFENCES (POCSO) ACT, 2012 

• To effectively address the heinous crimes of sexual abuse and sexual exploitation of children through 
less ambiguous & more stringent legal provisions, the POCSO Act, was introduced in 2012. (Amended in 
2019) 

• Provides protection to all children (both male:, and females) from sexual abuse. 
• The act defines a chi ld as a person below 18 years of age 
• It defines different forms of sexual abuse 

,., Penetrative sexual assault 

,,, Aggravated penetrative Sexual assault 
,,, Sexual assault 
-,,., Aggravated sexual assault. 
,., Sexual harrassment 
-,,., Use of child for Pornography 

PENETRATIVE SEXUAL ASSAULT: 

• If he penetrates his penis to any extent into the vagina, mouth, urethra or anus of a chi ld or makes the 
child to do so with him or any other person or 

• He inserts any object to any extent or a part of the body, not being t he penis, into the vagina, urethra or 
anus of a child or makes the child to do so with him or 3ny other person o• 

• Manipulates any part of the body of the chi ld so as to cause penetration into the vagina, urethra or anus 
or any part of the body of a chi ld or makes t he chi ld to do so with him or any other person 

AGGRAVATED PENETRATIVE SEXUAL ASSAULT: when the offence is committed by 

• Penetrative sexual assault committed by a police officer or public servant, 
• The management or staff of jail, remand home or other place of custody or care 

• The management or staff of a hostpital (both private and government) 

• The management or staff of an educational or religious institution 

• A relative or a person who is living in the same or share household with the chi ld 

• Gang penetrative sexual assault 
• Offence causes child to become physically or mentally disabled or infects the child with HIV or any other 

life threatening disease or infection 
• PP.netrative sexual assault causing grievous hurt or injury to the sexual organs of the child; 

• If t he child is mentally or physically disabled 

• If the child is< 12 yea rs of age 
• If the child becomes pregnant as a consequence of sexual assault 

• If the child is already pregnant 
• If the assault results in death of chi ld or 
• If the assau lt committed during a natural calamity 

SEXUAL ASSAULT: 
• Wth sexual intent touches the vagina, penis, anus or breast of the child or makes the child touch the 

vagina, peni s, anus or breast of such person or any other person 

AGGRAVATED SEXUAL ASSAULT: 
• Sexual assault done by a police officer, armed forces or security forces, public servant, management or 

st aff or jai l, hospital, educational or religious institution

Medical definition of abortion: expulsion of products of conception weighing 500 g or 
less, when it is not capable of independent survival (corresponds to gestational age 22 weeks) 

Legal definition of abortion: expulsion of products of conception from uterus any time before full term

Following points must be proved to convict the abortionist of criminal abortion 
1) The dead woman was pregnant 

2) The accused was responsible for the act which resulted in abortion 
3) The accused intended illegal abortion 
4) The death was due to abortion.

SHORT NOTES: 

1. tfHE MEDICAL TERMINATION OF PREGNANCY (MTP) (Amendment) ACT, 2021 
• The MTP (Amendment) Act 2021 will increase the ambit and access of women to safe aborti on services. 
• It will ensure dignity, autonomy, confident iality and justice fo r w omen who need to terminate pregnancy. 
• The new law will contribute towards ending preventable maternal mortality to help meet 

the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 3.1 (reducing Maternal mortality ratio), 3. 7 and 5.6 
(universal access to sexual and reproductive health and rights) 

MTP up to 20 weeks of Gestation 
• Indications 

,, The continuation of pregnancy would involve the risk of the life of a pregnant woman or 
grave injury to her physical or mental health . 

,, There is a substantial risk that the child being born with serious physical or mental 
abnormality 

,. Failure of contraceptive method or device in a married or unmarried woman 
• The opinion of one Registered Medical Pract itioner (RMP) is required. 

MTP up to 24 weeks of Gestation can be done in the following cases: (with the opinion of two RMP ) 
• Survivors of sexual assault or rape or incest; 
• Minors 
• Change of marital status during the ongoing pregnancy (widowhood and divorce); 
• Women with physical disabilities; 
• Mentally ill women including intellectual disabilities. 

• Fetal malformation that has a substantial risk of being incompatible w ith life or if the child is born, he/ 
she may suffer from serious physical or mental abnormalities; and 

• Women with pregnancy in humanitarian settings or disaster or emergency situations. 
MTP beyond 24 weeks of Gestation j 

• Permission of the State-level medical board is essential after 24 weeks in case of substantial fetal \ 
abnormalities. ' 

• For _ter~inat'.on of pregnancies resulting from rape and gestation period is> 24 w eeks, t he only 
option Is to file a writ petition. 

Qualification of Doctors t o do MTP: 
• Qualification category: 

" A - RMP registered before 1971 and 3 years' experience in OBG 
8 

,,, - RMP with Six months internship in OBG or 1 year experience in OBG 
,,, C - Assisted 25 cases of MTP and 5 cases MTP independently 
,,, Ca - 3 months expe · · OB 

. . rience tn G or Independently performed 10 cases of MTP under t he 
superv1s1on of a RMP in a hospital. 

,,, D - Diploma or M . Sin OBG 

• Up to 9 weeks: (medical methods of abortion o nly) - A, 8, C, Ca and D 
• Up to 12 weeks: A, B, C, and o 
• 12 - 20 weeks: A, B, and D 

• Beyond 24 weeks: A, B, C, Ca and D (Opinion of 2 RM P's required)

I 

INSTRUMENTS 
• Rupturing t he membranes using Uterine sound, Catheter, Pencil, Hairpin, Stick, fingers etc. 
• Dilation of the cervix 

,. Using bark of slippery elm 
,. Soft pieces of wood of different sizes with 3mm thickness is passed into the cervix & left in situ. 
,. It absorbs moisture & vaginal secretions & swells up that dilates the cervical canal ➔ Abortion. 

• Abortion stick 
,. Thin wood/ bamboo stick, 12-18 cm long. 
, One end wrapped with cotton and soaked with juice of marking nut or Calotropis 
, Twig of some irritant plat like Calotropis, Nerium odorum, Cerebra thevetia can also be used 
,. When inserted into the uterus, it stimulates uterine contractions and results in abortion 

• Air insufflation: Air is instilled into vagina by means of syringes or pumps 
• Electricity 

, -ve pole is applied over the cervix. 
, +ve pole is applied over sacrum/ lumbar vessels. 
, Current is passed ➔ Uterine contraction➔ Abortion 

• Syringing 
, An enema syringe with a hard bulb is used to inject fluid into the uterus & the hard nozzle is 

inserted into the cervix. 
, A mixture of air & fluid is forced into uterine cavity at a higher pressure than uterine veins. 
, The fluid detaches parts of amniotic sac & placenta from the uterine walls. 
, The uterus contracts causing hemorrhage & thus abortion. 

• Dilatation and Curettage 
MECHANICAL VIOLENCE 

• Severe pressure over the abdomen - kicking, blows, tight belts, kneading 
• Violent exercise - horse riding, jumping from height, jolting 
• Cupping - a flaming light is placed on abdominal wall, covered by a mug or pot. Air expands due to heat 

and escapes. On cooling, the air contracts and the vessel sets tightly on the abdominal wall. When the 
vessel is pulled, it may separate the placenta. 

• Very hot & cold-water baths alternately

4. ECBOLICS 

Ecbolics are drugs that increase uterine contraction 

They cause uterine contraction but do not relax or dilate the cervical canal and external os, which is 
necessary to expel the foetus and thus they are dangerous 

c Usually, they are effective only near term 

• E.g.: ergot, quinine, lead, pituitary extract 

• Drugs: ergometrine, oxytocin, prostaglandins

19. INFANTICIDE & CHILD ABUSE 

Define and discuss infanticide, foeticide and stillbirth 
Describe and discuss signs of intrauterine death, signs of live birth, viability of foetus, age determination of 
foetus, Hydrostatic test, Sudden Infants Death syndrome and Munchausen's syndrome by proxy 

iiii!iiiiii!i~=- Describe and discuss child abuse and battered baby syndrome 

1 Define infanticide. Describe methods usually adopted for carrying out infanticide. Add a note on macerated 
fe tus. 

INFANTICIDE: 

• It is the deliberate, unlawful, killing of a child under the age of one year, by act of omission or act of 
commission 

e In India, there is no separate IPC section for infanticide 
• Legally infanticide amounts to homicide 

METHODS ADOPTED FOR CARRYING INFANTICIDE: 
Acts of Commission. 

,. nrowr>ing - the child may be thrown in a reservoir of water or tank (antemortem drowning) or killed by 
some other means and thrown in water (postmortem submersion) 

• Strangulation - manual or ligature. 
• Suffocation - smothering or gagging. 

• Choking - putting a paddy seed or any small object deep into the mouth o 
• Burns 

• Concealed puncture wound 0 
- caused by nail or needle through fontanelle 

• Head injury 

• Fracture dislocation of cervical vertebrae 
• Poisoning 

Acts of Omission: 

• Failure to clear the air passages which may be obstructed by amniotic fluid or mucus . 
• Not protecting child from heat or cold . 

• Not providing proper assistance during labor - death by suffocation or head injury.  
• Failure to provide the child with proper food . 

• Not ligating the umbilical cord after it is cut - death by hemorrhage . 

~A_!:ERATION 

• A process of aseptic autolysis 

• Occurs when the dead child remains in the uterus for about 3 - 4 days immersed in amniotic fluid but 
devoid of air . 

• Signs of maceration are not seen if the child is delivered within 12 hours after death 



INFANTICIDE & CHILD ABUSE 

• Signs of maceration 

,. Earl ic<,t sign. reddening of skin with peeling seen in 12 hrs after death in utero. 
,. Red/Purplish skin with large vesicles after 24 hours 
,.. Distended abdomen 
,. Flexible joints and bones 
,.. All internal organs become soft 
,.. Collapse of vertebral column 
,. Body is soft, flaccid and flattens when placed on the table 
,.. Spalding's sign: loss of alignment or overriding of skull bones due to shrinkage of cerebrum. A 

sign of fetal death 
,. Robert's sign: appearance of gas shadow in the heart chambers and aorta; occurs as early as 12 

hours, but very difficult to interpret 

2 A dead fetus was recovered from the dustbin. Crown heel length was 35 ems. Finger-nail marks with contusions 
were noted in the neck. Answer the following: 
a) Estimate the gestational age with reason 
b) Mention the ossification centers which are likely to be found in this case. 
c) Is this fetus viable or not? 
d) What is the cause of death? 

a) GESTATIONAL AGE: Gestational age can be determined from crown-heel length with the help of Rule of Hasse 
• Up to S months, age of the foetus = Square root of length of foetus (in months) 
• After S months, age of the fetus= length of the foet us (in cm)/5 = (months) 
• The CHL of the given fetus is 35 cm. 
• 35/5 = 7 months is the age of the fetus 

b) OSSIFICATION CENTERS THAT APPEAR IN INTRAUTERINE LIFE 
• First ossification centre at clavicle: 5-6 weeks 
• 2 months - Mandible Ossification centers likely to be found 
• 5 months - Calcaneus, Manubrium in this age are : clavicle, Mandible, 
• 6 months -1st piece of sternum Calcaneus, Manubrium, First and 
• 7 months - Talus, 2nd piece of sternum second piece of sternum and Talus . 
• 8 months - 3rd piece of sternum 
• 9 months - Cuboid, Lower end of femur 

c) VIABILITY: 
• The capability of the child to lead a separate existence after birth by virtue of certain degree of 

development is called viability 
• The acceptable age of viability is 210 days (7 months or 28 weeks) 
• As the age of the given fetus is 7 months, it is viable 
• The appearance of ossification centre of talus corresponds to viable age of the fetus. 

d) CAUSE OF DEATH: Finger-nail marks with contusions were noted in t he neck of the child which points towards 
ll1anual strangulation or throttling (Refer Chapter 13. Essay Q. No. 8)

VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

3 Differentiate between still birth and live birth. Describe the features of live birth. 

LIVE BIRTH 
• A Child is considered live born if it shows signs of life even when only part of the child was out of the 

mother's reproductive passage, though the child may not have breathed or completely born 

STILL BIRTH 
• The child, who is born after 28th week of pregnancy, and did not breath or show any signs of life, at any 

time after completely born is ca lled a still born child 
• The child was alive in utero, but dies during the process of birth 

SIGNS OF LIVE BIRTH 

EXTERNAL SIGNS 
Shape of the chest 

• Before respiration 
,. Shape: flat 

,. Circumference: 1-3cm less than abdominal circumference 
• After respiration 

,. Shape: arched or drum shaped 
,. Circumference: 1-3cm more than abdominal circumference 

Vernix caseosa 

• Present in the body surface of a newborn child 

• Mainly seen over axil la, folds of neck, inguinal region, buttocks 

• It is either cleaned by wa shing or gets itself removed in 1-2 days in a live born 

Changes in the Umbilical cord 

• In live births, the free end of the umbilical cord attached with the baby shows clean cut margins and 
presence of a ligature 

• Blood clots in the cut end - 2 hours 

• Cord shrinks and dries - 24 hours. 

• Inflammatory ring seen - 36 to 48 hours. 
• Umbilical cord falls off - 5 to 6 days. 

• Complete hea ling of the umbilicus - 10 to 12 days 

Caput succedaneum 

• It is a soft swelling seen over the presenting part of the head 

• It is formed due to compression around the presenting part during delivery causing extravasation and 
diffusion of blood and accumulation of fluid in the subcutaneous tissue 

• It is a definite sign of live birth 

Cephalohematoma 

• Localized accumulation of blood deep to the scalp, between the bone and the periosteum 
• Its presence also suggests live birth

c) MEDICOLEGAL ISSUES: 
The following medicolegal questions needs to be answered. 

• Was the child still born or dead-born? 

• Was the child live born? 

• If born alive, how long did the child live? 

• What was the cause of death? 
The alleged mother should be examined for 

• Signs of recent delivery 
• Her mental condition. 

SHORT NOTES: 

1. DEAD BORN FOETUS* 
• A dead born foetus is the one which has died in utero and shows one of the following signs 

,. Rigor mortis 
,. Maceration 
,. Mummification 
,. Putrefaction 

RIGOR MORTIS 
• Rigor mortis occurs only after 2-3 hours of death 
• Hence if it is present in the muscles of the foetus, it indicates the child is dead few hours before birth 

MACERATION: Refer to Essay Q. No. 1 

MUMMIFICATION 
• When the dead fetus is retained in the uterus for many days and if there is scarcity or absence of 

amniotic fluid 
• No air entry and sterile condition 
• Indicates death before long time (> 2 weeks) 

PUTREFACTION 
• When the dead fetus is retained in the uterus and if the membranes are ruptured, sings of putrefaction 

appear in the fetus

6 HYDROSTATIC TEST or RAYGAT'S TEST***** 
Principle: 

• Specifi c gravity of the lungs decreases after breathing 
• Specific gravity before respiration : 1040 to 1050 
• Specif ic gravity after respiration : 940 

• As the specific gravity decreases, respired lungs float in water 
Procedure 

• A ligature is tied on the bronchi and lungs separated. 

• Each lung is individually placed on water . 

• If t hey fl oat , each lung is cut into 12 - 20 pieces and then placed on w ater . 
• A small piece of liver is kept as control. 

• If this liver also floats the test is of no value. 
• If the lung pieces still fl oat, they are each squeezed in between the thumb and index finger under surface 

of water to see if any bubbles of air escape 
• If they still f loat, they are taken out of water, wrapped in piece of cloth and squeezed by putt ing weight 

to remove t he tidal air. 

• The pieces are again placed on w ater 

• If they cont inue to float because of residual air, it indicates respi ration has t aken place . 
• If they sink after pressure, respiration has not t aken place . 
• If some sinks and some floats, feeble respiration has taken place .

g, sHAi<ENBAsvsvNDROME 
• Also called Infantile Whiplash Syndrome 

• The infant is held by thorax and shaken, causing repetitive acceleration-deceleration trauma 
• Because the children's head is relatively heavy, and the neck muscles are weak, shaking to and fro causes 

severe injuries 

• Injuries: paravertebral rib fracture, intracranial injuries, eye injuries 
• lntracranial injuries - diffuse axonal injury, subdural or subarachnoid hemorrhage 
• lntracranial injuries are produced without obvious external injuries 
• Permanent brain damage may occur due to habitual prolonged shaking of the child . 
• Eye injuries: retinal detachment, vitreous hemorrhage 
• Bruises are seen on the arms, hands, with traction lesions of periosteum of long bones

9. [HILD ABUSE/CHILD MALTREATMEN] 
• Child maltreatment is the abuse and neglect that occurs to children under 18 years of age. 
• It includes all types of physical and/or emotional ill-t reatment, sexual abuse, neglect, negligence, 

exploitation, which results in actual or potential harm to the child's health, survival, development or 

dignity 
• Child maltreatment is a global problem with serious life-long consequences 
• International studies reveal that 20% of women and 5-10% of men report being sexually abused as 

chi ldren, while 25-50% of all children report being physically abused 
Child maltreatment can be distinguished in to: 

• Physical abuse_ physical injury by hitting, beating, shaking, biting, scalding, burning, kicking, poisoning and 

suffocating. 
• Sexual abuse _ fondling, rape, and exposing a child to other sexual activities. 
• Emotional and psychological abuse - behaviors that harm a child's self-worth or emotional well-being such 

as shaming, rejection, withholding love, and threatening. 
• Neglect - the failure to meet a child's basic needs. 



--- - ---------- - ---~- ~--~ 
VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

Maltreated children are at increased ri·s k for behav,·o ra I,  p h ys,·c a I an d me ntal health problems such as; 

• Perpetrating or being a victim of violence 
• Depression 

• Smoking 

• Obesity 

• High-risk sexual behaviors 
• Unintended pregnancy 
• Alcohol and drug misuse 
• Via these behavioral and mental health consequences, maltreatment can contribute to heart disease, 

cancer, suicide and sexually transmitted infections.

10. SUDDEN INFANT DEATH SYNDROME 

• Synonyms: Crib death, Cot death, SIDS 

• SIDS is defined as the sudden and unexpected death of seemingly healthy infant, whose death remains 
unexplained even after thorough death scene investigation, case investigation, review of clinical 
history and a complete autopsy. 

RISK FACTORS . 
Maternal and antenatal factors Infant factors 

• Smoking • Age (peak 2-4 months) 
• Alcohol abuse • Male gender 

~ • Drug abuse (cocaine, heroin) • Prematurity 
• Nut ritional deficiency • Prone and side sleep position 
• Low socioeconomic status • Recent febrile illness 

V • Younger age • Smoking exposure (prenatal and 
• Single marital status postnatal) 
• Intrauterine hypoxia • Colder season, no central heating 

Theories of causation of SIDS 

• Prolonged Sleep apnea 

• Respiratory infection leading to nasal edema and mucus secretion . 
• Laryngeal spasm . 

• Vomited milk-based materials on t he mattress may get infect ed by Staphylococcus aureus and when this 
gets transmitted to the child, may lead to anaphylactic shock and sudden death. 

• Cow's milk allergy 

• Nutritional deficiencies: Calcium, selenium, biotin, magnesium, Vitamins C, D, E 

Autopsy Findings 

• Usually negative autopsy findings 

• Milk or blood-stained froth at mouth and nostri ls. 

• The only constant findings are multiple petechial hemorrhages on the visceral surface of the heart, lungs 
and thymus which are agonal in nature. 

• In a few, some pathologica l causes can be seen such as pneumonia, congenital heart diseases, tracheo­
bronchitis, etc.

~ 
INFANTICIDE & CHILD ABUSE 

ll, MUNCHAUSEN SYNDROME BY PROXYJ 

• A type of chi ld abuse, in which children are brought to doctors for induced signs or symptoms of illness 
with fic titious history. 

• The child is admitted frequently in the hospitals for non-existing conditions 
Methods of simulation of disease 

• Mother may prick the child's finger and adds blood to urine of the child and takes the sample to doctor. 

• Child's nose is closed with two finger and lower jaw is pushed up with palm to block airways. 

• A pillow may be put on the face of the child and then pushed onto the bed. 

• She may give insulin to the child and take him to the doctor for hypoglycemia. 
• She may also give emetics, laxatives, psychotropic drugs, CNS depressants, etc. 

Diagnosis 
• Illness produced or alleged or both by a parent 

• Repeated requests for medical care of a child leading to multiple medical procedures 
• Parental denial of knowledge of the cause of symptoms 

• Regression of symptoms when t~e child is separated from the parents

SHORT NOTES: 

1. CRIME SCENE INVESTIGATION 

• Criminal investigation in Ind ia is most ly conducted by police investigators . 

• The investigators are one of the first persons t o vi sit scene of crimes & to plan & decide upon further 
course of act ion 

• Presently, scene of crime investigation t eams in India do not have a medical m an (Med ic.a -legal 
expert/Forensic Pathologist/General Medical Pract it ioner) as a member barring few except ions. 

• Not surprisingly, therefore invest igatiMs usually lack quality in terms of interpretation of medico-legal 
complexities 

Objectives of Crime scene visit 

• To establish what happened (crime scene reconstruction) 
• To identify the responsible person 

• Collection, preservation, packaging, transportation, and documentation of physical evidence leh at 
the crime scene 

Importance of Crime scene visit by Doctor 

• Provides an opportunity to gain first -hand knowledge of vita l ci rcumstanti al evidences needed to 
interpret postmortem findings, to answers queries regard ing t ime, cause and manner of death, time, 
mechanism & nature of injuries 

• M inimizes misinterpretat ion of facts due to introduct ion of Artefacts due t o passage of t ime. 
• Best opportunity for interaction & liaison between various experts involved in t he case i.e. Investigating 

Officer, Forensic Scientist etc. 

• Judiciously select biological evidences to be collected in order to achieve maxim um uti lity 
Duties and Responsibilities of the Doctor on Crime scene 

• Check for any clin ical sign of life to be certain about death 
• If the person's death is so imminent and if the person happens to be able to comm unicate, dying 

declaration must be recorded. 

• A doctor is not supposed to touch or alter anything until the same has been identified, documented & 
photographed 

• Documentation of the crime scene : note taking, videography, photography, and sketch ing . 
• General observations about the scene, any evidence of struggle 

• Presence of drag marks or shifting of body from some other place must be noted . 

• Identification of the diseased 



I► T RACE EVIDENCE & FORENSIC SCIENCE 

• A systematic, thorough inspection and evaluation of the deceased 
" Description of clothing & signs of struggle/assault, stains, fibers/hairs or foreign objects fou nd therein. 

Description of rigor mortis, hypostasis, signs of decomposition etc. for estimation of time since death. 

, Presence or absence of defence wounds in hands/forearms in assault cases 
• Collection of evidences 

• Interviewing persons regarding the death 

Est imating the post-mortem interval at the scene 

: . BIOLOGICAL SPECIMEN COLLECTION 

Blooc'. 

• In all medico legal investigation cases, blood specimen should be obtained when available. 

• It is used as a reference sample for identification in unidentified cases and also for toxicological analysis . 

• In all suspected poisoning deaths or in all cases of unknown causes of death a femoral blood specimen 
should be collected. 

• Preservative : Sodium Fluoride (preservative)+ potassium oxalate (anticoagulant) 
Urine 

• Ur ine specimen is of great value in screening of unknown drug or poison, particularly substance of abuse 

since the concentrations are generally higher than in blood 

Also valuable in the quantitative analysis of alcohol, where there is uncertainty over the validity of a blood 

specimen. 
• Preservative: Thymol or saturated saline 

Bile 
• Bile is helpful in estimating the drugs, which are concentrated by liver and excreted into the gall bladder 

li ke opiates and acetaminophen (paracetamol) . 

• It is not routinely preserved, but only in selected cases. 
• It can be collected directly by incising the gall bladder into a glass bottle. 

Vitreouc; Humor 
• The vitreous humor specimen is particularly useful for alcohols, or in diabetes and insulin related deaths. 

• It is also very useful where the body has decomposed. T 
• It res ist s putrefaction longer than other body fluids as it is sterile and remains well protected in the eye. 

• Vitreous humor must be collected from both eyes in separate vials of 10 ml. It is preserved with sodium 

fluoride (10 mg/ml). 
Cerebrosp·nal fluid 

If needed it should be collected by cisternal puncture 

• The CSF sample has to be preserved in sodium fluoride. 

Other body fluids 
• h bl d nd ur·ine are not available other available body fluids like pericardia! and synovial 

1n  cases w ere oo a 
fluids can be used for toxi cological analysis like alcohol.

Phenolphthalein test (Kastle-Meyer test) 
• A drop of phenolphthalein reagent is added to the sample and then a drop of hydrogen peroxide 
• Positive reaction is indicated by pink color 

O-toluidine test 
• A drop of toluidine reagent is added to the sample 
• Positive reaction is indicated by the development of blue to green color 

Leucomalach1te green test 
• A drop of Leucomalachite green reagent is dissolved in water along with sodium perborate 
• Positive reaction is indicated by blue green color 

•· Washed blood stains can be readily detected by UV light. 

CONFIRMATORY TESTS 
Teichmann test (Haemin crystal test) 

• A small crystal of sodium chloride and 2-3 drops of glacial acetic acid are placed on a small piece of 
suspected stain on a glass slide 

• A coverslip is placed over it and the slide is heated and examined under microscope 
• Yellowish red or brownish black rhombic crystals of hem in or hematin chloride are seen if the suspected 

stain is blood. 
Takayama test (Haemochromogen crystal test) 

• A small piece of suspected material is kept on the glass slide and 2-3 drops of Takayama reagent is added 
and examined under microscope 

• Pink feathery crystals of Haemochromogen appear in few minutes 

Rhombic crystals of hem in Feathery crystals of Haemochromogen

Microscopy ~ 
• Humans have biconcave, circular, non-nucleated red cells except in new-born 

Spectrophotometry (Spectroscopy) 

• Most reliable for detecting both recent and old stains . 
• Hemoglobin and its derivatives exhibit characteristic bands of absorption at specific wavelengths which 

can be observed by a spectrophotometer 

• Hemoglobin and its derivates show a common absorption band at 400-425nm, apart from one or more 
bands at their characteristic wavelengths 

• Hence, the presence of two or more absorption bands in the spectroscope confirms the presence of 
blood in the sample

SPECTROSCOPY or HIMOGLOOIN DERIVATIVES 

• Oxyhemoglobin: two bands between Fraunhofer lines D and E 

• Reduced Hemoglobin: one broad band between D and E 

• Carboxyhemoglobin: simi lar to oxyhemoglobin, but rema ins unchanged after the addition of ammonium 

sulphide, while oxyhemoglobin becomes reduced hemoglobin 

• Methemoglobin: similar to oxyhemoglobin, with a th ird band between C and D and a fourth band 

betw een E and F 

TO FIND OUT WHETHER THE STAIN IS HUMAN OR NOT: 

• Precipitin test (Anti-human globulin inhibition test) 

• Agar gel diffusion (Ouchterlony method) 

• Agar gel electrophoresis 

6. MEDICO-LEGAL APPLICATION OF BLOOD GROUPS** 

Crime investigations 
• Blood stains may be present on the clothing of an accused person can reveal the identity of the vict im 

• Blood stains may be present under the fingernail s of the accused in case of throttling 

Disputed paternity 
• A blood group antigen cannot appear in a child unless present in one of the parents 

• If an individual is homozygous for a blood group antigen, it must appear in the blood of all his children 

• Blood grouping may exclude a certain person as the possible father of the child 

• But it cannot establish paternity definitely 

• E.g. : if the blood group of the child is AB, and if t he mother' s group is A, and if two men are alleged to be 

the father of the child, one with O group and the other with B group, the person with B group must be 

the father 
Disputed maternity 

• When there is accidental exchange of new-borns at hospitals 

• When two women claim the same chi ld 

• Suppositious child 

• In case of kidnapped child 

Grouping from body fluids 
• Blood group antigens can be demonstrated in the stains of semen, sweat, saliva, nasal secret ions etc. 

Identity 

• A person with a rare blood group may be identified based on blood group.

7. CHEMICAL EXAMINATION FOR SEMINAL FLUIDS! 
Florence test . 

• The stain is ext racted, dried on a glass slide and covered with a coverslip. 
A drop of Florence solution is added. 

• If semen is present, dark brown rhombic crystals arranged in clusters or rosettes of rholine per iodide 
appear immediately. 
Detects - Choline, which originates from seminal vesicles. 
A positive test is not proof of seminal fluid but confirms the presence of some vegetable o r animal 
substance. 

e A negative react ion proves that the stain is not semen. 
Barberio's test 

• A saturated solution of picric acid is added to dried stain extract on a glass slide covered w ith a coverslip, 
• If spermine (from prostatic secretions) is present, it produces yellow needle-shaped crystals of spermine 

picrate. 
Acid hosphatase test 

• Acid phosphatase activi ty is 500- 1000 times greater in human semen t han in any other bodily f lu id. 
• The levels remain high until till 40 years of age with gradual decrease thereafte r 
• Reagent is prepared by adding a-naphthyl and Brentamine fast blue B 
• Reagent is added to a moist sterile cotton swan with the suspected stain 
• Development of intense purple color within 2 minutes indicates the presence of semen 

Prostate specific antigen (PSA or p30) 
• The glycoprotein p30 is derived from prostate and found in seminal plasma, male urine and blood 
• P30 can be detected by elect rophoresis 

Conf irmatory tests fo Seminal stains . 
• Gold standard - demonstration of spermatozoa by Microscopy 
• Most commonly used - staining with HE or Christmas tree stain 
• Immunological methods 

o Seminal vesicle specific antigen (SVSA) detection - highly specific 
o Prostate specific antigen (PSA) detection 

❖ Invisible Seminal stains can be detected by UV light - gives blue-white fluorescence. 

8. LOCARO'S PRINCIPLE OF EXCHANGE! 
• When any two objects come into contact, there is always a transfer of material from one object to the 

other. 

• This is called Locard's exchange principle . 
• When a criminal commits a crime, traces from the crime scene may be carried away by the criminal or 

tools of the criminal. 

• Traces of the criminal or the tools of the criminal may also be left at the crime scene . 
• Detection of such evidence constitutes trace evidence (physical evidence) 

This physical evidence proves a link between the crime and the cr,·mm· aI.  
• Examples of physical evidence - fingerprints, footprints, hair, fibres of clothes, broken glass, blood stains, 

seminal stains etc. 



►~ n tACE EVIDENCE & FORENSIC SCIENCE X 
Applications 

• In rape cases, semen, blood, pubic hair, etc. of the rapist can be found on the victim . Vaginal epithel ial 

cells, pubic hair, b lood of the victim can be found on the rapist 

• In murder cases, blood of the victim can be detected on the weapon and on the assailant 

9. pOLYGRAPW (LIE DETECTOR) 

• Polygraph - t he process used in medical practice for comprehensive study of functioning of different 

body systems with part icular reference to circulation, respiration and peripheral nervous system 

• Principle: mental excitation or stimulation causes alteration of the body functions due to sympathetic 

excitation 

• These alterat ions in body functions can be recorded by an instrument called polygraph 

• Polygraph measures and records physiological responses, while the person is asked a series of questions 

• The parameters recorded are 
Blood pressure 
Pulse 
Respiration 
Skin conductivity (Electrodermal Response-EDR or Galvanic skin response-GSR) 

• When a person lies, due to sympathetic excitation, alterations in the responses occur which are 

recorded. 
Procedure 

• The questions will be suggestive in nature. 

• The person has to answer either yes or no. 

• Three types of questions are asked. 
,- Irrelevant questions (not related to the incident) 

,- Relevant questions (related to t he incident) 

, Control questions 

10. NARCO-ANAL YSIS or TRUTH SERUM TEST* 

• It is the procedure of investigation of a person after appl ication of an anaesthetic drug 

• Principle: at a point very close to unconsciousness, the subject would be mentally incapable of resistance 

to questioning and incapable to conceal his guilt 

• Drug commonly administered, 

, Sodium amytal 
, Thiopentone sodium (truth serum) 

,- Scopolamine hydrobromide 
• The subject goes to hypnotic or semiconscious state . 

• The answ ers are spontaneous, and the person is unable to manipulate t he answers. 

• Wrong dose can cause coma or death of the person . 

• M adras High Court (in 2006) and Bombay High Court (In 2004) ruled that subjecting of accused to 

narcoanalysis tests does not violate fundamental rights. 

• In 20l0, Supreme court of India opined that narco-analysis cannot be forced on any suspect.

11. BRAIN MAPPING* or BRAIN FINGER PRINTING 
• Introduced by Dr. Lawrence Farewell 
• A technique that measures recognition of familiar stimuli by measuring electrical brain wave responses 

to words, phrases or pictures related to a particular event 
• It is based on the theory that the suspect's reaction to the details of an event or activity will reflect if the 

suspect had prior knowledge of the event or activity. 
• Sensors are attached to the subject's head. 
• The subject is snown pictures or made to hear sounds related to crime. 
• If his brain contains the information - it responds by a specific pattern in EEG. 
• P300 waves and MERMER (memory encoding related multifaceted electro-encephalographic response) 

indicates the person's knowledge about the event . 
• It does not depend upon emotions. 
• It is 100% accurate .

1, Legal and ethical issues in relation to stem cell research 

• Stem cell therapy has shown immense potential treatment of diseases traditionally considered 

"degenerative, incurable and irreversible" such as diabetes, heart disease, spinal cord injuries, 

Parkinson' s, Alzheimer's disease 

• However, stem cell research has proved something of a political, ethical, social and legal minefield, 

creating challenges for regulatory bodies, policy makers and scientists as they traverse their way t hrough 

a tangled web of regulations and moral proselytizing 

STEM CELLS 

• Stem cells are a class of undifferentiated cells that are able to differentiate into specialized cell types. 

• Possess two important properties 

, Self-renewal: ability to go through numerous cycles of cell division while maintaining 

undifferentiated state 

, Pluori-Potency: capacity to differentiate into special ized cell types 

• Sources: Embryo, cord blood, the fetus or the adult cells 

Types 
• Totipotent (omnipotent) cells - can form an entire organism. E.g. : Zygote 

• Pluripotent stem cells -can differentiate into cells derived from any of the three germ layers E.g. Embryo 

(blastocyst) 

• Multipotent stem cells - can form multiple cell lineages but cannot form all cells of the body. E.g.: 

Hematopoietic stem cell 

ETHICAL ASPECTS OF EMBRYONIC STEM CELL RESEARCH 

• Adult stem ce lls and cord blood stem cells do not raise special ethica l concerns and are widely used in 

research and clinical care 

• Human embryonic stem cell (hESC) research is ethically and politically controversial because it involves 

the destruction of human embryos 

• Some people believe that an embryo is a person with the same moral status as an adult or a live-born 

child. From this perspective, taking a blastocyst and removing the inner cell mass to derive an embryonic 

stem cell line is t antamount to murder 

• A different view is that the embryo becomes a person in a moral sense at a later stage of development 
 

than fertilization

2. iBIOETHIC~ 
--. - Bioethics is the interdisciplinary study of ethical, social, and legal issues that arise in biomedicine and 

biomedical research . 
• Bioethics are concerned with the ethical questions that arise in the relationships among life sciences, 

biotechnology, medicine, politics, law, theology and philosophy 
• Bioethics includes 

,. medical ethics, which focuses on issues in health care; 
,, research ethics, which focuses issues on the conduct of research; 
,- environmental ethics, which focuses on issues pertaining to the relationship between human 

activities and the environment, and 
,. public health ethics, which addresses ethical issues in public health 
, Animal ethics 

• The field of bioethics has addressed a broad swathe of human inquiry, ranging from debates over the 
boundaries of life (e.g. abortion, euthanasia), surrogacy, the allocation of scarce health care resources 
(e.g. organ donation, health care rationing) to the right to refuse medical care for religious or cultu
re r

a a
s l 
ons. 

• The field of bioethics also extends to the applications of biotechnology, including genetic 
engineering, which affects humans, animals and plants, or to the impact of air, soil and wat

BIOMEDICAL RESEARCH 

3. HUMAN EXPERIMENTATION 

• It is a branch of cli nical research that deals with the employment of human beings as specimens for 
research and investigation. 

• It involves sySt ematic and scientific investigation that can be either interventional (a "trial") or 
observat iona l (no "t est article") and involves human beings as research subject s. 
Human experimentation is a medical (clinica l) research which requires the willingness of specimens and 

unwilling experimentation can be considered as "medical torture." 
• The Nuremberg Code says Human experimentation is justified only if its results benefit society and it is 

carried out in accord with basic principles that "sat isfy moral, ethica l, and legal concept s. 

• The World Medical Association (WMA) has developed the Declaration of Helsinki as a statement of 
ethical principles for medical research involving human subjects. 

• Research should be designed to reduce the risks of potential harm while ensuring anticipated benefits are 
achieved . This principle is in line with the Hippocratic Oath that states, "First, do no harm." 

• The purpose of medical research can never take precedence over the rights and interests of individual 
research subjects. 

• All medical research that involves humans as subjects must be conducted by individuals with the requisite 
scientific and ethical education and training 

• During the research, the physician-scientists should ensure minimal potentia l harm to the environment. 
• If the patient suffers harm, they should receive appropriate treatment and compensation if applicable . 

4. Discuss the constitution and functions of ethical committees 

Composition of Institutional Ethics Committee 
1. Chairperson (will not be affi liated to the Institute) 
2. Member Secretary (from the same Institution) 
3. Institutional members to be included from various facu lt ies such as basic, cl inical, operational, statistics, 

genetics and social sciences. 
4. 1 to 2 persons from basic medica l science area 
s. 2 to 3 clinicians from various Institutes/Hospitals/Medical Colleges to include Gynecologist , clinical 

pharmacologist or any other specialty 

6. One legal expert 
7. 1 to 2 social scientist/ representative of non-governmental voluntary agency 
8. 2 community members (they could be clinic clients/non-scientific who can voice out t he concerns of the 

pot ential participants) 
Functions of Institutional Ethics Committee 

• The three primary functions commonly cited for an ethics committee are Education, Policy development 

and review, and Case analysis 
• IEC will review and approve all types of research proposals involving human participants with a view to 

safeguard the dignity, rights safety and well-being of all actual and potential research participants 
• It will ensure that universal ethical values and international scientific standards are fo llowed in t erms of 

local community values and customs 
• They also monitor studies once they begin and - if necessary - may take part in follow-up actions after 

the end of the research 
• IEC w ill only review the research proposals (clinical, b_asic research, genetic, socio-behavioral o r 

operational studies), which are conducted at the Inst itute 
• IEC is t o ensure a compet ent review of all ethical aspects of the project pro posals received by it in an 

object ive manner .

2. ETHICS RELATED TO HIV P~TIENTSJ 

• HIV/A IDS has claimed more deaths than any other disease worldwide. 
• Because of the combination of the severity of disease, its transmissibility, and discriminat ion against the 

groups most affected by HIV, the emergence of AIDS gave ri se to many challenging clin ical ethics issues. 

Care and treatment for HIV/AIDS and its related research, especially in HIV-prevent ive vaccine trials, 

require stringent ethical guidelines. 

THE 4 BASIC PRINCIPLES OF MEDICAL ETHICS that apply to both cltnica: and research ethics are as follows 

• Autonomy 

• Beneficence 
• Non-maleficence 

• Justice 

Respect for Autonomy: 
• Autonomy is usually expressed as the right of competent adults to make informed decisions about their 

own medica l ca re 
• Patients have the right to control what happens to their bodies 
• This principle simply means that an informed, competent adult patient can refuse or accept treatments, 

drugs, and surgeries according to their wishes. 

• These decisions must be respected by everyone 

• This is also called the principle of human dignity 

8~neticence · 
• All healthcare providers must strive to improve their patient's health, to do the most good for the patient 

in every situatipn. 
• But what is good for one patient may not be good for another, so each situation should be considered 

individually. 
• In clinical research, the risks are minimized so that the potential benefits of research outweigh the risks 



--.... 
VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

Non-maleficence: 

• "First, do no harm" 

• In every situation, healthcare providers shou ld avoid causing harm to their patients.  

• Where harm cannot be avoided, there is an obligation to minimize the harm and not to increase the risk 
of harm to others 

• When combining beneficence and non-maleficence, each action must produce more good than harm 

Justice: 
• Doctors should try to be as fair as possible when ottering treatments to patients and allocating scarce 

medical resources 

• Treat others equitably, distribute benefits/ burdens fairly . 
• In cl inical research, individuals must be treated fairly, including fair selection and distribution of 

participants within the research recruitment 

CONFIDENTIALITY 

• The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) has made specific recommendat ions for keeping 
reporting confidential, including anonymous testing to reduce the risk of discr imination 

• Healthcare providers have a duty to report HIV infections and AIDS cases to public health authorities, 
because of the benefits to the public health that outweigh t he risk to individual 

• Healthcare providers may also be permitted to disclose a patient's HIV infection to persons at risk of 
infection without legal penalty to help prevent serious harm to an identifiable person and minim ize the 
risk of spread of the disease (World Medical Association, 2013 ) 

• Similarly, the concept of privileged disclosure between colleagues should be introduced for the benefit 
and protection of pat ients 

INFORMED CONSENT 

• Informed consent is based on the principle that competent individuals are entitled to choose freely 
whether to part icipate in research 

• Obta ined by the researcher from the research participants before conducting the study 
• Informed consent is intended to protect the individual 's freedom of choice and their autonomy . 

HIV TESTING 

• HIV testing should guarantee freedom and security of the indiv iduals, the right to privacy and dignity, the 
pol icy of voluntary informed consent together w ith pre- and post-counselling. 

• It is recommended that all pregnant women be tested for HIV as a rout ine part of prenatal care to 
prevent mother-to-child transmission 

• No employer may require an employee, or an appl icant for employment, to undertake an HIV test . 
• Authorized testing will be granted by the Labour Court in case where the emplo yer wan t s t es t·i ng as a 

condition of employment. 

• Testing is permissible when initiated by the employee

,- Selective testing

• Public health approach (opt-out) .

3. ETHl~AL D~LEMMA: BENEFIC_~N~E VS AUTONOMY 

• Ethical dilemmas in healthcare are common and can occur when there are two or more confl icti ng ethical 
principles or va lues. 

• Patient autonomy and beneficence often clash, w hen t he patient's best interest s may go against t heir 
wishes, beliefs or preferences, o r t hey cannot make decisions themse lves due to a lack of capacity . 

Examples: 

• A termina lly i ll child is on life-support and has sust ained brain damage. Based on evidence, doctors opine 
that withdrawing l ife-su pport is in the 'best interests' of t he child, as t here is minimal chance of regaining 
consciousness. Even if the child does, it is likely to suffer from severe cognitive and physical di sabi lities. 
However, the ch ild cannot decide as and t he parents disagree w it h the doctors. 

• A menta lly il l patient is in cris is and is t hreat ening to harm themselves and others. Crisis intervention has 
been umuccessful. Mental health professionals break confidentialit y to inform t he police who detain the 
patient under t he M enta l Health care Act 2017 for their 'best interests'. The patient is det ained and 
treated without t heir agreem ent for thei r safety and others. 

• A girl had sickle cell disease, w hich was threatening her life due to her deteriorating condition. Her 
parent' s re ligious beliefs, as Jehovah's Witnesses, would not allow them to consent to the blood 

transfusion. 
Healthcare professionals can balance pat ient autonomy and benefi cence by: 

• Person-centred care - provides personalized and compassionate care and treatments that meet 

individual patient need s, wishes, be liefs and preferences. 
• Shared decision-making - work t ogether w it h the patient to respect his autonomy and balance it with 

beneficence . 
• Effective and open communication -ensure that communicat ion w ith pati ents is open and transparent 

so patients bett er understand and are well-informed. 
• Robust healthcare policies and guidelines - every healthcare provider has policies on ethics specific to 

their secto rs and clear guidel ines for resolving eth ica l conflicts. 
• Ethical training and education -appropriate t raining on hea lt hca re eth ics and the pol icies, ru les and 

guidelines relati ng to t heir sector.

4. What are the primary barriers in implementing healthcare as universal right? What strategies can be employed 
to overcome these barriers? 

UNIVERSAL HEAL TH CARE: 
• India has adopted several policies, both at the national and state levels, toward improving access to 

healthcare. It has also been enthusiastic in achieving Universal Health Coverage (UHC). 
• India's UHC service index score has improved in the last decade, but it still ranks poorly on effective UHC 

coverage with a score of 47. 
BARRIERS OF UNIVERSAL HEALTH COVERAGE IN INDIA 
The lack of a systemi~pproach to health reforms 

• The health policies are vertical, often disease-specific programs, and fragmented instead of taking a 
comprehensive "systemic" approach. 

Barriers related to health financing 
• There are shortcomings in all three main aspects of health financing - the amount of funds allocated to 

health, how funds are pooled, and how services are purchased. 
• Inadequate government health expenditures is a major constraint to achieving UHC. 
• There is also inefficient utilization of existing funds. 
• The Government health insurance programs targets mostly at low-income groups ( ~40% population) and 

the commercial health insurance plans caters to high-income consumers. The middle-income households 
t hat constitute the majority of India's population are excluded from government schemes. 

• Most insurance plans covers only hospitalizations and excluded outpatient care and medication. 
Barriers related to the organization of healthcare delivery 

• Fragmentation across the health system (especially between the public and private sectors and among 
different levels of care), - has led to inequities in access to high-quality healthcare 

• Only those who can afford private healthcare, have better access to better quality care than those who 
depend on public facilities. 

• Indian health system is a hospital- and physician-centric, focusing on curative, doctor-led, care instead of 
comprehensive primary care . 

• Challenges with human resources for health (HRH) · unequal HRH planning and allocations, focusing on 
merely increasing the number of physicians instead of concentrating on role definitions, , poor quality of 
training across personnel, weak support structures, and low motivation 

Politics 

• Health programs-are centralized and implemented in national missions, which are often not responsive to 
local contextual realities. 

• Insufficient political priority and commitment to health by successive governments as well as by the 
public 

STRATEGIES TO OVERCOME THESE BARRIERS: A comprehensive systemic approach towards health care, 
sufficient health funding, expanding government health insurance schemes to middle class people, re-organizing 
health care delivery to patient-centric and preventive approach and pollical commitment.

s. COMM UNICATION RfTWHN DOCTORS, PUBLIC /\ND MCDIAJ 

• High-quality hea lthcare requires the effective communica tion skil ls of physicians. 
• Poor communication can lead to patient dissatisfaction, untreated illnesses, and complaints. 

BENLFITS OF GOOD COMMUNICATION SKILLS 

• Development o f meaningful and trustworthy relationship between the doctors and patients 
• The diagnostic capability of the doctor is greatly enhanced 
• Useful in managing difficult clinical encounters and thus decreases the frustration of both the doctor and 

the pati en t or att endant in situat ions of emotional outbursts 
• Patient's level of satisfaction is improved by better recognition and understanding of their ailment and 

the treatment av ailable 

This further increases their compliance to the advices given by the doctor 
• It has positive impact on patient's psychology, mental health, tolerance power and quality of life 
• Decreases work stress and increase job satisfaction of doctors 

COMPONENTS OF COMMUNICATION: fffc ctIvc• commun1ca1ion has three basic components 
• Verbal component - dea ls with the content of the message including se lection of the words. 

, M ost important and it includes Information about the nature, course and prognosis of the 
disease; various treatment options available; nature, cost and yield of the investigat ions and 
risks/benefits of invasive procedures 

• Non- verbal component - body language like posture, gesture, facial expression and spatial distance 
• Paraverbal component - includes tone, pitch, pacing and volume of the voice. 

Three main goals of physician-patient communication. 
• Creating a good interpersonal re lationship. 
• Facilitating the exchange of personal information. 
• Including the patient in decision-making. 

BARRIERS TO GOOD COMM UNICATIONS 
• Most important _ lack of insight due to inadequate knowledge & training in communicat ion skill s. 

• Not keeping patients adequately informed. 

• Non-ver b a I componen ts Of communications are frequently neglected. 

• language barrier .

Criteria for IDEAL SUICIDAL POISON 
• Cheap 

• Easily available 
• No bad taste so it can be easily consumed with food or drink . 
• Highly toxic 
• Should not cause much pain or suffering before death . 
• Certain death 
• Opium, Barbiturates, cyanide satisfy most of the above criteria . 
• But commonly used poisons for suicide are organophosphates and organocarbamates .

• Route of administration 

► Sublingual, intravenous and inhalation routes cause most rapid absorption 
► Intramuscular, subcutaneous and rectal routes can also absorb poisons rapidly 
► Broken or ulce rated skin absorbs poisons quicker than healthy skin

• Associated diseases 

)., Associated co-morbid conditions can worsen the condition of a person due to poisoning 

► Sedatives and tranquilizers are tolerated in high doses by maniac patients 

• Sleep 
:,. Absorption of many poisons is slow during sleep owing to slow metabol ism 

• Cumulative action 

:,.. When the rate of excretion is less than the rate of intake, the poison accumulates in tissues, and 

it is called a cumulative poison E.g.: Lead 

:,.. Lead may not cause toxicity when consumed once in a low dose. But when low dose is consumed 

for long periods, it accumulates in the tissues and cause toxicity 

• Idiosyncrasy 
:,.. Few individuals may react adversely to a particular drug, t hough the general population tolerates 

the drug well. E.g.: aspirin 

• Tolerance 
► Due to prolonged use, an individual may develop tolerance to certain drugs 

► He may not show any signs of toxicity even when the poison is consumed at a h igh dose 

,, E.g.: opium, alcohol

ANTIDOTES 

• Antidote is a remedy to counteract the effect of poison 
• Types 

; Physical(mechanical) 
). Chemical(neutrallzatlon) 
').. Pharmacological/Physiological (antagonists) 
'; Chelation therapy 

Physical antidote~ 
• They prevent or reduce the action ot poison by their physical presence 
• They do not destroy , inactivate or antagon ize the action of poison 
• Demulcents (form protective action on mucous membrane of stomach) - egg albumin, starch, milk 
• Adsorbents (adsorb poisons) - activated charcoal 
• Diluents (delay absorption) - milk, water, boiled rice or vegetables 

Chemica_uintidotes 
• They react with the poison and form new compounds which may be non-toxic/less toxic/inso
• lub

W le
e  

ak non-carbonate alkalis for acids 
• Weak vegetable acids (citric acid or acetic acid) for alkalis 
• Common sa lt for silver nitrate (silver chloride formed) 
• Egg albumin for mercuric chloride (mercuric albuminate formed) 
• Freshly prepared ferric oxide for arsenic poisoning (ferric arsenate formed) 

Physiological or Ph~rl!lacological antidotes 
• They act on different systems of the body, producing signs and symptoms opposite to

LEGAL DUTIES 
• Note the prelim inary particulars: Name, age, sex, address, occupation, accompanying person' s name, 

date and time of examination 

• Note 2 identification marks 
• Carefully elicit history whether sym ptoms appeared after intake of food, quantity consumed etc. 

• Maintain records properly 
• Collect and preserve samples (vomitus, gastric lavage fluid , urine) 

• If the patient is serious, make arrangement for dying declarat ion 

• In the event of death, do not issue death certificate, inform the police 

• If a medical practitioner is summoned by a police officer, he is bound to give all information regarding 

the case (S 175 Cr PC) 

Suspected homicidal poisoning 
• Inform the nearest police station or Magist rate (Section 39 Cr PC) 

• Report to the police; Non-compliance is punishable under 176 IPC (Cl 209 BNS) 

• Precaution to prevent possibil ity of further administ rat ion of poison 

• A reliable attendant or a staff nurse appointed by the doctor himself 

• Patient not allowed to eat anything brought by friends/ attendant s except the nursing staff 

Suicidal poisoning 
• Government doctor - inform police 
• Private practitioner - need not inform 

Accidental poisoning . 
• If there is danger to public heal t h (e.g . food poisoning or cont amina t ion of drinking wa ter source) _ notify 

the public health authorities
