6. Classify poisons. Add a note on preservation of viscera for chemical analysis in a case of death due to suspected 

poisoning. 

Classification of poisons: Refer to Essay Q. No: 1 

PRESERVATION OF VISCERA FOR CHEMICAL ANALYSIS: 

Indications: 

• When death occurs due to poisoning whether suicidal/accidental or homicidal, to know the exact nature 

& type of poison. 

• In all fatal cases of suspected poisoning, where the medical officer suspects the presence of Poison by 

smell or some other evidence during autopsy. 

• To exclude poisoning in instances where the cause of death could not be ascertained during postmortem 

and there is no pathological lesion or injury sufficient to cause death. 

• Decomposed bodies. 

• When the investigation officer requests for preservation of viscera. 

Y!scera preserved routinely for chemical analysis : 

i. Entire stomach & its contents (Entire preferably) 

i1. Proximal 30 cm of small intestine & its contents 

iii. Liver 500 g - cut into small pieces 

iv. Half of each kidney - cut into small pieces 

v Blood 30 ml (should not be collected from the CAVITIES) 

vi. Urine 30 ml 

~~ction of blood: 

• 20ml of blood can be drawn from peripheral vessels like femoral vein, iliac or subclavian vein. 

• Blood for toxicological analysis should never be collected from the heart, thoracic or abdominal cavities. 



-
362 t VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY -

In special cases, the following viscera may also be preserved: 
• Brain - alcohol and barbiturate poisoning 
• Spinal cord - strychnine, Gelsemium poisoning 
• Lungs - Inhaled poisons, anesthetic deaths 
• Heart - Oleander, digitalis 
• Long bone (preferably femur) & Scalp hair - Heavy metal poisoning 
• CSF - alcohol poisoning 
• Skin & underlying tissue (affected area) - Injected poisons, corrosives 

Preservatives used: 
PreseNative used . . 

All poisons except corrosives, aconite Saturated saline 
All acids except* Rectified spirit 
Blood (Alcohol, cocaine, cyanide, CO) Sodium fluoride + potassium oxa late 
Blood (oxalic acid, ethylene glycol, fluoride) Sodium cit rate 
Carbon monoxide Liquid paraffin 
Urine Thymol or Toluene 
Histopathological examination 10%formalin 
Suspected rabies & Virologic studies 50% glycerin 

• Rectified spirit* cannot be used as preservative for, 
:, Alcohol 

,,. Formaldehyde 
',, Acetic acid 

,,. Kerosene 
:,... Chloral hydrate 

,,. Paraldehyde 
,, Chloroform 

,- Phenol 
,, Carbolic acid 

,- Phosphorus poisoning 

• Formalin should not be used as preservative for preservation of viscera for chemical analysis because it 
hardens and fixes the tissues and extraction of poisons becomes difficult. 

Quantity of preservative: 
• It should be equal to that of the viscera in bulk . 

• The viscera and preservative should not fill the container, but only reach 2/3rd of its height. 
• It prevents bursting of the container in case any gas is accumulated due to decomposition of viscera . 

Dispatch of viscera 
• Requisition to the Chemical Examiner, Regional Forensic Science Laboratory 
• Specimen should be sent in clean, sterile and separate containers 
• A sample of the preservative used is also sent in separate bottle 
• Sealed and labeled, properly giving details of name of the deceased, date and place of autopsy, name 

and signature of the doctor 

• Sent through a police constable 



GENERAL ASPECTS OF TOXICOLOGY 

, ANALYTICAL TOXICOLOG~ 

• Analytical toxicology is the detection, identification, and measurement of foreign compounds 

(xenobiotics) in biological and other specimens 

WALITATIVE METHODS 

Bedside clinic tests to detect poison/drug 

to/our Te~ 
• Trinder's test : 100 ml of Trinder's reagent+ 2 ml urine ➔ mix for 5 seconds ➔ violet or purple colour 

indicates the presence of salicylates (salicylic acid, salicylamide, and methyl salicylic acid). 

• Ferric chloride test: 1 ml of 5% ferric chloride solution+ 2 ml urine ➔ persistent purple colour ➔ 

presence of phenol, phenothiazines, phenylbutazone, oxyphenbutazone, or salicylates . 

• FPN test : 1 ml of FPN reagent+ 1 ml of urine or stomach contents ➔ mix for 5 seconds ➔ pink, red, 

violet, to blue (phenothiazines) or Green or blue (Tricyclics) 

• 0 -Cresol test : 0.5 ml concentrated HCI + 0.5 ml urine or stomach contents ➔heat in a boiling water bath 

fo r 10 minutes, and cool ➔ Add 1 ml of aqueous O-cresol solution (10 gm/L) to 0.2 ml of the hydrolysate, 

followed by 2 ml ammonium hydroxide, and mix for 5 seconds ➔ blue or blue-black colour ➔ 

paracetamol or phenacetin. 

• Dichromate test : Add equal volumes of 10% sodium dichromate in 50% su lfuric acid to the urine sample 

➔ green colour ➔ ethanol. 

• Marquis test : Add a mixture of 3 ml concentrated sulfuric acid and 3 drops of formalin to the gast ric fluid 

➔ purple colour which gradually tu rns blue ➔ opium or its derivatives. 

• Lee-Jones test : Few crystals of ferrous sulfate+ 5 drops of 2% sodium hydroxide+ 5 ml gastric fluid ➔ 

Boil and cool ➔ Add 10 drops of 10% HCI ➔ greenish-blue colour indicates cyanide; purple colour 

indicates salicylates. 
• Qualitative Desferrrioxamine colour test (QDCT) . 2 ml of gastric fluid+ 2 drops of 30% hydrogen 

peroxide are placed in two plastic tubes. 5 ml of Desferrrioxamine solution is placed in one tube, and the 

resulting colour change is compared with the other t ube (control) . Orange or red colour ➔ toxic levels of 

iron. 
• Desferrioxamine challenge test (OCT), (variation of QDCT): 25 to 50 mg/kg of Desferrrioxamine is 

administe red to the patient by i.m. injection. If the urine voided subsequently is pinkish in colour (vin 

rose · urine) ➔ Iron poisoning 
• Meixner test: Stool or gastric sample is diluted w ith methanol ➔ centrifuged ➔ Add 1-2 drops to a piece 

of newspaper ➔ encircle t he spot with a pencil and dry it ➔ Adda few drops of concentrated HCI to the 

spot ➔ Blue colour forms within a few minutes ➔ amatoxin (present in most toxic mushrooms) . 

•~-th_e_r T-e-st_~,  
• lsonitrile test: Small amount of gastric contents+ 10 ml water + 1 ml purified aniline ➔ add 2 ml 20% 

sodium hydroxide ➔ Heat gently for a few minutes ➔ foul odour (skunk odour) ➔ carbon tetrach loride, 

chloral hydrate, chloroform, methyl bromide, or any other chlorinated hydrocarbon. 

• Tens1lon (edrophonium challenge) test : 10 mg edrophonium is given IV in a case of sudden paralysis. 

Dramatic recovery occurs in case of myasthenia gravis. In poisoning cases (e.g., botulism) no 

improvement. 

• Melzer's test: To detect toxic mushrooms (especially Amanita phalloides). The spores are stained with 1 
 

drop of M elzer's reagent and viewed under a microscope. Spores of A. phal/oides and a few other deadly 

. h a bluish black colour (amylo1d react ion). 
mushrooms wI 11 s ow

Thin layer Chromatography 

• Thin Layer Chromatography is a technique used to isolate non-volatile mixtures. 
• The experiment is conducted on a sheet of aluminum foil, plastic, or glass which is coated w ith a thin 

layer of adsorbent material. 
• The material usually used is aluminum, cellulose, or silica gel. 
• On completion of the separation, each component appears as spots separated vertically. 

QUALITATIVE METHODS 
• Chromatography 

, Chromatography is defined as the separation of the components of a mixture by slow passage over or 
through a material that absorbs the components differently. 

, Gas chromatography - for volatile compounds (e.g. alcohols) 
, High Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) 

• Ultraviolet Spectrophotometry 
• Mass Spectrometry 
• Atomic Absorption Spectrophotometry 
• Neutron Activation Analysis 
• Radio lmmuno Assays

3. PARASUICIDE 

• Synonyms: attempted suicide, suicidal gesture 
• An apparent attempt at suicide, in which the aim is not death 
• They action is often impulsive and undertaken to get rid of an intolerable situation 
• E.g.: superficially cutting the wrists, sub lethal drug overdose consumption 
• Commonly seen in adolescents and young adults 
• It is the strongest known indicator for a future successful suicide attempt 
• 50% of those who have committed suicide have history of suicidal gesture

~CTI\./ATED CHARCOAL*] 
7
· - • It is the most preferred method for decontamination of ingested poison 

• Activated charcoal is a black slurry material 
• It has a large surface area 

• It acts as a mechanical antidote - adsorbs poisons and thus reduces their absorption 
• Most effective within 1 hour of ingestion 
• Dose - 1-2g/kg body weight 
• It is effective in most poisons 
• Not useful in - corrosives, heavy metals, alcohol, cyanide, hydrocarbons

B. !CHELATING AGENTSj 

• They are metal complexing agents 

• They are used in heavy metal poisoning 

• They have a greater affinity for metals then endogenous enzymes and form complexes with heavy metals 

• These heavy metal complex is more soluble in water and thus excreted in urine 

• Commonly used chelating agents: 
.,., Dimercaprol 
:,.. EDTA 
.,., Penicillamine 
,.., Desferrioxamine 
,.., DMSA 
,,,.,. DMPS 

DIMERCAPROL (BAL- British anti-lewisite) 
• They have great affinity for sulfhydryl (SH) radicals 
• It has two unsaturated SH groups 
• Antidote for: arsenic, lead, mercury, thallium 
• BAL can precipitate hemolysis in G6PD deficient individuals 

EDTA (ethylene di-amine tetra-acetic acid) 
• Commonly used as an anticoagulant 
• Antidote for almost all heavy metal poisoning 
• First choice chelating agent for arsenic and mercury poisoning 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY I 

Penicillamine 
• Useful for copper poisoning 

Desferrioxamine 
• Useful for acute iron poisoning 

DMSA (succime,) 
• Superior to EDTA in the treatment of lead poisoning 
• Also useful in mercury and arsenic poisoning 
• It is less toxic to kidney and can be given in G6PD deficient patients 

9. Antidotes** 
Refer to Essay Q. No: 4 

10. iFAILURE TO DETECT POISON IN CHEMICAL ANALY_hl 
L--• In certain cases, the poison could not be detected by chemical analysis, even though, clinical features 

during life and circumstantial evidence strongly suggest death due to poisoning. 
The possible causes for negative chemical analysis report 

• The poison might have been eliminated by vomiting or diarrhea 
• Volatile poisons (like alcohol, cyanide) may evaporate and not found in the viscera and blood if there is 

a time lag between preservation and analysis 
• The poison after absorption might have been metabolized and eliminated from the system. 
• Improper preservation (not using the right preservative, faulty techniques etc.) 
• Some vegetable alkaloidal poisons cannot be detected by chemical methods. 
• Biological toxins and venoms which are protein in nature cannot be separated from body tissues 
• Faulty techniques of analysis 
• Insufficient quantity of poison 

11. [POISON INFORMATION cENTIUj 
• Poison information center (PIC) or Poison control center (PCC) is a specialized unit that provides 

information on poisoning management and immediate information on early diagnosis, treatment, 
management, prevention and hazards management of poisoning through well trained poison information 
specialist. 

• Poison control centers answer questions about potential poisons in addition to providing treatment 
management advice 

• Poison information center (PIC) can play a vital role in the prevention and management of poisoning 
cases through provision of information to public and also to healthcare professionals. 

• In India, the National Poisons Information Centre (NPIC) was established in 1995 in the Department of 
Pharmacology at the All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi. 

• The centre provides round the clock toxicological information and advice on the management of 
poisoned patient s adapted t o the level of the enquirer. 

• The basis of this service are the databases on poisoning, drug reactions and also the cont inuous and 
systematic collection of data from the library. 

• It has a computer software called INTOX to provide information about all kinds of poisons 



GENERAL ASPECTS OF TOXICOLOGY 

iz. 'THE NARCOTIC DRUGS AND PSYCHOACTIVE SUBSTANCES (NDPS) ACT 1985l 
• According to this Act, a narcotic drug refers to cocoa leaf, cannabis (hemp), opium, poppy straw and 

includes all manufactured drugs (cocoa derivatives, medicinal cannabis, opium derivatives and poppy 

straw concentrate and any other narcotic substance preparation) 
• The Act defines psychotropic substance as any substance, natural or synthetic, included in the list of 

psychotropic substances specified in a Schedule to the Act 
• If a person possess in a small quantity, any narcotic drug or psychotropic substance intended for personal 

use and not for sale or distribution, or consumes any narcotic or psychotropic substance, he shall be 
punished with imprisonment for up to one year or with fine or with both. 

• The Act prohibits the cultivation of poppy, cannabis and coca plants, however restricted cultivation is 
allowed for medicinal o r scientific purpose

• OPCs are powerful inhibitors of acetylcholinesterase (AChE) (true cholinesterase and 

pseudocholinesterase) 
• AChE - enzyme that degrades the neurotransmitter acetylcholine (ACh) in

[ PESTICIDES 

CNS effects 
Initially Later 

• Ataxia • Depression of respiratory and 

• Rest lessness Cardiovascular centers 

• Confusion • Drowsiness 
• Emotiona l !ability • Hypothermia 
• Tremors • Convulsions 
• Slurred speech • Coma 
• Generalized weakness 

INTERMEDIATE SYNDROME 

• Develops 1- 4 days after resolution of acute organophosphate poisoning symptoms 
• Causes 

',, Inadequate treatment (subtherapeutic oxime doses) 
,.. Inadequate ventilation 
;, Prolonged cholinesterase inhibition 

• Features 
)., Motor cranial nerve palsies 
, Weakness of facial, extraocular, palatal and neck muscles 
, Weakness of proximal limb muscles 
, Sparing of distal muscles 
,.. Areflexia 
, Acute respiratory paralysis 

• Persists for 4-18 days, may require mechanical ventilation 
• Do not respond to oximes or atropine 
• Treatment: supportive 

ORGANOPHOSPHORUS INDUCED DELAYED POLYNEUROPATHY IOPIDP] OR DELAYED SYNDROME 

• OPIDP occurs after 1-4 weeks 
• Due to the inhibition of Neuropathy target esterase (NTE], 
• Distal muscle weakness and atrophy with relative sparing of the neck muscles, cranial nerves, and 

proximal muscle groups 
• Spasticity and ataxia may be present 
• Due to demyelination 
• Do not respond to oximes or atropine 
• Treatment: supportive 

DIAGNOSIS 
• Measurement of cholinesterase activity 

,.. RBC cholinesterase is a more usefu l marker as it correlates better with CNS AChE 

o Normal levels: 5000 - 10000 U/L 

0 Mild poisoning: 30 - 50% of enzyme activity 

0 Moderate poisoning: 10 - 20% of enzyme activity 

o Severe poisoning: < 10% of enzyme activity 
,.. Plasma cholinesterase is easier to assay and is more readily avai lable. 

• P-Nitrophenol detection in urine 

• Thin layer chromatography 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY t 

TREATMENT 
• Prevention of further absorption 

► Remove patient from site of exposure 
► Contact poisoning: Strip and wash t horoughly with soap and water 
► Ocular exposure: irrigate the eye with water or normal saline 

• Life-saving supportive measures 
, Airway maintenance - most important 
i,, Adequate oxygenation 
► Intubation may be necessary in cases of respiratory distress or seizures 
',- IV fluid administration to replace losses 

• Decontamination: Stomach wash and/or activated charcoal 
• Antidotes 

► ATROPINE 
o 1 - 2 mg IV every 15 mins 
o The endpoint for atropinization is dried pulmonary secretions and adequate oxygenation. 
o Tachycardia or mydriasis must not be used to limit or to stop subsequent doses of atropine 
o Improves muscarinic and CNS manifestations 
o No effect on nicotinic manifestations 

, OX IMES - cholinesterase reactivators 
o Reactivates the AChE by competing with the phosphate moiety of the OPC and splits away 

from the AChE molecule. 
o Predominantly reverses nicotinic effects. Muscarinic and CNS effects are also controlled t o 

some extent. 

o Oximes are benefici al only if administered before OPC-AChE complex becomes irreversible 
(aging). No benefit if it is given after 48 hours. 

o Pralidoxime (2-PAM) is t he most commonly used oxime. 
o 1-2g in 100ml of NS, IV over 30 mins. Repeat after 1 hour if muscle w eakness and 

fasciculations are not relieved. 
o The maximum dose should not exceed 12 g in a 24-hour period. 
o Atropine and Pralidoxime are synergistic in action. 
o Not effective in carbamate poisoning (e.g. Baygon) and nerve gases. 
o Hagedorn oximes - effective against all OPCs. 

o Sugar oximes (Diacetyl monoxime-DAM) - crosses the blood brain barrier effectively and 
provide protection against the neurological effect s of OPC poisoning. 

• Treatment of convulsions: DIAZEPAM 

Cause of death: Respiratory failure 

POST-MORTEM APPEARANCES 
Externa l 

• Smell of kerosene from mouth, gastric contents and body 
• Blood stained froth at mouth and nose 
• Congest ion of face 

• Cyanosis of lips, nose, fi ngers and extremities 



a: PESTICIDES 

Internal 
• Stomach contents have kerosene like odor (ingested poison) 
• Stomach mucosa congested with submucosal petechial hemorrhages. 
• Lungs: & Brain congested and edematous

8. A 35 years old farmer i.s brought to your hospital with alleged history of ingestion of an unknown poisonous 
substance. Patient has excessive salivation, lacrimation, urination, defecation and emesis. On examination, 
pupil is constricted. 
a) What is the most likely diagnosis? Give reasons. 
b} Which investigations will support your diagnosis? 
c) Describe treatment of this poisoning in detail. 

MOST LIKELY DIAGNOSIS: ORGANOPHOSPHORUS COMPOUND POISONING which is suggested by 
• Farmer with h/o ingestion of unknown substance. (most likely a pesticide) 
• Excessive sa livat ion, lacrimation, urination, defecation and emesis. (muscari nic symptoms) 
• Pupillary constriction . 

INVESTIGATIONS & TREATMENT: Refer to Essay Q. No: 1 

'J A 17-year old was brought to the casualty in a semi-conscious state, after he had consumed an insecticide, 
Malathion. Blue-green stains were present on the front of his shirt. Kerosene like odor could be perceived 
around him. He was declared dead after half an hour, as he failed to respond to resuscitative measures. 
a) What kind of compound is Malathion? What are the signs and symptoms? 
b) How do you manage and treat the case? 
c) What are the samples to be collected for chemical analysis before death and at autopsy? Mention the 

preservatives used. 
d) What is the medico-legal importance? {4+4+4+3} 

MALATHION is a type of ORGANOPHOSPHORUS COMPOUND 
Signs, symptoms and Management: Refer to Essay Q. No: 1 

SAMPLES TO BE COLLECTED FOR CHEMICAL ANALYSIS BEFORE DEATH: 
• Blood - for RBC cholinesterase and plasma cholinesterase estimation; Thin layer chromatography 
• Urine - for P-Nitrophenol test 
• First sample of Gastric lavage - for chemical analysis 

SAMPLES TO BE COLLECTED AT AUTOPSY: Refer to Chapter 23; Essay Q. No: 6 
• In Pesticide poisoning, in addition to the routinely preserved viscera, 10g of fat from abdominal wall or 

mesentery shall also be preserved 
• Preservative used for suspected OPC poisoning: Saturated solution of Sodium chloride (most commonly 

used) or Rectified spirit (ideal) 

MEDICOLEGAL IMPORTANCE· 
• Suicide: OPC consumption is one of the common methods of suicide in India, because of its easy availability 
• Homicide: rare, because of strong smell and bad tast e 
• Accident: common in farmers and agricult ural laborers. OPC gets absorbed via skin and mucosa . 

OPC compou nds resist putrefaction for a longer time. 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY J 
IO. Dcsmbe the mode of action, clinical features and treatment in a case of carbamate poisoning. 

CARBAMATE POISONINd 
MFCHANISM OF ACTION 

• Carbamates inhibit acetylcholinesterase (AChE) by carbamylation 
• Carbamates do not cause ageing of the AChE enzyme, and toxicity is therefore usually more brief and self-

limited than with the OP compounds 
• Carbamates do not cross the BBB, so CNS toxicity is minimal. 

Clinical features: Refer to Essay Q. No: 1 

TREATMENT OF CARBAMATE POISONING 
• Prevention of further absorption · Refer to Essay Q. No: 1 
• Life-saving supportive measures Refer to Essay Q. No: 1 
• Decontamination· Refer to Essay Q. No: 1 
• Atropine: Refer to Essay Q. No: 1 
• Treatment of convulsions: diazepam 
• OXIMES ARE CONTRAINDICATED - because it produces a carbamylated oxime, which is a more potent 

cholinesterase inhibitor than carbamate itself 

11. Write an essay on Aluminium Phosphide poisoning . 

~ LUMINIUM PHOSPHIDE POISONING 
• Aluminium phosphide (AIP), a solid fumigant pesticide is widely used in India for grain preservation 

h a
o t

m  
es and in warehouses. 

• It is a common pesticide used in in India as it is cheap, effective, free from toxic residue an
a d

f  
f d
e o

c e
t s

s  
e n
e o
d t
  
viability 

• It protects the stored grain from pests, insects and rodents by liberating toxic ph
• o
 sphine (PH3) gas 

It is available in the form tablets of 3 g 

MECHANISM OF ACTION 
• On contact with moisture [or HCI or water in the stomach], AIP releases PHOSPHINE . 

AIP + H20 ➔Al(OHh + PH3 

AIP + 3HCI ➔AIC'3 + PH3 

, The exact mode of action of PH3 is not known but at cellular level, it 
, Inhibits mitochondrial oxygen uptake. 
, Blocks oxidative phosphorylation. 

Formation of highly reactive hydroxyl radicals 

• AIP can be absorbed as such and possibly deposited in liver leading to slow release of PH3 which may be 
responsible for extended poisoning in humans 



pfSTICIDES 

SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS 
• GIT: Nausea, vomiting, metallic taste, retrosternal burning, esophageal strictures 
• Garlicky or decaying fish odor 

• Cardiovascular: profound and refractory hypotension, tachycardia myocarditis, arrhythmias 

• Respiratory Cough, dyspnoea, rhonchi, crackles, 

• Hepatobiliary - Jaundice, hepatomegaly, raised transaminases, hepatic failure 
• Renal - Acute renal failure 

• CNS: Anxiety, apprehension, Drowsiness and coma, disturbances in consciousness 
TREATMENT 

• Airway maintenance - Breathing control - Circulation monitoring 
• Gastric lavage with KMn04 (1 :10,000) or magnesium sulphate to inactivate or oxidise the unabsorbed 

poison. 
• Gastric lavage should not be done with water since it may liberate more phosphine. 

• Activated charcoal (100 g) may be given to absorb PH3 
• There is no specific antidote to phosphine 
• Magnesium sulphate - acts as a membrane stabilizer against the free radical stress. 

• It can reverse some arrhythmias and reduces mortality by 50% 
• Phosphine is a stable, partially water-soluble gas and is excreted to some extent in the urine but is mainly 

excreted through breath 
• Hence, adequate hydration and renal perfusion by IV fluids and low-dose dopamine must be maintained 

to allow its renal excretion. 
POST-MORTEM FINDINGS 

• Garlicky odour in the mouth and gastric contents 
• The lungs show desquamated epithelium with alveoli thickened by haemolysed red blood cells and 

leucocytic infiltration around bronchioles 
• The liver shows congestion, oedema, areas of centrizonal necrosis and fatty infiltration. 
• The heart shows congestion, oedema, fragmented fibres, focal areas of myocardial necrosis and 

leucocytic infiltration. 
• The kidneys show congestion, oedema, necrosis and degenerative and regenerative activity of the 

tubules. 
• The stomach and the intestines also show congestion, oedema and leucocytic infiltration with sloughing 

of mucosa. 
Circumstances of poisoning 
• The poisoning is generally suicidal 

• Rarely homicidal 
• Occasionally accidental (children)

ZINC PHOSPHID[ 

• Zinc phosphide is a widely used rodenticide against small mammals (mice, rats, field mice, and squirrel) 
• Zinc phosphide is hydrolyzed by gastric acid and is transformed into phosphine gas. 
• Phosphine gets absorbed through t he stomach and intestine into the blood stream

4. ORGANOCHLORINES 
• They are nonselective insecticides 
• They are CNS stimulants 
• E.g. DDT, Endrin (plant penicillin), Dieldrin, Lindane 

Mechanism of action 
• Selectively bind to open (activated) Na+ channels and slow their closing (inactivation), thus allowing the 

influx of sodium ions. 
• The continuous influx of sodium ion causes depolarization and repetitive firing of the nerve membrane 

which is clinically seen as tremors 
Signs and symptoms 

• Prodromal signs/symptoms: agitation, confusion, dizziness, headache, hyperesthesia and parest hesias, 
leg weakness 

• CNS stimulation: Myoclonus, Tremors, Seizures 
• Hyperthermia 
• o,tated pupils 

Management 
• Life-saving supportive measures 

,- Airway control and adequate oxygenation 
>- Intubation may be necessary in cases of respiratory distress or seizures 
,- IV fluid administration to replace losses 

• Decontamination 
:.,... Avoid from further exposure 
,- Contact poisoning: Strip the patient and wash thoroughly with soap and water 
:.,... Ocular exposure: irrigate the eye with water or normal saline 
,- Ingestion : stomach wash and/or activated charcoal 

• No specific antidote available 
• CHOLESTYRAMINE 

-,, A non-absorbable steroid binding ion exchange resin 
,, Increases fecal excretion of OCs 

• Treatment of convulsions. diazepam 
• Contraindicated drugs - atropine, adrenaline 



83 
f pfSTICIDES X 3

s. pYRETHROID~ 
• Pyrethroids are widely used for insect control both at home (e.g., Baygon spray, mosquito repellents, 

etc.) and in agricultural fields 

• They are used for the treatment of scabies and lice in humans. 
• They have favorable toxicological profiles with high selectivity & toxicity to insects & low toxicity to 

humans. 
• Type I pyrethroids - no alpha cyano group. 
• Type II pyrethroids have an addition of a cyano group. 
• Type II pyrethroids are more potent insecticides due to the cyano group in their structure . 

Mechanism of action 
• Pyrethroids modify the gating characteristics of voltage-sensitive sodium channels, thereby delaying 

their closure . This results in a protracted sodium influx (tail current) 
• Pyrethroids also decrease chloride channel currents in brain, nerves, muscles, and salivary glands 

Clinical features 
• Common symptoms - facial paresthesia, skin itching, skin burning, dizziness, nausea, vomiting, and 

muscle fasciculations 
• Type I pyrethroids cause ''T'' syndrome 

',, Reflex hyperexcitability 
:,... Fine tremors 

• Type II pyrethroids cause choreoathetosis-salivation syndrome 

-,,, lncoordination, choreoathetosis, seizures 
Direct effects on the skeletal and cardiac muscle, and salivary gland 

Treatment 

• No specific antidote available 

• Mainly supportive and symptomatic treatment

25. METALLIC & NON METALUC IRRITANTS

1. Write in detail about signs, symptoms and Postmortem changes of Arsenic poisoning* 

A -
RSENIC. POISONING 

• Metallic arsenic in pure form is not toxic 
• Arsine gas is the most toxic, followed by trivalent arsenic compounds (e.g., sodium arsenite, arsenic 

trioxide, potassium arsenite) 
Sources 

• Arsenic is used in the manufacture of pesticides and rodenticides in the agricultural industry . 
• It is widely used in t imber preservatives, glass production 
• In many parts of the world, arsenic is a natural contaminant of ground waters 
• Sodium arsenate is used as an ant-ki ller 
• Melarsoprol is used to treat trypanosomiasis 
• Arsenic trioxide is occasionally used in t he treatment of relapsed acute promyelocytic leukaemia 

Action 

• Metallic irritant - inflammation and necrosis of intestinal mucosa - hemorrhagic gastroenteritis 
• Trivalent arsenic (As3

') binds with sulfhydryl groups of mitochondrial enzymes and cause uncoupling of 
oxidative phosphorylation. 

ACUTE POISONING 
• GIT: 

,, Metallic taste in the mouth 
, Nausea, vomiting, colicky abdominal pain 
, Garlic odour in breath 

, Initially blood stained diarrhea; later profuse watery diarrhoea resembling rice-water stools of 
cholera. 

• Cardiomyopathy, arrhythmias 
• Fatty degeneration of liver 
• Shock 

• Renal and hepatic fai lure 

• Hyperthermia 

• Loss of hair (alopecia) 

• Convulsions, coma and death 
Investigations 

• Ingested arsenic: X-ray abdomen shows radio-opacity . 
• Elevated serum(> 0.9 µg/dl) and urine arsenic levels 
• Best analytical method: Atomic absorption spectroscopy and Neutron activation analysis 



~ METALLIC & NON-METALLIC IRRITANTS 

Treatment 
• Hemodynamic stabilization 
• To prevent further absorption: 

, Gastric lavage with warm water and milk 
, Activated charcoal not very effective 
, Demulcents 
► Whole bowel irrigation 

• Chelation therapy: 
, Dimercaprol (BAL) is the chelating agent of choice. 
:.- DMSA (Succimer) and DMPS are preferred now as they do not cause accumulation of arsenic in brain 

and can be given orally 
► EDTA is also useful. 

CHRONIC POISONING (ARSENICOSIS) 
Causes 

• Usually occurs as an occupational hazard in those working with paints, dyes, cosmetics. 
• Overdose of certain homeopathic and ayurvedic medicines containing arsenic. 
• Arsenophagists: Some people take arsenic daily as an aphrodisiac and they acquire tolerance to 250-300 

mg or more in one dose. 
• From contaminated groundwater. 

Clinical features 
• First stage - Gastrointestinal: Loss of weight, loss of appetite, colicky pain, constipation or diarrhoea, 

coated tongue, Nausea, Vomiting. 
• Second stage - Dermatological: 

,., Raindrop pigmentation - Speckled brown pigmentation on the skin flexures, temples, shoulders, 
eyelids and neck 

,., Leucomelanosis - areas of depigmentation on the normal/hyperpigmented skin 
, Hyperkeratosis of palms and soles - multiple hard wart like growths 
,, Aldrich-Mee's' lines - transverse white bands on the nail plate (also seen in Thallium poisoning) 
, Nasal septal perforation 
, Bowen's disease - carcinoma in situ of skin. 
, Chronic ulceration and Gangrene: Black foot disease 

• Third stage - Neurological: 
;... Peripheral neuropathy (sensory-motor) - numbness & tingling in glove and stocking distribution 
► Anemia, leukopenia, thrombocytopenia 
,, Impotence 
,., CVS - Hypertension, ischemic heart disease, cardiac failure 
, Chronic nephritis 

• Fourth stage - Neuromuscular: 
,, Weakness and atrophy of extensor muscles - wrist drop, foot drop 
, Tremors 

► 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY ] 

Post-mortem appearances 
• Rigor mortis lasts longer 
• Delays putrefaction 
• Red velvet appearance of stomach mucosa 
• Subendocardlal petechial haemorrhages 
• Fatty liver degeneration . 

• Arsenic can be detected in bones and hairs even many years after death 
• Chemical analysis: bones and nails preserved 

Medico-legal importance 
• Ideal homicidal poison - odourless and tasteless, simulates a natural disease (cholera) 
• Accidental chronic poisoning - consumption of tube well water with high arsenic levels 
• Chronic poisoning - ayurvedic drugs 
• Arsenophagists - some people take arsenic daily as tonic or aphrodisiac and acquire tolerance. 
• Arsenic was used as murder weapons in royal families and called "the poison of kings and king of poisons." 
• Postmortem imibition of arsenic: 

,, Post-mortem imbibition: the absorption of a poison into the body from the environment after death. 
,- During exhumations, imbibition from surrounding earth should be taken into considerat ion. 
,. Arsenic contamination in soil : easily absorbed by keratin tissue ➔ concentration will be higher in hair 

and nails than the surrounding 
,- Arsnenic poisoning: concentration of arsenic will be more than in soil/water .

5. o) Describe till' !>igns, symptoms, treatment and autopsy findings in a case of chronic lead poisoning

CHRONIC LEAD POISONING! 

• Chronic lead poisoning is also ca lled Saturnism or Plumbism 
• Lead toxicity usually results from chronic repeated exposure and is rare after a single ingestion . 

Sources 

• Chronic lead poisoning is usually seen as an occupational hazard in those involved . t h f 
in e manu acture of 

storage batteries, pigments, car radiators, and plumbing 

• Lead paint dust is the most common source of lead exposure for children 

• Lead is found in some traditional Hispanic and Ayurvedic ethnic medicines 
• Low dose consumption from drinking water supplied through lead pipes 

• Prolonged use of vermilion, dyes and cosmetics containing lead 

Mechanism of act ion 

• Lead combines with sulfhydryl groups and interferes with mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation, 
enhances oxidation and ce ll apoptosis 

• This causes defective heme synthesis, proximal renal tubular and osteoblast dysfunction 



I ., ''/ VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY I 

Clinical features 
• Lead produces a var·i ety of adverse effects on ce II u Ia r f unc t1·0 n an d Pr imarily affects the nervous system , 

gastrointestinal tract, and hematopoietic system. 
• Leod is a cumulative poison, remains accumulated in bones as phosphate and carbonate 
• Symptoms 

, Colicky abdominal pain 
-, Constipation 
-,. Headache 
', Irritability. 

• Severe poisoning may cause coma and convulsions 
• Signs 

, Facial pallor/circum oral is the earliest sign (due to vasoconstriction and anemia) 
',, Anemia - due to impaired heme synthesis and increased fragility of RBCs 
>-- Blue line on gums ca lled lead line or Burtonian line (seen with poor oral hygiene, at the junction of 

gums and teeth due to deposition of lead sulphide) 
',, Lead palsy - common in adults, wrist drop, foot drop may occur 
';, Retinal stippling - on ophthalmoscopic examination 
:.- Reproductive system - sterility in both males and females, abortions in carrying women 
,- Lead osteopathy: X-ray shows radio opaque metaphyseal bands (lead lines) at the metaphyses of 

long bones in children. These lines reflect bone growth arrest. 
, Saturnine gout - joint pain due to inhibition of uric acid excretion by lead. 
Y Encephalopathy - the most common manifestation in children. Often due to tetraethyl lead - lipid 

soluble and distributed in brain 
• lead-containing bullet fragments in or near joint spaces can result in chronic toxicity . 

lead poisoning in children

• Aminolaevulinic acid in urine > 5 micro grams

7. Describe the signs, symptoms, treatment and autopsy findings in a case of chronic mercury poisoning. 
---- ---

CHRONIC MERCURY POISONING or HYDRARGYRISM 
Poisonous compounds of mercury and their sources 

• Mercuric chloride or corrosive sublimate: used in medicine and laboratories as a preservative 
• Mercurous chloride: used as purgative 
• Methylated mercury: high content seen in sword fish 
• Dental fillings composed of mercury amalgam pose a very small risk of chronic mercury poisoning 
• Dimethyl mercury is super toxic-a few drops of exposure via skin absorption or inhaled vapor can cause 

severe cerebellar degeneration and death 
Mechanism of action 

• Mercury binds with sulfhydryl, phosphoryl, carboxyl and amide groups and interferes with mitochondrial 
oxidative phosphorylation 

• Direct oxidative effect of mercuric ions may cause Proximal convoluted tubule necrosis 

Clinical features 
CHRONIC INGESTION 

• Minamata disease (Hunter Russell syndrome) - due to eating sword fish containing high content o
m f

e  o
r r
c g

(methyl mercury) 

• Pink disease or Acrodynia (allergic reaction to inorganic mercury)- redness, swelling, vesiculatio

mation of palm, fi ngers, soles and toes in children 
• Colitis 
• Manifestations are similar to Kawasaki disease 
• If mother consumes during pregnancy - Cerebral palsy in ch ild 

CHRONIC INHALATION 

• Metall ic taste in the mouth, weakness, loss of weight, ataxia, irr itabil ity, depression 
• Classic triad of 

, Gingivostomatitis 
, Intentional tremors (Danbury tremors) 
, Neuro-psychiatric changes 

• Gingivitis, hal itosis, blue line on gums, increased salivat ion 
• Danbury tremors 

, Coarse intentional tremors first in hands 
, Later involves lips and tongue 
, Finally arms and legs 

, Hatter's shakes or glass blower's shakes - when the t remor becomes severe to impair some skilled
a  

ctivities like shaving, writing etc. 
, The most severe form is called concussio mercurialis in which no movement 

• is
  possible 

Mercurial Erethism: insomnia, irritability, anxiety, emotional instabil ity 
• Mercurialentis - brownish deposit of mercury on anterior lens capsule 
• Kidney- membranous glomerulonephritis, PCT necrosis 

Diagnosis 
• Blood mercury level: Normal < 3 µg/100 ml 
• Urine mercury level: Normal < 3 µg/100 ml 
• MRI: Atrophy of cerebellar hemispheres (in organic Hg poisoning) 



I METALLIC & NON-METALLIC IRRITANTS \ 
Treatment 

• Remove from the source of exposure 
• Stomach wash with water and egg white or milk or 5% albumin 
• Inorganic mercury: BAL+ DMSA 

• Organic mercurial : DMSA or DMPS 
• Neurologic toxicity is not reversible after chelation 

Postmortem appearances 
• Non-specific findings 
• Gingivitis, colitis, necrosis or liver and kidney 
• Necrotic changes in the renal tubules

6. jCOPPER SULPHAT~ POISONING 
• Copper sulfate (blue vit riol) occurs as large blue crystals . . 
• Copper inhibits the sulfhydryl groups on enzymes in important an f iox·i d an t systems including G-6-PD and 

glutathione reductase, reducing their free radical scavenging activities 
ACUTE POISONING 

• Acute hepatic necrosis 
• Hemolysis ➔ myoglobinuria, hemoglobinuria & acute renal failure 
• Methemoglobinemia, methemoglobinuria 

CHRONIC POISONING 
• Causes: Occupat ional hazard of workers in copper factories and Wilson's disease 

Clinical features 
• Anemia 
• Green or purple line on gums (Clapton's line) 

• Vineyard's sprayer's lung - Bordeaux mixture (copper sulfate+ lime) is sprayed in vineyards to control 
fungal growth on grapes, melons, and berries. When it is chronically inhaled by sprayers, they develop 
interstitial pulmonary fibrosis and histiocytic granulomas 

• Greenish discoloration of hair 

• Chalcosis lentis - deposition of copper in the cornea or lens causing greenish-brown discoloration 
• Kayser-Fleischer ring - deposition of copper in the Descemet's membrane of cornea 

Treatment 

• D-penicillamine - chelating agent of choice 
• Hemodialysis - ineffective 

7. lfosTMORTEM APPEARANCES OF COPPER SULPHATE POISONING! 
• Skin - yellow due to jaundice 

• In chronic cases - green line may be seen in the gums 

• The skin of contact and mucous membranes of mouth and tongue may have a bluish or bluish green 
appearance 

• Bluish or bluish green froth may be seen at mouth and nostrils 
• Bluish or bluish green appearance of mucous membranes of esophagus & stomach 
• Mucous membrane of the stomach is congested with desquamation and hemorrhagic spots 
• Degenerative changes in the liver and kidney may be seen 



I METALLIC & NON-METALLIC IRRITANTS i r 

Exposure 
, Occupational exposure occurs from the maintenance and cleaning of ducts and flues at smelting plants 

where thallium particles can be breathed in. 
• Accidental ingestion of rat poison 
• Cocaine, heroin and herbal products may be contaminated with thallium. 
• Eating food such as fish and shellfish contaminated with thallium. 

Mechanism of action: 5 major toxicologic effects: 
• Disruption of potassium-dependent processes 
• Riboflavin sequestration 
• Interference with cysteine residues 
• Ribosomal inhibition 
• Myelin sheath injury 

Clinical features 
• Acute poisoning - Gastrointestinal, neurological, and dermatological features 
• Chronic poisoning - neurological features predominate 
• Severe stomach pain, nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea 
• Painful and rapidly progressing peripheral neuropathies (numbness, tingling and pain, especially on the 

soles and palms) 
• Sudden hair loss - primarily affects the scalp, temporal parts of the eyebrows and the eyelashes, 
• Diplopia, abnormal color vision, ptosis, VII CN palsy 
• Skin rash , redness and scaling of the palms and soles, acneform or pustular eruptions of the face 
• Mees lines (transverse white lines on the nails) appear in the nail plate 

Treatment 
• Initial stabilization - assess ABCs (Airway, Breathing, Cardiovascular system) 
• Prussian blue 
• Activated charcoal 
• Potassium chloride 

9. ~RO~T OXICl!'J 
• Iron is found in many over-the-counter (OTC) multivitamins. 
• Iron toxicity from intentional or accidental ingestion is a common poisoning. 
• Iron overdose has been one of the leading causes of poisoning deaths in children< 6 years 
• Iron tablets are particularly tempting to young children because they look like candy. 
• Iron overdose in adults is typically a suicide attempt 

Pathophysiology 
• Ingestion of less than 20 mg/kg of elemental iron is non-toxic. 
• Ingestion of 20 mg/kg to 60 mg/kg results in moderate symptoms. 
• Ingestion of> 60 mg/kg can result in severe toxicity and lead to severe morbidity and mortality 

Clinical features 
• Iron toxicity is classified as corrosive or cellular . 
• Corrosive: Ingested iron can cause direct caustic injury to the gastrointestinal mucosa, resulting in 

nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, and diarrhea. 
► Significant fluid and blood loss can lead to hypovolemia. 
► nd 

Hemorrhagic necrosis of gastrointestinal mucosa can lead to hematemesis, perforation, a
peritonitis.

13. IODINE TOXICITY 

• The chief use of iodine 1s for its ant iseptic property. 
• It is bactericidal, sporicidal, protozoacidal, cyst icidal, and virucidal. 

• Radioactive iodine is used in t he t reatment of t hyroid cancer. 
• The ant iarrhythmic drug amiodarone releases iodine and may cause either t hyrotoxicosis or 

hypothyroidism after prolonged use 
• Iodine toxicity most commonly results from over-consumption of dietary supplement s. 
• Foods cont aining iodine include iodized salt, drinking water, milk, certa in seafood, and seaweeds 

Clinical features 
• Inhalation of iodine vapor - severe pulmonary irritat ion, which can lead t o pulmonary edema. 

• Skin and eye exposures - corrosive burns. 
• Ingestion - corrosive gastroenterit is wit h vomiting, hematemesis, and diarrhea ➔ volume loss and 

circulatory collapse. Mucous membranes are usually stained brown, and the vomitus may be blue if 
st archy foods are already present in t he stomach 

• Chronic iodine toxicity may lead to thyroiditis, hypothyroidism, hyperthyroidism, and thyroid papillary 
cancer 

Treatment 
• Correction of thyroid abnormalities 
• There is no specific antidote to iodine poisoning 
• Management is largely support ive.

• An occupat ional hazard in persons working in welding, galvanizing, smelting indust ries 
• Due to.  inhalat ion of fumes of metals - ZINC (most common) , copper,  magne s1· um, ·i ron, ch  rom·1 um,

• Excessive salivation and metallic taste in the mouth 
• It relieves in weekend (no exposure) and again starts on Monday (hence M onday feve r) 
• Treatment: Supplemental oxygen and supportive t reat ment

• Hydrofluoric ac,d - the only acid that causes liquefact,on necrosis.

complications & Sl'quelae 
• Permanent scars on skin 
• Gastrointestinal hemorrhage 
• Esophageal and gastric perforation 
• Strictures of esophagus and stomach [hourglass stomach] 
• Chemic.al peritonitis - due to entry of gastric contents into the peritoneum following perforation. 
• Pyloric stenosis 
• Sepsis 
• Increased risk of cancer 

Bedside test. Litmus paper test 
• The litmus test is a quick method of determining whether a liquid or gaseous solution is acidic or basic 

(alkaline) 
• Place a drop of liquid sample on a small strip of paper 
• Red paper turns blue when the pH is alkaline, while blue paper turns red when the pH turns acidic 

Treatment 
• Maintain airway, breathing and circulation. 

Remove all contaminated clothes and irrigate exposed skin and eyes with running water or saline . 
• Acid should be immediately diluted by giving a glass of milk or water. 
• Demulcents, like olive oil, milk, egg white, starch water or butter can be given. 
• Neutralizing agents (alkalis) should not be given as the resulting exothermic reaction may cause more 

damage. 
Prednisolone 60 mg/day may be given in divided doses. (to prevent strictures) 

• Correct ci rculatory shock, IV fluids and blood products are administered in the event of significant 
bleeding or vomiting. 
Antibiotics should be given if evidence of perforation exists. 

• Morphine to relieve pain .

• Emesis 
• Gastric lavage 
• Activated charcoal

n in

 1  Carbolic acid poisoning. 

CARBOLIC ACID (PH-ENOL) POISONING1 

Acute poisoning 
• Also known as carbollsm 
• Numbness of skin due to damaged nerve endings. 
• A white eschar forms and falls off in 7-10 days leaving a brown stain. 
• Hot burning pain from mouth to stomach which is followed by tingling anesthesia. 
• Strong odor of phenol in breath. 
• Corrosion of lips, tongue and mouth. 
• Painful speech and deglutition. 

h • Initially respiratory alkalosis (due to stimulation of respiratory centre) followed by metabolic acidosis. 
• When there is retching and vomiting - the fumes will be inhaled resulting in - laryngeal edema, 

Tracheitis, bronchitis, and Pulmonary edema. 
• Hypotension, arrhythmias. 
• Initially CNS stimulation, later depression. 
• Hemolysis, Methemoglobinemia in severe cases . 

Carboluria 
• Urine is scanty and contains albumin and hemoglobin. 
• Phenol is converted to pyrocatechol and hydroquinone in the liver before it is excreted in the urine. 
• On exposure to air, they are further oxidized giving the dark smoky green color. 
• Later it becomes almost black on standing. 
• This is called carboluria. 

Test for phenol: The Folin-Ciocalteu reagent (FCR) test: The principle is the reduction of the FCR in the presence 
of phenolics resulting in the production of molybdenum-tungsten blue that is measured spectrophotometrically 
at 760 nm and the intensity increases linearly with the concentration of phenolics. 

Treatment 
• Contact poisoning: irrigation with polyethylene glycol solution. 
• Ingestion 

,, Airway management and Stabilization. 
,, Gastric lavage can be done with 10% glycerin or olive oil (only acid poisoning where it is 

indicated) 
-,, Normal sa line with sodium bicarbonate - to treat shock and acidosis. 
, Hemodialysis - in case of renal failure. 



coRROSIVES 

Chronic poisoning or Phenol marasmus 
• Anorexia, weight loss, dark urine and pigmentation of skin and sclera 
• Yellowish discoloration of cartilage, sclera, skin (and intervertebral discs) due to the deposition of 

Hydroquinone and pyrocatechol - a condition called ochronosis. 
• This is also seen in alkaptonuria 

POSTMORTEM FINDINGS: 
• Smell of phenol from the body. 
• Corrosion of skin around mouth . 
• Tongue - grey and thickened. 
• Stomach mucosa - coagulative necrosis resulting thickening and leathery appearance. 
• Kidneys - renal tubular necrosis. 
• Lungs and brain - congested and edematous. 
• Carbolic acid delays putrefaction.

3. NITRIC ACID POISONINc;l 
Clinical features & Management of Mineral acids (Sulphuric acid, Hydrochloric acid and Nitric acid) are all same. 
Refer to Essay Q. No: 1 

Differentiating features of Nitric acid 

• Xanthoproteic reaction: Concentrated nitric acid reacts with organic matter or tissue and produces 
yellowish discoloration of the tissue due to the formation of picric acid 

• Corrosion is less severe when compared to Sulphuric acid 

• Corroded areas, stains on clothing and teeth appear yellow 

Bedside tests: Brown ring test or Ferrous sulphate test can be performed by adding iron (11) sulfate to a solution 
of a nitrate, then slowly adding concentrated sulfuric acid such that the acid forms a layer below the aqueous 
solution. A brown ring will form at the junction of the two layers, indicating the presence of the nitrate ion.

5. 'oXALICACID POISONING' 

• In the form of oxalate, it is present in spinach, rhubarb, cabbage. 
• Do not damage the skin but destroys mucous membranes of the digestive tract. 
• Contact with mucosa (eye, mouth) ➔ more damage. Gives a scalded appearance. 
• Ingestion: Burning pain in the mouth, throat and epigastrium, Dysphagia, odynophagia, Intense thirst, 

eructations, Vomiting of brownish black substance [acid hematin; coffee grounds vomit] 
• It extracts tissue calcium ➔ Hypocalcemia. 

, Tetany 
;... Numbness & tingling of fingers and toes, paresthesia, 

:,... Chvostek sign 
• Tubular necrosis & excretion of envelop shaped crystals of calcium oxalate (Oxaluria) 

• Shock 

1><! 

Envelope Shaped Crystals of 
CALC IUM OXALATE 

Management 

• Gastric lavage with calcium salts (gluconate) or milk 
• Antidote: Limewater, calcium lactate, calcium gluconate or calcium chloride 

• 10% Calcium gluconate IV - 10 ml at frequent intervals 
• Dialysis for renal failure

SHORT NOTES: 

1. 'TOXALBUMIN"' /PHYTOTOXl_!',!J 
• A Toxalbumin is a super-toxic protein that resembles bacterial toxin in action 
• It is a Ribosome Inactivating Protein (RIP), that inactivates ribosomes and thus stops protein synthesis 
• There are 3 types: type I, II, and Ill 
• Plant toxalbumin or phytotoxin 

, Ricin in Ricinus communis 
, Crotin in Croton t iglium 
, Abrin in Abrus precatorius 

• Animal toxalbumin 
, Snake 
, Scorpion venoms 

• Also ca lled fabricated bruise or artificial bruise 
• A pseudobruise or artificial bruise is caused by the application of a chemical substance on the skin which 

produces discoloration that simulates a bruise 
• Toxins commonly used to produce pseudobruise - Juic.e of marking nut, Calotropis 

Medico-legal importance 
• Artificial bruise can be produced to get more compensation 
• To falsely accuse an individual of revenge

3. ABRUS PRECATORIUS*J 
/RATI suj) 

• Common names: Rosary bead, Rosary pea, Rati 
• Abrus precatorius is a plant irritant poison. 
• The seeds are egg shaped, bright scarlet colour with a large black spot at one end. 
• Seeds have a thick shell which is not digestible. Thus they are harmless when taken without crushing. 
• Active principle: Abrin [a toxalbumin similar to viper snake venom] 

• Mechanism of action: Abrin inhibits protein synthesis . 



pLANT IRRITANTS 

Clinical features: 

, GI irritation, gastroenteritis, rectal bleeding, 
,.. Cold perspiration, trembling of hands. 
,.. Circulatory collapse, hemolysis, agglutination of red cells, hemorrhagic diathesis. 

Medicolegal importance: 
Suis: 

, The seeds are crushed and made into paste. 
~ From the paste, sharp pointed needle like spikes or su1s are made. 
,.. Two needles (to resemble fangs of snake) are inserted into a wooden stick, with their sharp 

edges pointing outside. 
, Then a blow is struck on the cattle so that the suis are driven into the body 
, Inflammation, edema, Oozing of hemorrhagic fluid and necrosis occurs around the site of 

puncture. 
,.. The animal becomes drowsy, does not take food and dies in a few days. 
,.. The signs and symptoms resemble viper snake bite . 

Used as an Abortifac ient, Cattle poison and arrow poison. 

Treatment: 

Gastric lavage with Activated charcoal. 

l ocal inJected cases: Needle should be dissected out. 
Antiabrin - given subcutaneously.

28. ANIMAL IRRITANTS 
Competencies achieved after reading this chapter 

IMfl•M Describe features and management of Snake bite, scorpion sting, bee and wasp sting and spider bite 

ESSAYS: 

1. a) Define Ophitoxemia. What are the common causes of poisonous snake bite in India? Write in detail about 
the clinical features, and treatment of Cobra envenomation. 
b) Classify the poisons belonging to the 'Irritant' group with one example each. Describe the first aid measures 
and the hospital management of cobra bite. 

Classification of Irritant poisons: Refer to Chapter 23. Essay Q No.1 

Snake bite causing envenomation is called Ophitoxemia 

Common causes of venomous snake bite in India (Big four in India) 
• Common krait 
• Indian cobra 
• Russel's viper 
• Saw scaled viper 

COBRA ENVENOMATION 
Cobra venom is neurotoxic 

• Dry bite - When the snake bites, but venom fails to get injected, it is called dry bite. Approximately 20% 
of all snakebites are dry bites. Exception: 80% of sea snake bites are dry bites. 

General features 
• Fright is the most common feature in all snake bites, due to the fear of rapid and unpleasant death. 
• Hypotension 
• Feeble pulse 
• Rapid respiration 
• Declining consciousness 

FANG MARKS 
• Usually there will be two prominent fang marks (A) separated by 1-4 cm 
• •

A side swipe  
may produce a single puncture wound. 

• When the snake strikes multiple times, multiple puncture wounds will 
♦ B 

be present 
• Sometimes a single row of teeth marks will be present (B) 

Local symptoms 
• Local symptoms within 5-10 minutes 
• Local symptoms are mild when compared to viper envenomation. 
• Small reddish wheal 
• Burning pain and mild swelling at the site of bite 
• Oozing of blood-stained fluid 



I VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

Systemic features 

• Nausea, vomiting 

• Patient feels sleepy w ith slight intoxication 

• Ptosis - earliest neurologic symptom 

• Ophthalmoplegia - ext raocular muscle weakness, strabismus, diplopia 

• Later, paralysis of lower limbs, trunk, neck and head (head falli·n g forward ,  ·1 na bITI tY  t O r aise head) ' vocal 

cords (dysphonia), tongue, muscles of deglutition (dysphagia) 

• Complete paralysis after 2 hours 

• Convulsions, Coma 

• Death due to respiratory failure 

MANAGEMENT 
Field Management of Snake bite poisoning (FIRST AID) (mnemonic: R-1-GH-T) 

• R: Reassurance 

• I: Immobilization: bitten limb immobilized with a splint since any movement will hasten systemic 
absorption of the venom. 

• Clean the bite area . 
• Pressure immobilization - Sutherland Wrap: 

, Bandage applied over the wound which is loose enough to fit one finger in. 
,- The pressure should be maintained between 50 to 70 mmHg. 
,- This is done to occlude lymphatic circulation without interfering with arterial flow or venous 

drainage. 
,- Recommended for cobra and krait bites. 

,- Not recommended for bites by vipers whose venom can cause local necrosis. 
• GH: Get to Hospital immediately 

• T: Tell the doctor of any systemic symptoms such as ptosis t hat manifest on the way to hospital. 

DO NOT in SNAKE BITE: 

• Cut the wound. 
• Try to suck out venom. 
• Apply torniquet. 

• Giving oral foods (especially alcohol), 

Hospital management 

• Admission in ICU and monitor cardiac rhythm, oxygen saturation, urine output, vital signs. 
• Measure limb ci rcumferences every 15 minutes until swelling has st abilized 
• Fluid resuscitation 

• Vasopressors: Dopamine 

• "4eostigmine - effective in post-synaptic neurotoxin (Cobra) 
• Neostigmine - not effective in pre-synaptic neurotoxin (Krait, Russel Viper) 
• l\trop ne premeditation to counteract the muscarinic effects of neostigmine (cobra) 
• Wound care: Application of a dry, steri le dressing 

• Prophylactic antibiotics 



ANIMAL IRRITANTS 

pQLYVALENT ANTI-SNAKE VENOM 

• Covers the Big four in India (Common krait, Indian Cobra, Russel's viper, Saw scaled viper) 

• Key to the management 

• But not routine as it is hazardous . 

• Useful when given within 4 hours of bite 

• Initial dose depends upon the severity of envenomation 
• There is no good evidence to suggest children should receive either more ASV because of body mass or 

less in order to avoid adverse reactions 
• Hypersensit ivity testing before administration is not done as t here are no alternatives. 
• local administration of ASV near or at the bite site should not be done. 

During pregnancy, Monovalent ASV is preferred where the offending snake is ident ified, otherwise, 
polyvalent ASV venom should be administered 

Indications for administration of anti-snake venom 
• Haemostatic abnormalities 

, Spontaneous systemic bleeding 
,- Non-coagulable blood in 20 min whole blood clotting test (20 WBCT) 

• Neurotoxic signs: ptosis, external ophthalmoplegia dysphagia, paralysis 
• Cardiovascular abnormalities: Shock, cardiac arrhythmia 
• Renal abnormalities: 

,- Acute renal failure: oliguria/anuria, rising blood creatinine/ urea, hyperkalemia. 
, Hemoglobin-/myoglobinuria: dark brown urine. 

• Local envenomation 
,- Local swelling involving more than half of the bitten limb OR cro!'>sing a join (in the absence of a 

tourniquet) 
, Rapid extension of swelling (for example beyond the wrist or ankle within a few hours of bites on 

the hands or feet). 
, Development of an enlarged tender lymph node draining the bitten limb. 

2. Define Ophitoxemia. Write in detail about the clinical features, and treatment of krait envenomation. 

KRAIT ENVENOMATION 
• Krait venom is neurotoxic 
• Most patients are bitten while asleep on the floor and present in the morning with paralysis 

General features & Fang marks: Refer to Previous question 
Local features 

• Local signs are minimal or insignificant 
• Minimal or no local swelling 

Systemic features 
• Abdominal pain 
• Intense drowsiness and intoxication 
• Ptosis - earliest neurologic symptom 
• Ophthalmoplegia - extraocular muscle weakness, strabismus, diplopia 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

• Later, paralysis of lower limbs, trunk, neck and head (head falling forward, inability to raise head), vocal 
cords (dysphonia), tongue, muscles of deglutition (dysphagia) 

• Complete paralysis after 2 hours 
• Coma 
• Death due to respiratory failure 

MANAGEMENT: Refer to previous question 

3 Define Ophitoxemia. Write in detail about the clinical features, and treatment of viper snake envenomation. 
Snake bite causing envenomation is called Ophitoxemia 

VIPER ENVENOMATION1 
• Viper venom is hemotoxic (Vasculotoxic) 
• General features & Fang marks: Refer to Essay Q no. 1 

Local features 
• Marked local symptoms - reddish and painful swelling 
• Swelling quickly spreads to involve entire limb or adjacent trunk (malignant edema) 
• Persistent bleeding from the bite site 
• Blisters in and around bite site and spread to involve entire limb 
• Extensive necrosis of skin, subcutaneous tissues, muscles in some cases 
• Regional lymphadenopathy 
• Compartment syndrome 
• Gangrene of the foot 

Systemic features 

• Hematologic abnormalities are characteristic of viper envenomation : elevated BT & CT, disseminated 
intravascular coagulation, Primary pathological fibrinolysis . 

• Hemorrhagic manifestations 
,- Epistaxis 
).. Hemoptysis 
-,, Bleeding from gums and f loor of mouth 
,, Hematuria 
,, Ecchymosis or Purpuric spots on the skin 
,, Hemorrhages in the GIT, Genitourinary tract 
).. Anterior pituitary hemorrhage 
, lntracranial hemorrhages 
,. Hemorrhage into the renal calyces 
).- lntravascular hemolysis 

• Paralysis is not a feature of viper bite. 
• Dilated pupils 
• Hypotension, tachycardia 
• Cause of death: Hemorrhagic shock

MLI of snake bite: 

• Snake bite is mostly accidental. 
• Suicidal or Homicidal are rare. Egyptian Queen Cleopatra committed suicide by snake bite 
• Cattle poison: A poisonous snake and a banana are placed in an earthen pot. The vessel is heated. The 

snake gets irritated and bites the fruit and injects venom into the pulp. The pulp is then crushed and 
smeared on a rag. The rag is thrust into the animal's rectum with a bamboo stick. This method is used to 
obtain hide from cattle for cheap prices.

3. '81TES OF SEA SNAKES 

• Venom is myotoxic 

• Little or no local signs 

• Generalized aching, stiffness and tenderness of the muscles 

• Polymyositis 

• Trismus 

• Rhabdomyolysis - Myoglobin and K+ are released from damaged skeletal muscles . 
• Hyperkalemia 

• Progressive flaccid paralysis 

• Urine - dark brown [Coca-Cola colored]. Tests for Hb positive [due to presence of myoglobin] 

• Cause of death: renal failure due to Myoglobinuria

5, SCORPION STING/SCORPION ENVENOMATION 
- - :J 

• Scorpion venom is a toxalbumin 
• It is hemolytic or neurotoxic 
• The toxicity is more than that of snakes, but only a small quantity is injected 
• Scorpion venom opens neuronal sodium channels which causes an autonomic storm resulting in the 

release of massive amounts of catecholamines from the adrenals 
• Most of the stings occur on the hands and feet 
• Mortality is rare except in children 

Clinical features 
• Sym pathetic overdrive symptoms predominate, causing tachycardia, arrhythmia, hypertension, 

hyperthermia, and pulmonary edema. 
• Parasympathetic symptoms include hypotension, bradycardia, salivation, lacrimation, urination, 

defecation, and gastric emptying 
• Common signs: Mydriasis, nystagmus, hypersa livation, dysphagia, and restlessness 

Management 

• Immobilize the limb 
• Tetanus toxoid injection 
• Local wound care 
• Pain control by local anesthetic injection 
• Prazosin 30 µg/kg/dose (1 mg for adult, 500 µg for children) is given orally

/. oefme drunkenness. What are the various stages of alcoholic intake? Write in detail about the clinical 
examination of a person who has consumed ethyl alcohol. 

DRUNKiN~ES~ 
Drunkenness is a consequence of drinking intoxicating liquors to such an extent as to alter the normal condition 
of an individual and significantly reduce his capacity for rational action and conduct. 
STAGES OF ALCOHOL INTOXICATION 
Stage of excitement 

• Blood alcohol level SO - 150 mg/100 ml 
• There is a sense of euphoria in a non-addict person 
• This is due to inhibition of higher inhibitory centres of brain - resulting in excitation 
• Increased self-confidence and lack of self-control 
• The person may disclose secrets 
• Impairment of cognition, sensory perception, judgment 
• Slurring of speech, drowsiness, impaired memory 
• Visual acuity is impaired 
• Desire for sex is increased, but performance is impaired 

Stage of lncoordination 
• Blood alcohol level 150 to 250 mg/100 ml 
• Becomes cheerful, excitable, quarrelsome or irritable, due to further depression of higher centers 
• Face is flushed 
• Rapid pulse 
• Pupils dilated and there is sluggish reaction to light 
• Alcohol gaze nystagmus 
• In-coordination of fine and skilled movements 
• Temperature becomes subnormal 

gage of coma 

• Blood alcohol level > 350 mg/100 ml 
• Thick and slurred speech 
• Marked incoordination 
• The person feels giddy with staggering gait and may fall 
• Pupils are constricted. But if the person is stimulated (pinching the skin or slapping gently), pupils dilate 

for a while and slowly constricts. This is called Mc Ewan sign 
• The person may become comatose

• Maintain Airway, Breathing, Circulation

1 Define 'Drunkenness.' l xp/ain how the state of drunkenness renders the driving of a vehicle dangerous. What is 
Widmark's formula? 

Drunkenness definition: Refer to Essay Q. No. 1 

IMPACT OF ALCOHOL CONSUMPTION ON SAFE DRIVING. 

• Alcohol use impairs driving skills and increases accident risk . 

• While driving under the influence of alcohol, the risk of having an accident ca using injury or death 
increases exponentially 

• Even with a small amount of alcohol assumption, drivers are twice likely to be involved in traffic accidents 
than sober drivers 

Judgment 
• Alcohol consumption reduces a person's ability to t hink clearly, reason, make smart decisions, and 

exercise caution-all necessary to drive safely. 
Alcohol decreases the ability to judge the car's position on the road, or the location of other vehicles, 
center line, or road signs. 

Concentration 
• Alcohol limits a driver's ability to concentrate on mult iple tasks such as speed, weather conditions, and 

t he actions of motorists around him-all critical for safe driving 

Comprehension 
• A drunk driver is less able to comprehend traffic signs, traffic signals, or a dangerous driving situation. 
• This can limit his ability to respond appropriately and slow down, stop suddenly, or swerve out of the way 

of a crash . 
Coordination 

• Alco-hol consumption affects a driver's fine and gross motor skills. 
• This can limit hand, eye, and foot coordination, which also makes it more difficult to respond quickly in an 

emergency. 
Vi~ion alld hearing 

• When a person is drunk, his vision & hearing are impaired. . . 
. . . • • • d the ability to judge distances-necessary to avoid a rear-end 

• There Is a reduction In penphera 1 v1sIon an 
coll ision.

- . d ability to act quickly in response to 

• Alcohol can slow down a person's reflexes, comprehension, an 

changing driving conditions.

'wlDMARK'S FORMULA: 
'-- -

• Used for calculating the amount of alcohol ingested if his blood alcohol level is known and vice-versa. 

• Formula for blood: a=prc 
,.. 'a' - alcohol ingested (gm) 
,.. 'c' - concentration of alcohol in the blood 
,.. 'p' - weight of the body (kg) 
,.. ' r' -Widmark's constant. 

• Formula for urine: a=3/4 x prq, 
,- q - concentration of alcohol in the urine 

3. Define drunkenness. Describe the clinical features, treatment and postmortem appearances of methanol 
poisoning** 

Drunkenness definition: Refer to Essay Q. No. 1 

METHANOL POISONING 
• Methanol is a common ingredient in antifreeze, paints and solvents. 
• It is also used in photocopying fluid, shellacs, and windshield-washing fluids. 
• Poisoning usually follows ingestion of contaminated alcohol beverages or 'methylated spirits. 

Mechanism of action 

• Methanol is oxidized in the liver to formaldehyde by alcohol dehydrogenase 
• Formaldehyde is converted to formic acid by aldehyde dehydrogenase 
• Formic acid causes Retinal toxicity and metabolic acidosis 

Alcohol Aldehyde 
dehydro9enue dehydro9e11au 

Methanol -... Formaldehyde . F-THF-S.._ 
~ ' Formic acid 

~ CO2 + H 0 
2

Ethanol 
Fomeplzole 

Clinical features 

• General: nausea, vomiting, headache, dizziness, vertigo 
• GIT: abdominal cramps, pancreatitis 

• CVS: Tachycardia, hypotension, hypothermia 
• RS: dyspnea, cyanosis, breath smelling of alcohol 

• CNS: depression, delirium, convulsions, coma 

• Drunkenness is not as marked as seen in ethanol 
• Kidney: Acute tubular necrosis 

• High anion gap metabolic acidosis 



CNS DEPRESSANTS 

• Eyes: most characteristic features 
, Opt c nluritis and optic atrophy 
, Dilated and fixed pupil 
, Photophobia 
, Blurred vision (flashes of light, snowstorm or snow field vision - as if seeing through a snowstorm) 

, Central or peripheral scotoma 
Constricted visual fields 

, Decreased light perception 
Sudden complete loss of vision due to optic neuritis and optic atrophy 

• Cause of death: acidosis or Respiratory failure 

Treatment 
• Gastric lavage with sodium bicarbonate solution 
• Emesis can also be tried within 2 hours of consumption 

• Folinic acid or Folic acid can be given 
• Eth .... "lol ,s the ~pec,fic antidotr 

, Ethanol competes for the enzyme alcohol dehydrogenase 
, Thus, prevents metabolism of methanol which is excreted unchanged in urine 

• 4-methyl pyrazole or Forr ep,zole (competitive antagonist of alcohol dehydrogenase) is a newer antidote 

• Haemodialysis - to be done in severe poisoning 

Post-mortem appearances 

• Cyanosis - especially in the upper parts of the body 
• Stomach and duodenum - mucosa congested with hemorrhagic spots 

• Pancreas - hemorrhagic pancreatitis 

• Lungs - congestion, edema, desquamation of alveolar epithelium 

• Liver - fatty change 
• Kidney - tubular degeneration 
• Eyes - retinal edema, optic atrophy 

CNS - Cerebral oedema, even necrosis in the basal ganglia 
• Viscera for chemical analysis should be preserved in saturated saline solution

SHORT NOTES: 

1. rDELIRIUM TREMEN~* 

• Delirium, is a term used to describe an acute confusional state, is a transient global disorder of attention, 

with clouding of consciousness. 
• Delirium tremens is an acute organic psychosis seen during alcohol withdrawal 

• It is the most severe form of ethanol withdrawal 
• Usually manifest within 24-72 hours after the last alcoholic drink (but may occur up to 7-10 days lat er) 

• It can also occur following excessive consumption of alcohol 

Clinical features 

• Features are dramatic in onset 
• Profound global confusion is the hallmark of delirium tremens 

• Clouding of consciousness, disorientation, loss of recent memory 

• Coarse tremors of face, tongue and hands 

• Auditory, Visual or tactile hallucinations (often of snakes, bugs, etc) 

• Autonomic hyperactivity, diaphoresis, dehydration 

• Electrolyte disturbances (hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia) 
• Cardiovascular abnormalities. 
• Seizures 

• Tendency to commit suicide, homicide, violent assault, damage to property 
Treatment 

• Reassurance 

• Drug of choice : Diazepam IVS mg every S minutes 

• Thiamine - to prevent Wernicke's encephalopathy 

• Correction of fluid and electrolyte disturbances 

2 WERNICKE'S ENCEPHALOPAT~ 

• It is an alcohol induced acute thiamine deficiency 

• Caused by lesions in the medial thalamic nuclei, mammillary bod· d . 
ies, an superior cerebellar vermis

anisocoria (unequal size of pupils)

• 80% cases progress to Korsakoff's psychosis if untreat ed 
• Treatment: IV thiamine + glucose

4. ETHY f'\JE ,:;LYCOL DQISONING 

Ethylene glycol is the major constituent of antifreeze solutions 

• Polyethylene glycol is used for whole bowel irrigation (GIT decontamination) 

• Ethylene glycol itself is not toxic. Toxicity is due to its metabolites glycolic and oxalic acids 

Glycolic acid causes metabolic acidosis 

Clinical features: Refer to Chapter 23 . Short note Q No. 4 

S. HERO 

Chemical names - diacetylmorphine, diamorphine 

A semisynthetic opioid drug synthesized from morphine 

The enhanced euphoria effect when it is administered parenterally is ca lled rush 

• IV administration of heroin is called mainlining, and users are known as mainliners 

It is called Brown sugar as it acquires a brown color when mixed with coffee powder, coco or jaggery 

Combination of heroin and cocaine is called speedball 

• Chasing the dragon: inhalation of heroin by heating it on aluminum foil using a hand-hold flame 

Drugs used for detoxificat ion: Methadone, Buprenorphine, LAAM (Levo-a-acetylmethadol) 

Opioid antagonists: Naltrexone, Naloxone

It inhibit s aldehyde dehydrogenase.

This symptoms resulting in dislike of alcohol by the patient.

, DEJ.IRIANTS 

4. COCAINE POISONING 

• Cocaine synonyms: coke, snow, she (he - heroin) 
• MOA: Blocks reuptake of biogenic amines (dopamine, norepinephrine, epinephrine) and causes an 

hyperadrenergic state 
ACUTE POISONING 

• Elevated pulse, blood pressure, respiration and temperature 
• CNS: Cerebrovascular accidents (frequent cause of stroke in < 45 years), subarachnoid or intracerebral 

hemorrhage. 
• CVS: myocardial infarction 

CHRONIC POISONING 

• Chronic abuse of cocaine is called cocainism 
• Irresistible craze, crave, or impulse to intoxication by cocaine - Cocainomania 

• A chronic coca ine addict can tolerate up to 10 grams per day 
• It causes blackening of tongue and teeth 
• Ulceration and perforation of nasal septum or soft palat e are also seen (because it is inhaled by snorting) 

• The person appears emaciated, pale with sunken eyes 
• He loses interest in fam ily, friends, work, sex and develops psychosis 
• Magnan's symptom is characteristic of Cocainism. It is a type of tactile hallucination. There is a feeling as 

if grains of sand are lying under the skin or small insects are creeping on the skin (formication). 

5. ~ALLUCINOGENS (PSYCHED!!.!_CS) 

The commonly used Hallucinogens are 
• LYSERGIC ACID DIETHYLAMIDE (LSD) 

, Dry mouth, sweating, mydriasis 
, Tachycard ia, tremor, ataxia 
, Hallucinations 
, Feeling of depersonalization 
,, Hyperacusis, synesthetic experiences (stimulation of one sensory modality is perceived as 

sensation in a different modality, e.g., sound produces a sensation of colour), changes in body 

image and time space perceptions. 
, Intense emotions, panic-like symptoms and paranoid reactions are common 

• Phencyclidine (angel dust) 
, Mood changes, intense anxiety, perceptual disturbances, paranoid reactions and motor 

stereotypes 
• Ketamine {a short acting anesthetic) 

• Mescaline 
• Magic mushrooms {contains psilocybin) 
• Methylene dioxy methamphetamine (MOMA, Ecstasy) 

, Drug-induced compulsive dancing 

, Hyponatremia 
, Hyperthermia. 

Flash backs or bad trips: re-experiencing, following cessation of use of a hallucinogen, of one or more of the 

Perceptual symptoms that were earlier experienced while on the drug

ASPHYXIANTS 

Rebound effect 
• CO binds cardiac & skeletal myoglobin, with a 3 times greater affinity than Hb. 

• Carboxy-myoglobin dissociation is slower than that of COHb due to its increased affinity. 

• There is a possible rebound effect with late release of CO from myoglobin and its subsequent binding to 

hemoglobin. 

Clinical features of acute carbon monoxide poisoning 

COHblevels Severity i Signs anc:I symi:,toms 
< 10% No signs 
10-20% Mild Mild headache, breathlessness on exertion 

20-30 % Throbbing headache, irritability, emotional instability 

30-40% Moderate Nausea, vomiting, dizziness, confusion, cherry red color of 

skin, dimness of vision, chest pain 

40 - 50% Collapse 

50-60% Severe Convulsions, Tachycardia 

60-80% Coma, diminished reflexes 

>80% Rapid death 

Diagnosis 

• COHb levels in blood: Normal - 1% in non-smokers & 4% in smokers; levels> 2 % in non-smokers and> 

10% in smokers indicate CO exposure 

• Pulse-oximetry shows decreased oxygen saturation 

• Arterial blood gas analysis - metabolic acidosis 

• ECG: ST depression or elevation, T wave flattening or inversion, arrhythmia 

• Blood containing CO is pinkish 
Treatment 

• Maintain a patent airway and assist ventilation, if necessary. 

• Remove the victim from exposure. 

• The half-life of the carboxy-hemoglobin (COHb) complex is about 4-5 hours in room air but is reduced 

dramatically by high concentrations of oxygen. 

• Administer 100% oxygen 
• Hyperbaric oxygen (HBO) can provide 100% oxygen under higher than atmospheric pressures, further 

shortening the half-life of COHb 

• Convulsions can be treated with IV diazepam 

Post-mortem appearances 

• Cherry red (Pink) color of skin - well appreciated in fair skinned individuals in the areas of post-mortem 

staining and extremities 

• Cutaneous blisters {anoxic blebs) 

• Cherry red color of blood and tissues 

• Necrosis and cavitation of globus pallidus and putame in basal ganglia are the most characteristic 

lesions 

• Sample of blood should be collected from peripheral vein for chemical analysis. The blood sample should 

be sealed off with a layer of liquid paraffin on top to prevent escape of CO.

.'. Describe the Mechanism of action signs, symptoms, treatment and postmortem findings in a case of Cyanide 

(Hydrocyan,c acid) poisoning** 

CY . c- "'"'"-C'. 1•Jr 

• Cyanide is a highly toxic chemical used widely in research and commercial laboratories and many industries 

Sources 
Its gaseous form, hydrogen cyanide, is an important component of smoke in fires 

Cigarette smoking 

Burning of plastics 

Cyanide-generating glycosides are also found in some plants 

, Seeds of Apple, apricot, plum (amygdalin) 

Roots of cassava, lima beans (linamarin) 

Grains of sorghum (dhurrin) 

• Cyanide is generated by the breakdown of mtroprusside (a potent anti-hypertensive), and poisoning can 

result from rapid high-dose infusions 

Cyanide is also formed by metabolism of acetonitrile, a solvent found in some nail polish removers 

Me ha r of 1cfo n 
• Cyanide is rapidly absorbed by inhalation, skin absorption, or ingestion. 

• It disrupts cellular function by inhibiting cytochrome oxidase and preventing cellular oxygen utilization 

(histotoxic anoxia) 
lin c If tu e 

Cyanide is the os nr'd of all poisons 

Inhalation - immediate loss of consciousness, followd by convulsions and death 

Ingestion - slower onset of action 

• CNS: headache, anxiety, ataxia, confusion, convulsions, coma 

CVS: initially bradycardia and hypertension, followed by tachycardia, hypotension and arrhythmias 

RS : dyspnea, tachypnea, pulmonary edema 

GIT: ingestion causes nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain 

The odour of " 1 ter almonds" may be detected on the victim's breath or in vomitus 

Skin: Jnrh red colour of skin cmd mucou_ membrane is characteristic due to increases oxygen saturation 

of venous blood 
r r Cr t 

Remove the victim from exposure 

For suspected cyanide poisoning due to nitroprusside infusion, stop the infusion. 

For cyanide ingestion, administer activated charcoal

• Hydrocyanic acid is a vegetable acid present in many fruits (almond, apple, apricot, cherry, plum, pear) , 

spinach, bamboo shoots and plants like cassava. 
It is present in the form of a glycoside - amygdalin 
Amygdalin is present along with an enzyme called emulsin. 
In the small intestine, the bacteria convert amygdalin into cyanide in the presence of emulsin.

1. METHY 
MIC is a chemical used in the manufacture of polyurethane foam, pesticides & plastics 

• Tobacco smoke from some brands of cigarettes also contains MIC, approximately 4 µg per cigarette. 
• It has a strong odor, but it can begin to make people sick before the chemical can be smelled. 

MU . .,. Bno c a ar:ed 
• Occurred during the night of December 2 and 3, 1984. 
• Nearly 40 metric tons of MIC was released from the Union Carbide pesticide plant 

Over 2500 people residing in adjacent areas c.1ec o p lmonary edema within 3 days of the accident.

• Difficulty breathing

1 Describe the signs, symptoms, treatment and postmortem findings in a case of Cascabela thevetia poisoning. 

~AS_~ABELA THEVETIA/THEVfrlA PERUVIANA; 

• Common name: yellow oleander 

• Cascabela thevetia is a cardiac poison. 

• All parts of the plant are poisonous. 

• Fatal dose: 5-10 seeds, 5-10 leaves, 20 g of root 

Active principles: Cerebrin, Thevetin, Thevetoxin, Peruvoside 

Clinical features (common for all cardiac poisons) 

• Genera l

:BARBITURATE:. POISONING) 
Therapeutic. uses 

• Sedative 
• Hypnotic 

• Pre-operative sedation 
• Anti -epileptics 

Mechanrsm of i.lc ion 

• Barbiturates are CNS depressants 

• It acts synergistically with alcohol which is also a CNS depressant 
• They facilitate and prolong the inhibitory effects of GABA and glycine 
• GABA is the major inhibitory neurotransmitter in the CNS 

Clinical features 

• CNS depression 

,. Sedation and hypnosis 

,,.., Confusion, delirium, hallucinations 
,, Slurred speech, ataxia, lethargy, headache 
,. Positive Babinski sign 

,. Decreased deep tendon reflexes 
Hypothermia 

,. Coma 

• Eyes
