r PHARMACOLOGICAL TOXICOLOGY, FOOD POISONING, DRUG DEPENDENCE, MISCELLANEOUS \ 
• Skin 

,. Cutaneous blisters (barbiturate blisters barb burns) 

' Seen at site of friction/pressure . E.g. interdigital clefts, buttocks, back of thighs, calves and 

forearms, inner aspects of knees and ankles 

,. Self-limiting lesions; heal in 1-2 wks 

, Inflammation usually absent 

• Barbiturate automatism - accidental poisoning in persons who take barbiturates for treatment of 

seizures and also in addicts 

Treatne t 

• Maintain: Airway, Breathing & Circulation 

• Provide oxygen continuously to improve cyanosis 

• Treat hypothermia by slow rewarming 

• For hypovolemia or shock - give IV fluids and vasopressors 

• Gastric lavage: can be done up to 6-12 hours of ingestion 

• Activated charcoal can be given 

• Emesis - contraindicated because of possibility of impending coma 

• There is no spec1f1c an11dote for barbiturates 

• Forced alkaline diuresis: useful in phenobarbitone poisoning 

• Haemodialysis 
• Exchange transfusion 

Post mortem findings 

• Cyanosis of fingers and toes 

• Froth at mouth and nose 

• Barbiturate blisters - on the dependent parts of the body (buttocks, back of thighs and calves) 

• Stomach - may contain capsules or tables; mucosa congested and eroded 

• Lungs - congested, pulmonary edema 

2· a) Define substance dependence and substance addiction. 

b) Classify drugs of dependence and abuse. 

c) List the complications of drug abuse. 
d) Describe what is "body packer" and "body stuffer" syndrome. 

SUBSTANCE DEPENDENCE AND SUBSTANCE ADDICTION: 

• Dependence is defined as a psychological and physical reliance on a substance and losing control of 

substance abuse. 
• Addiction - altered behavior due to compulsive substance use, despite harmful consequences. (cost 

them personal and professional obligations and relationships, financial stability, and more) 

• Users can become addicted to more than one substance, and they can also develop addictions to sex, 

gambling, food, porn, love, and technology. 

• Someone struggling with addiction may start mentally or physically declining over time. 

Now-a-days, Substance use disorder (SUD) is a preferred term (for both dependence & addiction)

~ RMACOLOGICAL TOXICOLOGY, FOOD POISONING, DRUG DEPENDENCE, MISCELLANEOUS \ 
BODY PACKER SYNDROME: 

• Body packers are carriers of illicit drugs (usually heroin or cocaine) for smuggling across international 
borders. 

• Drugs may be packed in balloons, capsules, condoms, fingers of rubber gloves, or condoms and ingested . 

• Antidiarrheals, anti-propulsives and antiemetics are also taken to reduce gut motility during 
transportation . 

• When the body packer reaches the country of destination, he takes a laxative, defecates, retrieves the 
packets and delivers them to the drug dealer. 

• People doing this are called body packers, (drug) mules, swallowers, or couriers . 

• Body packer syndrome - accidental and unintentional poisoning with illicit drugs in these persons . 

• Accidental poisoning occurs due to bursting or leaking of packets . 

• Sometimes, drugs may passively diffuse through the layers of packing and cause poisoning. 
Plain X-ray 

• Double-condom sign - Well-defined, homogeneous oval densities surrounded by a translucent rim 

• Rosette sign - air trapped in the knot where the condom is tied . 

Management: Whole bowel irrigation - to remove packets 

BODY STUFFER SYNDROME: 
• When a person who possesses an il legal drug is arrested, he swallows them to conceal the evidence. 

• This is called Body stuffer. 
• Drugs may be concealed in the ears, mouth, nose, vagina or rectum (body pushers). 

• These drugs are not wrapped properly and result in intoxication w ithin few hours. 

SHORT NOTES: 

---- --
1. BENZODIAZEPINE _!:_OISONIN~ 

• Benzodiazepines (BZDs) - the most commonly prescribed medications for anxiety, sedation & sleep. 
• Overdose can be intentional in suicidal patients; accidental in combination w ith other central nervous 

system (CNS) depressants, such as alcohol and opioids 
• Older people, who commonly have diminished drug clearance and polypharmacy, are at especially high 

risk of overdose 
Clinical features: 

• Primarily present with CNS depression ranging from mild drowsiness to a coma-like, stuporous state. 
• Classic presentation isolated BZD overdose: CNS depression (excessive sedation) with normal vital signs 

• Anterograde amnesia 
• Respiratory depression . the most important adverse effect requiring immediate intervention. 

• Ataxia _ most common sign of toxicity in children 
• Respiratory compromise occurs in less than 10% of pediatric cases 

• BZD overdoses are very rarely fatal, 
Treatment 

• Emergency and supportive measures: Maintain an open airway and assist ventilation if necessary. 

• Specific antidote: FLUMAZENIL 



~, VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

2. OPIUM POISONING 
• Opiates are naturally occurri·n g compounds deri·v ed f rom t h e J· U·t ce o f the  Po ppy Popover somniferum 
• Opioids refers t o derivatives of naturally occurri·n g opi·u m (e .g., morp hm' e, he  roin,  codeine'  hydrocodone) 

as well as new, syntheti.c  opi•a te analogs (e.g., fentany1, b utorp ha noI,  mepe ridine , and methadone) 
Mild or moderate overdose: 

• Pin-point pupils. 
• Blood pressure and pulse rate are decreased 
• Bowel sounds are diminished, 
• Muscles are usually flaccid 

Severe overdose 
• Coma 
• Respiratory depression 
• Apnea often results in sudden death 
• Cardiotoxicity is seen in propoxyphene intoxication 
• Methadone intoxication causes prolonged QT interval and torsade de pointes 

Opioid withdrawal syndrome 
• Anxiety 
• Piloerection (goose bumps), 
• Heightened sensation of pain 
• Abdominal cramps and diarrhea 
• Insomnia 

Treatment 
• Emergency and supportive measures: Maintain an open airway and assist ventilation if necessary. 

Administer supplemental oxygen. 
• Decontamination: Administer act ivated charcoal orally 
• Con~ider whole-bowel irrigation after ingestion of sustained-release product s 
• Antidotes: Naloxone, Nalmefene 

3. .PARACETAMOL/ ACET AMI-NOP HEN- POISON~ 

• Acetaminophen is a widely used drug found in many over-the-counter and prescription analgesics and 
cold remedies 

• Mechanism of toxicity: hepatotoxicity is caused by NAPQI or N-acetyl-p-benzoquinone imine, a highly 
toxic product of normal metabolism of acetaminophen by cytochrome P-450 

Toxicity 

• Acute ingestion of more than 200 mg/kg in children or 6-7 gin adults is potentially hepatotoxic. 
• Early phase: anorexia, nausea, or vomiting. 
• A massive overdose may cause altered mental status and metabolic acidosis. 
• After 24-48 hours 

, AST & ALT begin to rise 
, Hepatic necrosis becomes evident. 

• If acute fulminant hepatic failure occurs, death may ensue. 
• Encephalopathy, metabolic acidosis, and a continuing rise in PT /INR indicate a poor prognosis. 

Acute renal failure occasionally occurs, with or w ithout concomitant liver failure

• Hemodialysis 

S. MUSHROOM POISONING* 

• Amantia phalloides is the most commonly encountered species 

• They cont ain potent cytotoxins (amatoxins). 

• Ingestion of even a portion of one mushroom of a dangerous species may be sufficient to cause death . 

Signs and symptoms (Phalloides syndrome) 
• Delayed onset (8-12 hours after ingestion) of severe abdominal cramps, vom iting and pro fuse diarrhea 

• Hepatic necrosis, hepatic encephalopathy, and frequently acute kidney injury in 1- 2 days 

• Cooking the mushrooms does not prevent poisoning. 

• Acute pseudo-obstruction and dilation of the colon in the absence of any mechanica l obstruction 

(Ogilvie's syndrome) 
• The charact eristic pathologic finding in fatalities from amatoxin-conta inlng mushroom poisoning I~ acute 

massive necrosis of the liver 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

Treatment 
• Stabilization: restoration of fluid and electrolyte balance, IV glucose 
• Decontamination: Gastric lavage, activated charcoal, forced diuresis, hemoperfusion 
• Antidotes: 

',, Cimetidine 
,- Silymarin (silibinin), a derivative of milk thistle 
, N-acetyl cysteine 
► Thioctic acid 

• For massive liver damage - liver transplantation 

6. \BOTULISM] 
• ~tulism is an acute neurologic disorder that causes potentially lite-threatening paralysis due to a 

neurotoxin produced by Clostridium botulinum or related species (C baratii and C. butyricum) 
Exposure may occur via 4 routes 

• Ingestion (foodborne botulism) 
• Colonization of a wound (wound botulism) or intestines (infant botulism) by C/ostridium botulinum 
• Cosmetic or therapeutic (botox) injection (iatrogenic botulism) 
• Bioterrorism (inhalational or ingestion-related botulism) 

Clinical features 
• Nonspecific findings such as nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, malaise, dizziness, dry mouth,

• Cranial nerve paralysis manifests as blurred vision, diplopia, ptosis, extraocular muscle weakn

• Symmetrical descending paralysis or weakness of motor and autonomic nerves 
• Respiratory muscle weakness may be subtle or progressive, advancing rapidly to respiratory fa

7 SOL VENT ABUSE] 
• Solvent abuse (or 'volatile substance abuse') is the deliberate inhalation of volatile chemicals for the 

purpose of getting intoxicated or 'high' 
• The products associated with solvent abuse are lega l, accessible and inexpensive 



r ,.. 
PtfARMACOLOGICAL TOXICOLOGY, FOOD POISONING, DRUG DEPENDENCE, MISCELLANEOUS 

• There are four general categories of inhalants: 

,, Volatile solvents: Paint thinners, Nail polish removers, gasoline 

,, Aerosols: spray paints, spray deodorants, hair spray 

,- Gases: medical anesthetics (chloroform, nitrous oxide) refrigerants 

, Nitrites: leather cleaners, room deodorizers 

• The peak age of inhalant abuse is 14 to 15. However, abuse is seen in children as young as 5 to 6 years 

Methods of abuse 

• Sniff or snort fumes from a container or dispenser 

• Spray aerosols directly into the nose or mouth 

• Inhale substances from a balloon or a plastic or paper bag (bagging) 

Effects of Inhalant abuse 
• Initial effects: stimulation, disinhibition and euphoria 

• Followed by hallucinations, slurred speech, disturbed gait, disorientation 

• Rebreathing when 'bagging' potentiates the intoxication by causing hypercapnia and hypoxia 

• Sudden sniffing death syndrome - leading cause of death among inhalant abusers 

• Chronic effects: irritability, tremor, ataxia, nystagmus, slurred speech, decreased visual acuity, deafness 

• Postmortem: one lung, urine sample and unfixed tusse from liver and brain samples should be preserved 

in nylon bags. 

8. ;LATHYRISM1 

• Lathyrism is a neurodegenerative disease characterised by spastic paraplegia 

• Occurs following excessive consumption of the legume Lathyrus sativus (Khesari dhal) 

• Lathyrus sativus forms the staple food of poor villagers, especially during the period of food scarcity 

following a flood or famine 

• The toxin in lathyrus seeds is BETA OXALYL AMINO ALANINE {BOAA) which is water soluble 

• If more than 30% of total diet contains khesari dal and taken for >6 months, spastic paralysis results 

• Sudden onset of leg weakness on getting up from sleep is the most common presentation 

• on neurological examination, the patients have spastic paraplegia, scissoring gait, exaggerated knee and 

ankle reflexes and extensor plantar response. 

• Most patients walk slowly without the need of a stick (no stick stage), while others need one or two sticks 

(one or two stick stage) and those crawling on hands and knees (Crawler stage) 

• Vitamin c prophylaxis - 500 to 1000 mg.daily can repair the damages 

• Removal of the toxin by various methods like steeping method, parboiling (boiling for 1 hour and 

removing the water removes about 70% of the toxin) 

9· KEROSENE Po1s0WNG] 
• Kerosene contains a mixture of hydrocarbons 

• Used as a solvent for insecticide sprays (hence, kerosene odor is perceived in insecticide poisoning) 

Clinical features of acute poisoning 
• Most cases are accidental in nature 

• Usually children are affected 

• Kerosene taste in the mouth, burning sensation in the throat 

• Nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, diarrhea 



VISION - FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY 

• Breath, vomit and urine smell of kerosene 

• Lungs are the main organs affected - dyspnea, tachypnea, bronchospasm, chemical pneumonitis, 
pulmonary edema, atelectasis 

• Kerosene sensitizes the myocardium to endogenous catecholamines resulting in arrhythmias 
• CNS depression 
• Skin lesions - bullae, blisters, burns 

Treatment 
• Mainly supportive treatment - oxygen administration 
• Gastric lavage and emesis - contraindicated 
• No specific antidote available

7. ICHTHYOTOXICOSIS/ICHTHYOSARCOTOXISMSI 

• It is poisoning caused by stinging or biting or from consumption of poisonous fishes. 
• Five types of fish poisoning: scombroid, ciguatera, tetrodotoxic, paralytic shellfish and neurotoxic 

shellfish. 
Ciguatera poisoning: 

• The most prevalent ichthyosarcotoxism worldwide 

• Clinical features include nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, pruritis, hypotension, severe headache, paresthe~1c1, 
tingling and numbness of lips, throat and tongue, tremors, ataxia and muscle weakness. 

• Pathognomic of ciguatera - strange reversal of temperature discrimination, such t hat hot objects feel cold 
and vice versa. 

Scombroid poisoning: 

• Erythema of face, urticaria, pruritis, flushing of skin, sweating, throbbing headache, abdominal cramps, 
bronchoconstriction and angioneurotic edema 



f't{ARMACOLOGICAL TOXICOLOGY, FOOD POISONING, DRUG DEPENDENCE, MISCELLANEOUS 

Tetrodotoxic poisoning: 
• Dysesthesia and paresthesia's of lip, tongue, mouth and face. 

Ataxia, weakness, fasciculation and ascending paralysis in 4-24 hours 
• Dilated pupils in severe poisoning

EXAMINATION OF BONES - VIVA 

Q: How will you determine race from skull? 
A: Cephalic Index: Refer to Chapter 3. Short Answer Q. No.2 

Q: What is Krogman's rule/ formula? 
A: Accuracy of sex determination from different bones is called Krogman's rule. 

• Entire skeleton = 100% 
• Pelvis alone = 95% 
• Skull alone = 90% 
• Skull and Pelvis = 98% 
• Long bones alone = 80% 

Q: What is the ideal bone for Sex determination? 
A: Pelvis 

Q: Which bone can be used for sex determination before puberty? 
A: Pelvis

Q: Which bones are used for stature estimation? 

A: Long bones - femur, tibia, fibul a, humerus etc. 

Q: What 1s the equipment used to measure length of long bones? 

A: Hepburn Osteometric board

Q: What are the Formulas for stature estimation? 
A: Trotter & Glaeser formula, Karl Pearson Formula, Dupertius & Hadden Formula. Indian formula- Nat 
formula, Pan formula, Siddique fo rmula, Mehta fo rmula. 

Q: What method is used for stature estimation from fragmented bones? 
A: Steele Method. Deduce ratio of various segments of long bones in relation to their entire length. Then 
use regression formu la. 

Q: What is the Rule of thumb for approximate estimation of stature? 
• Femur· 270/c of total height 
• Tibia: 22% of total height 
• Humerus: 20% of total height 
• Vertebral column: 35% of total height 
• Stature by skull : total height of individual is 1 / 7t1i of height of skull from bregma to chin. If skull 

without mandible, it is 1 / 8t1i from top to maxilla

11. What is Romberg test/sign? 
The Rombe rg test is a very common assessm ent of an individua l's ba lance and coordination. The Romberg 
sign is present when a patient is able to stand with feet together and eyes open, but sways or falls with 
eyes closed. It is a test for cerebellar function. 
The Rombe rg test is a ve ry s imple assessm ent that can be pe r fo rm ed without any s pecial equipme nt or

individua l unless they require s upport. 
3. Observe the s ubj ect for 20 seconds a nd note any swaying or fa lling.

POTENCY CERTIFICATE - VIVA 
(Read Chapter 15) 

1. Define impotency. 
2. Define sterility 
3. Define infertility. 
4. Causes of impotency in males 
5. What is artificial insemination. 

6. What is the test for potency? 
• Nocturnal penile tumescence test 
• Men typically have 3 - 5 erections per 8 hours of sleep, during REM phase. This is called nocturnal 

penil e tumescence (NPT) 
• Nocturnal penile tumescence testing involves placing several bands around the penis, connected to a 

device such as the Rigi scan monitor, which monitors his nocturnal erections, including how many 

occur, how long they last, and how rigid they are. 

• Inadequate or absent nocturnal erections suggest organic dysfunction, whereas a normal result 

indicates a psychogenic cause 
• It is considered one of the most reliable methods to differentiate psychogenic impotence from 

organic impotence.

MCCD · VIVA 

Q: Who can issue Medical Certificate of Cause of Death? 
A: MCCD should be issued by the Doctor who has medical attendant of the deceased during life or 
attended the patient within past 14 days prior to his death 

Q: Wh en a doctor can issue Medical Certificate of Cause of Death? 
A: The doctor can issue MCCD when he/she is absolutely certain 

► About the cause of death and 
► It is a natural death 

Q: Define Cause of death 
A: The primary or underlying cause of death is defined as that condition or injury (or circumstances of 
the injury) that in itiated the train of morbid events leading directly to death. 

Immediate Cause of death: The final disease or injury causing the death 



► 
CTICAL EXERCISES - \ 

EXERCISES FOR MCCD 

Case 1: A 60-year old male patient, a known case of type-2 diabetes mellitus for 20 years, is admitted for 

management of gangrene of left lower limb which started to develop 1 O days ago. 3 days after admission he 

developed seps is a nd succumbed to septic shock a day later. He is also a known case of Hypertension for 10 

years. 

Cause of death Time interval between onset and death 

(a) Septicemia 3 days 
due to ( or as a consequence of) 

I (b) Gangrene ofLimb 10 days 
due to ( or as a consequence of) 

( c) Diabetes Mellitus 20 years 

II Hypertension 10 years 

• The underlying cause of death which started the sequence should be the last entry under I. 

• Consequently, if there is no chain of events and underlying cause is the only event, it should be entered 

under (a) as it is both the immediate & underlying cause. 

• Due thought should be given to the last entry under I because it is picked up for statistical purposes as 

underlying cause of death 
• Terminal events like circulatory failure, respiratory failure or cardiorespiratory arrest etc. and 

modes of dying must be avoided 

Case 2: A 48-year old male, name BBB, developed cramps, epigastric pain, which radiated to back, followed by 

nausea and vomiting. 24 hours after its onset, the patient sought medical attention. He had a 10-year history of 

excessive alcohol consumption and 2-year history of frequent episodes of similar epigastric pain. The patient 

Was admitted to the hospital with the diagnosis of acute exacerbation of chronic pancreatitis. The next evening 

after admission, the patient became disoriented, restless and hypotensive. Despite medical intervention, the 

Patient died. He is also a known case of COPD for S years. 

Cause of death Time interval between onset and death 

(a) Acute Pancreatitis 2 days 

due to ( or as a consequence of) 

I (b) Chronic Pancreatitis 2 years 

due to ( or as a consequence of) 

(c) Excessive alcohol consumption 10 years 

II Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease 5 years 



VISION · FORENSIC MEDICINE and TOXICOLOGY J 

Case 3: A 68-yea r old male, name ODD, was admitted to the hospital with progressive right upper quadrant pain 
of several weeks' duration. Hepatomegaly was present. A needle biopsy of liver was diagnostic of moderately 
differentiated hepatocellular carcinoma. The pati ent was sta rted on chemotherapy. 6 months later, the patient 
developed sharp diminution of liver function and DVT of his left thigh and admitted to the hospital. 3 days later 
the patient developed pulmonary embolism and di ed 30 mins later. He is a known case of type 2 Diabetes 
melli tus for 20 years. 

Cause of death Time interval between onset & death 
(a) Pulmonary embolism 30 mins 

due to (or as a consequence ot)

fETI]s EXAMINATION/ 

Crown heel length: 

Weight: 

Skin: Color, Whether wrinkled or not 

Scalp hair: appeared or not, length 

Body hair (Lanugo hair): present or absent 

Eyebrows and eyelashes: appeared or not 

Fingernails and toenails: appeared or not; whether reached the tip offingers/toes 

Shape of the chest: Flat/barrel shaped 

Meconium: whether present in large intestine or not 

Position of diaphragm: corresponding rib level 

Sex: 

Umbilical cord: attached or not 

Ossification centers: Talus, calcaneum, sternum, lower end of femur 

Age: use Hasse's rule to find age from Crown heel length 

Viability: 

Cause of death:
